Exploiting Separable Structure in Neural Ranking




                       Andrew Parry




     Submitted in fulfilment of the requirements for the
              Degree of Doctor of Philosophy




                   School of Engineering
            College of Science and Engineering
                   University of Glasgow




                      September 2026



                     © Andrew Parry, 2026
Abstract

Search systems rank documents by how likely they are to satisfy a user’s information need.
Strong modern systems score each document against the query in a single pass through a
large neural language model, but this design conflates two fundamentally different kinds of
evidence: properties of a document that may be estimated before any query arrives, and
the relevance of those properties to a specific query once it is observed. Classical retrieval
systems maintained a structural separation between these two quantities. Neural systems
do not. This thesis argues that restoring that separation yields concrete improvements in
efficiency, effectiveness, and robustness.
    On efficiency, the cost of the neural systems that collapse both kinds of evidence into
a single non-decomposable scorer is shown to rest on weaker foundations than commonly
assumed. When all evidence is entangled in one scorer, the expense of the whole pipeline
must be justified against a single aggregate outcome, and that outcome is itself unreliable: a
large-scale re-annotation of the TREC Deep Learning 2019 test collection shows that inter-
annotator disagreement on graded relevance is large enough that differences between closely
performing systems fall within the margin of that disagreement, and that the systems consid-
ered most advanced are precisely those most sensitive to annotator variation. On the training
side, a generalisation bound for ranking distillation shows that its dominant terms depend on
the training signal rather than on the expense of data selection, and negatives sampled from a
simple term-matching baseline produce rankers statistically equivalent to those trained with
expensively mined data.
    On effectiveness, the decomposition maintained by classical probabilistic retrieval is
reintroduced: a document-quality score is computed once, offline, and composed at query
time with an existing ranker through additive fusion. This improves first-stage lexical re-
trieval and downstream re-ranking, requiring only a cached score lookup per document at
inference time.
    On robustness, the conflation of these two kinds of evidence within a single scorer is
shown to introduce exploitable vulnerabilities. Neural ranking models are susceptible to at-
tacks that require no knowledge of the target query: keyword stuffing adapted to prompt
tokens, and rewriting by a large language model, both significantly improve the rank of ma-
nipulated documents, and positional biases can be further exploited by injecting promotional
content that neural scoring fails to detect. A defence is proposed that exploits the asymmetry
between the components: a lexical scorer penalises what the neural scorer misses, and their
disagreement identifies adversarial content without degrading clean-input effectiveness.

                                                       πQ = µQ .

Chapter 1

Introduction

When a user submits a query to a search engine, the system must determine which of the
documents in its collection are most likely to satisfy the underlying information need. This
task, known as ad hoc retrieval, is the foundational problem of information retrieval [Sarace-
vic, 2007]. The query is typically short and underspecified: a query such as “python memory
management” may correspond to a need for a tutorial, a language specification, a technical
discussion, or a research paper, and the system must infer which documents are useful from
a few terms alone [Belkin, 1980]. The system’s response is a ranked list: documents are
ordered by their estimated relevance to the query, following the Probability Ranking Princi-
ple [Robertson, 1977], and the user examines results in the order presented. The quality of
this ranking is called retrieval effectiveness, and improving it has been the central objective
of the field for over fifty years.
     A retrieval system must solve two problems. First, it must represent queries and docu-
ments in a form that permits comparison [Salton et al., 1975, Robertson and Spärck Jones,
1976]. Second, it must score each candidate document against the query to produce the
ranking [Robertson, 1977, Ponte and Croft, 1998]. How these two problems are solved has
changed dramatically over the history of the field, but a subtler question has accompanied
every generation of models: what does the system know about a document before any query
arrives, and what can it only determine once a specific query is observed? A document’s
length, writing quality, topical authority, and source reputation are properties that are in-
dependent of any particular query. Whether a document addresses a particular information
need can only be assessed once that need is expressed. Classical retrieval systems kept these
two sources of evidence explicitly separate, and the learning-to-rank systems that followed
still drew much of their signal from document-centric features computed in advance; mod-
ern neural systems fold both into a single learned score. The consequences of that change
motivate this thesis.
     The remainder of this chapter proceeds as follows. Section 1.1 traces the trajectory from
term-weighting to neural ranking, identifying the costs that have accompanied effectiveness
gains. Section 1.2 shows that the problems arising from this trajectory share a common
structural cause: the collapse of a separation that classical systems maintained. Section 1.3
characterises this collapse. Section 1.4 states the thesis and decomposes it into testable


                                              1
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION                                                                        2

hypotheses. Section 1.5 documents the origin of the contributing materials.


1.1     From Term-Weighting to Neural Ranking
This section traces the development of ranking models from classical term-weighting to mod-
ern neural approaches, not as a historical survey, but to identify a specific structural property
that was present in early systems and lost in later ones. That property, the explicit separation
of query-independent and query-dependent evidence, is the subject of the thesis.
    The earliest effective retrieval systems ranked documents by the frequency with which
query terms appeared in them, adjusted for the prevalence of those terms across the col-
lection [Spärck Jones, 1972, Robertson and Spärck Jones, 1976]. If a user issues the query
“python memory management,” a document containing all three terms would receive a higher
score than one mentioning only “python,” particularly if “memory management” is infre-
quent in the collection. This intuition was formalised through term-weighting functions:
scoring functions that combine term frequency (how often a term appears in the document),
inverse document frequency (how rare the term is across the collection), and document length
normalisation [Robertson and Spärck Jones, 1976, Ponte and Croft, 1998].
    The most widely used term-weighting function, BM25 [Robertson et al., 1995], remains
deployed in production search engines today because it is fast, interpretable, and compati-
ble with the inverted index, the data structure that allows a search engine to look up which
documents contain a given term without scanning the entire collection. Under BM25, the
contribution of each query term to the final score is explicit: an operator can trace the con-
tribution of each term to the ranking and adjust the system’s behaviour by tuning a small
number of parameters. Crucially for this thesis, BM25 also maintains an explicit document
length normalisation component that functions as a query-independent prior over documents:
longer documents are penalised relative to shorter ones through a parameter that can be tuned
independently of the query-dependent scoring.
    The learning-to-rank systems that followed combined many such signals within a single
learned function, but a substantial part of the feature vector consisted of document-centric
features (length, link-based authority, static quality estimates) computed in advance of any
query, so the query-independent evidence remained explicit and separately addressable be-
fore neural rankers absorbed it [Liu, 2009, Qin et al., 2010].
    The vocabulary mismatch problem motivated the development of a fundamentally dif-
ferent approach. Relevant documents often fail to share surface terms with the query due
to synonymy (“car” versus “automobile”), paraphrase (“how to fix” versus “troubleshooting
guide”), or domain-specific terminology [Furnas et al., 1987]. A term-weighting function
that relies on exact lexical overlap will fail to identify these connections. Neural ranking
models address this limitation by learning semantic representations with large pre-trained
language models. Dense retrievers encode queries and documents independently into em-
beddings, so that semantically related texts occupy nearby regions of the embedding space
even when they share no surface terms [Karpukhin et al., 2020], while cross-encoders in-
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION                                                                         3

stead process the query and document jointly and score their interaction directly [Nogueira
and Cho, 2019]. In both cases the system can recognise relevance beyond exact lexical
matching. Neural rankers have achieved substantial effectiveness gains on standard test col-
lections [Hofstätter et al., 2020, Pradeep et al., 2021], and the field has invested heavily in
scaling these models.
     This investment in increasingly powerful neural ranking models has, however, come
at considerable cost in computation [Scells et al., 2022], in the complexity of the training
pipeline [Lin et al., 2021, Zhao et al., 2024], and in fragility [Wu et al., 2023a]. Train-
ing state-of-the-art neural ranking models requires elaborate multi-stage pipelines: large-
scale labelled data produced through human annotation [Nguyen et al., 2016] or knowledge
distillation [Zamani et al., 2018, Lin et al., 2020, Hofstätter et al., 2020], negative mining
cascades [Reimers and Gurevych, 2019, Qu et al., 2021, Xiao et al., 2022] that depend on
expensive intermediate retrieval, and iterative cycles whose components are often tightly
coupled [Lin et al., 2020]. At inference time, the most powerful neural models impose sub-
stantial latency costs. Cross-encoders, which jointly process the concatenated query and
document text through a transformer [Nogueira and Cho, 2019], and LLM-based list-wise
rankers, which compare multiple candidates simultaneously [Sun et al., 2023, Pradeep et al.,
2023], both constrain the number of candidates that can be scored under production latency
budgets [Dean, 2009].
     The field’s continued investment in this trajectory rests on two premises: that the marginal
effectiveness gains reported for each generation of models are real, and that the elaborate
pipelines used to produce them are necessary. Both premises warrant scrutiny. On the evalu-
ation side, relevance judgments vary across annotators, over time, and with the complexity of
the information need [Voorhees, 2000], and it is unclear whether measured improvements at
the margins where expensive models operate reflect genuine advances or fall within the noise
of annotation variability. On the training side, the necessity of each pipeline component has
rarely been isolated: when a system that uses expensive negative mining, multi-teacher dis-
tillation, and iterative hard-negative selection achieves a new state of the art, the marginal
contribution of each component is confounded with the others.
     A third concern, distinct from evaluation and training but equally consequential, is that
these increasingly powerful neural systems have introduced attack surfaces that did not exist
under classical retrieval. Neural rankers learn to associate textual patterns with relevance
scores, and adversarial content can exploit these learned associations to manipulate rank-
ings [Wu et al., 2023a]. Because the scoring function is non-decomposable, there is no
internal basis against which to check whether a high score reflects genuine relevance or ad-
versarial manipulation. This vulnerability is not incidental; it is structural, and it is a direct
consequence of collapsing all evidence into a single scorer.
     These three concerns (fragile evaluation, costly training pipelines of uncertain necessity,
and adversarial vulnerability) appear distinct. The next section shows that they share a com-
mon structural cause.
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION                                                                    4

1.2     Separable Structure in Classical Retrieval
The problems identified above all arise from replacing a compositional scoring architecture
with a non-decomposable one. To understand why, it is necessary to examine the structural
property that classical systems maintained and that neural systems abandoned.
   Classical probabilistic retrieval models did more than weight terms; they maintained an
explicit separation between what the system knows about a document independently of any
query and what it learns when a specific query is observed. Under the query-likelihood
formulation, relevance decomposes as

                                              p(q | d) p(d)
                                 p(d | q) ∝                 ,                           (1.1)
                                                  p(q)

where p(q | d) is a query-dependent likelihood, capturing how well a document’s language
matches a particular query, and p(d) is a query-independent document prior, encoding sta-
ble beliefs about the document that hold regardless of the query: length [Robertson et al.,
1995], authority [Page et al., 1999], domain suitability [Bendersky et al., 2010], or source
quality [Peng et al., 2008]. The term p(q) is constant across documents for a given query
and can be ignored for ranking purposes [Ponte and Croft, 1998, Hiemstra, 2001, Zhai and
Lafferty, 2004].
    This decomposition reflects a fundamental asymmetry in retrieval. Documents exist in
the collection before any query arrives; their properties can be estimated once, offline, and
stored. Query-document interaction can only be determined once a specific query is ob-
served. The prior-likelihood decomposition respects this asymmetry. The prior p(d) can be
computed once for every document in the collection; the likelihood p(q | d) must be eval-
uated per candidate per query. We refer to this property as separability: the architectural
commitment to maintaining query-independent and query-dependent evidence as indepen-
dently estimable components, enabling their independent evaluation and update.
    In practice, classical systems exploited separability pervasively. PageRank [Page et al.,
1999] is a document prior: it scores documents by link structure without reference to any
query. Spam classifiers [Cormack et al., 2011] are document priors: they assess content
quality offline. Static quality estimators [Bendersky et al., 2011] are document priors: they
predict document usefulness from surface features computed at index time. In each case,
the prior was a separable component that could be inspected, tuned, or replaced without
modifying the query-dependent scorer. In a deployed system, this separation determined
what could be precomputed, what could be evaluated at the component level, what could be
updated as the collection evolved, and what could be controlled by a system operator.


1.3     The Collapse of Separability in Neural Ranking
The transition from classical to neural ranking abandoned this separation. Neural rank-
ing models collapse the prior-likelihood distinction of Equation 1.1 into a single query-
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION                                                                      5

conditioned scoring function. Whether implemented as bi-encoder similarity, where queries
and documents are encoded separately and compared via dot product [Karpukhin et al.,
2020]; cross-encoder re-ranking, where a transformer processes the concatenated query and
document [Nogueira and Cho, 2019]; or learned sparse retrieval, where a model predicts
term importance weights [Formal et al., 2021], contemporary models absorb document-
side structure, corpus-level regularities, and query-independent utility into their parameters
through training data distributions, negative sampling procedures, and end-to-end optimisa-
tion.
    Bi-encoders partially mitigate this by precomputing document representations offline,
but the resulting embeddings are typically used only for similarity scoring rather than as
explicit sources of document-level evidence. For cross-encoders, prompt-adaptation exper-
iments have shown that a substantial fraction of the scoring behaviour of models such as
monoT5 is attributable to query-independent document properties: tuning only a small num-
ber of prompt tokens, combined with a separate document-quality head, is sufficient to re-
cover much of the full model’s effectiveness [Braga et al., 2025]. This suggests that online
computation in cross-encoder re-ranking is substantially allocated to recovering document-
level properties that could have been estimated offline. The document prior has not been
eliminated; it has been marginalised into the learned representation, entangled with query-
dependent signal in a way that prevents its separate estimation, reuse, or control.
    This entanglement connects directly to the three problems identified in Section 1.1:
    • Evaluation fragility. When all evidence is collapsed into a single, non-decomposable
      (or monolithic) scorer, the cost of the whole pipeline must be justified against an ag-
      gregate outcome whose measurement may itself be unreliable. Marginal gains cannot
      be attributed to specific components.

    • Training cost. When training stages are coupled end-to-end, the contribution of each
      stage cannot be isolated, and expensive components cannot be replaced by cheaper
      alternatives without retraining the entire cascade.

    • Adversarial vulnerability. When the scoring function is non-decomposable, adversar-
      ial manipulation that exploits learned associations cannot be detected by any internal
      check, because the system has no separable basis against which to compare.


1.4     Thesis Statement
This thesis argues that exploiting separable structure in neural ranking, specifically the ca-
pacity to identify, estimate, and compose query-independent and query-dependent evidence
as distinct components, yields concrete improvements in efficiency, effectiveness, and ro-
bustness. We propose to recover this structure through term-weighting, broadly construed:
    • For training, we propose that a classical term-weighting retriever provides sufficient
      supervision for effective ranking distillation, replacing costly iterative data-selection
      procedures.
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION                                                                       6

    • For effectiveness, we propose that a learned document-quality prior, composed with
      existing rankers through offline score fusion, improves ranking precision without in-
      creasing query-time cost.

    • For robustness, we propose that calibrated lexical components combined with neu-
      ral scores provide a separable basis for detecting adversarial manipulation that non-
      decomposable scorers cannot identify.

    In each case, retaining query-independent evidence as an architecturally distinct compo-
nent exposes interpretable mechanisms that permit independent evaluation, explicit control,
and modular composition with neural scoring functions. The thesis claims are about separa-
bility as a principle; term-weighting provides the primary instantiation because term-weights
are compact, computationally inexpensive, interpretable, and available without additional
training.
    These claims are decomposed into five hypotheses, organised in three stages.

Stage 1: Efficiency of Evaluation and Training. The field’s investment in increasingly
expensive non-decomposable systems rests on marginal benchmark gains and elaborate train-
ing pipelines. If the benchmarks cannot reliably measure those gains, and if the pipelines
can be simplified without loss of effectiveness, then the investment is not justified on its own
terms. We test both foundations.

    • H1.1: Inter-annotator agreement under graded relevance is insufficient to reliably dis-
      criminate between closely performing neural ranking systems on widely used test col-
      lections, limiting the practical value of marginal effectiveness gains from increasingly
      expensive non-decomposable models. (Chapter 3)

    • H1.2: BM25-sampled negatives provide sufficient supervision for effective ranking
      distillation without the multi-stage mining cascades that dominate current practice;
      under minimally sufficient locality and teacher effectiveness, neural model-based data
      augmentation does not improve effectiveness beyond this baseline. (Chapter 4)

Stage 2: Effectiveness through Prior-Likelihood Decomposition. Motivated by these
findings on evaluation reliability and training efficiency, we turn to what separable alterna-
tives can offer. We reintroduce the query-independent document prior that classical proba-
bilistic retrieval maintained but that neural systems have collapsed into learned representa-
tions.

    • H2: A query-independent document-quality prior, learned offline and composed with
      existing rankers through score fusion, improves both first-stage retrieval and down-
      stream re-ranking effectiveness without increasing query-time cost. (Chapter 5)
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION                                                                     7

a boxed statement and closes with a Verdict paragraph at the cited location.

Stage 3: Robustness to Adversarial Manipulation. We examine the vulnerabilities that
arise from the entanglement of query-dependent and query-independent evidence and test
whether separable scoring components can detect and mitigate them.

    • H3.1: The entanglement of query-dependent and query-independent evidence in neu-
      ral ranking models creates exploitable vulnerabilities: sequence-to-sequence models
      are susceptible to query-independent adversarial attacks, including keyword stuffing
      and LLM-based document rewriting, that require no knowledge of the target query
      and transfer across model scales. (Chapter 6)

    • H3.2: Separable scoring components respond asymmetrically to adversarial manipu-
      lation, producing a detectable disagreement signal: ensembles that interpolate neural
      scores with lexical components reduce the effectiveness of positional bias exploitation
      and promotional content injection without degrading non-adversarial retrieval. (Chap-
      ter 7)

Chapter 2

The Ranking Problem and Solutions

This chapter develops the conceptual and technical foundations on which the contribution
chapters depend. Its primary purpose is not to survey related work exhaustively but to build,
piece by piece, the decomposition s(q, d) = fθ (q, d) + gφ (d) that separates query-dependent
and query-independent scoring components. Every contribution chapter investigates a con-
sequence of collapsing or restoring this separation: Chapter 5 shows that an explicit gφ im-
proves effectiveness; Chapters 3 and 4 show that separable components are easier to evaluate
and cheaper to train; and Chapters 6 and 7 show that the disagreement between fθ and gφ un-
der adversarial conditions provides a detection and mitigation signal that non-decomposable
scorers cannot produce.
    We begin by formalising the ranking problem and the evaluation paradigm underpin-
ning offline experiments (§2.1 and §2.2). We then cover language modelling for retrieval
(§2.3), classical learning-to-rank (§2.4), and modern neural language models used for rank-
ing (§2.4.4). We discuss training practice, including data augmentation, loss design, and
distillation (§2.4.5), and generalisation, including scaling and continuous adaptation (§2.5).


2.1     The Ranking Problem
Let D = {d1 , . . . , dN } be a corpus and q ∈ Q a user query. A ranking system computes a
scoring function s : Q × D → R and returns an ordering πq of documents by decreasing
s(q, d). In classical IR, s is derived from lexical statistics under vector-space or probabilistic
assumptions [Spärck Jones, 1972, Robertson, 1977, Salton and McGill, 1983]. In language-
modelling IR, s(q, d) is tied to the likelihood of generating q from a document language
model p(· | d) [Ponte and Croft, 1998]. In learning-to-rank, s is learned from relevance
judgments R ⊆ Q × D × Z≥0 [Liu, 2009].
    A useful decomposition distinguishes dynamic (query-dependent) and static (query-independent)
components:
                               s(q, d) = fθ (q, d) + gφ (d) + hψ (q),                       (2.1)

where fθ models interactions between q and d; gφ encodes document utility priors (e.g.,
authority, quality, recency); and hψ captures query-specific biases (e.g., length or type priors)


                                               10
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                                 11

[Robertson, 1977]. Equation 2.1 is the additive, learned counterpart of the probabilistic
decomposition in Equation 1.1, with gφ (d) in the role of the document prior p(d), fθ (q, d)
the query likelihood p(q | d), and hψ (q) the query evidence p(q). Because hψ (q) is constant
across documents for a fixed query, it does not affect the induced ordering πq and can be
dropped without loss of generality for the ranking task. The separation between fθ and gφ
is substantive, however: it clarifies design trade-offs for effectiveness (how much of s can
be captured by a cheap static component), efficiency (offline computation and caching of
gφ ), robustness (regularising fθ with gφ to resist adversarial manipulation), and adaptability
(updating gφ as the collection evolves without retraining fθ ).


2.2     Evaluating Ranking Effectiveness

2.2.1    Cranfield Collections
The ad hoc retrieval task is typically formalised as follows. A user has an underlying in-
formation need that is only partially observable to the system. The user expresses this need
via a query q (often a short text string), and the retrieval system must assign scores s(q, d)
to documents d in a corpus D in order to produce an ordering that is useful to the user. To
study such systems empirically, we require testbeds in which information needs, documents,
and some notion of relevance are instantiated in a controlled and reusable way.
    The Cranfield paradigm provides precisely this abstraction. A Cranfield-style test collec-
tion consists of three components: (i) a fixed corpus of documents; (ii) a set of topics, each
intended as a proxy for an information need; and (iii) a set of relevance assessments, in which
human assessors judge, for each topic, which documents are relevant according to specified
guidelines [Cleverdon et al., 1966, Voorhees, 2001, Voorhees and Harman, 2005]. Topics are
often represented as short queries, possibly augmented with descriptions and narratives that
define the intended notion of relevance. The resulting judgments, frequently stored as qrels,
form a ground truth against which ranking functions can be evaluated offline.
    Constructing complete relevance judgments for all topic-document pairs is infeasible at
scale. Modern test collections therefore rely on pooling: for each topic, multiple retrieval
systems are run, and the top k documents from each system are merged into a pool. Hu-
man assessors then judge only the documents in this pooled set, an approach proposed in
the design of early reusable test collections [Spärck Jones and van Rijsbergen, 1975] and
operationalised at scale by TREC [Voorhees and Harman, 2005, Zobel, 1998]. Pooling
is cost-effective and leverages system diversity, but it induces incompleteness: documents
that are relevant yet not retrieved by any pooled system remain unjudged and are typically
treated as non-relevant by evaluation measures that assume complete judgments [Buckley
and Voorhees, 2004]. The depth of pooling directly affects the reliability of the resulting col-
lection: deeper pools capture more relevant documents but at higher annotation cost, while
shallower pools risk systematic bias against systems that were not included in the pool con-
struction [Zobel, 1998, Büttcher et al., 2007, Sakai, 2008]. As a result, measured effective-
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                                 12

ness is an approximation to the true effectiveness that would be obtained under exhaustive
judgments.
    Empirical studies quantify these effects: shallow pools bias evaluation against relevant
documents that no pooled system retrieved and against novel systems absent from pool con-
struction, and the measured value of depth-sensitive metrics such as nDCG shifts with pool
depth [Zobel, 1998, Buckley and Voorhees, 2004, Lu et al., 2016].

Depth-k pooling. Formally, given a set of participating systems S and a topic t, depth-k
                               S
pooling forms the pool Pt = S∈S topk (S,t), the union of the top k documents each system
returns for t. Only the documents in Pt are presented to assessors; every document outside
the pool is left unjudged and, under measures that assume complete judgments, is treated as
non-relevant. The pool depth k trades annotation cost against completeness: a larger k re-
covers more relevant documents but requires more judgments, while a smaller k concentrates
judging effort on the highest-ranked documents at the risk of the incompleteness biases noted
above. This construction, and the labelled-set assumptions it induces, is what later chapters
refer to when they discuss pooling and its effect on measured effectiveness.
    Re-annotation and replication studies have examined the reliability of Cranfield-style
evaluations. When multiple assessors independently judge the same topic-document pairs,
non-trivial disagreement is observed: assessors differ both in their interpretation of relevance
and in their consistency over time [Voorhees, 2000, Bailey et al., 2008]. Nevertheless, within
certain ranges, relative system orderings (e.g., which of two systems is better on average) are
often stable across assessors, pooling depths, and metric choices [Voorhees, 2000, Sanderson
et al., 2010]. This robustness is a key justification for the Cranfield paradigm as a tool for
comparative evaluation, even when absolute scores and fine-grained differences are subject
to uncertainty. Chapter 3 will revisit and challenge this finding in the context of modern
graded-relevance collections.
    Many ad hoc retrieval benchmarks also incorporate temporal structure. Newswire and
web collections, for example, span months or years, during which both document distribu-
tions and user intents may drift [Mizzaro, 1997, Dalton et al., 2020]. A query about a political
event posed early in a collection’s time span may have different relevant documents than the
same query posed later, even if the surface form of the query is unchanged. Static testbeds,
which fix topics and judgments at a particular time, necessarily abstract away such dynam-
ics. As retrieval models become increasingly sensitive to temporal and contextual factors,
this tension between static collections and evolving information needs presents a recurring
challenge for evaluation.


2.2.2    User Models
Offline evaluation measures do more than simply aggregate relevance labels across ranks:
they implicitly encode assumptions about how users interact with ranked lists. A typical
perspective is that a user examines results from top to bottom, may click on some subset of
them, and stops once sufficiently satisfied or fatigued. A user model formalises this process
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                                13

in terms of examination probabilities, click probabilities conditional on relevance, and stop-
ping rules. Many widely used measures can be derived as expectations of user utility under
such models [Moffat et al., 2013, Carterette et al., 2011].
    Position-based models (PBM) assume that the probability of examining a result depends
only on its rank position i, via an examination probability ei , and that, conditional on exam-
ination, the click probability is determined by the document’s relevance [Richardson et al.,
2006, Craswell et al., 2008]. In this view, higher ranks receive more attention simply be-
cause users are more likely to inspect them, and measures that apply a deterministic discount
(e.g., log-based discounts in DCG) can be interpreted as approximations to these position-
dependent examination probabilities.
    Cascade and dependent-click models add a sequential structure. In a pure cascade model,
the user examines results from top to bottom, deciding at each rank whether to click and
whether to stop or continue [Craswell et al., 2008, Guo et al., 2009]. The probability of
examining rank i depends on the outcomes at earlier ranks, leading to dependency between
clicks. Dependent-click models generalise this idea by allowing examination probabilities
at rank i to depend on both position and the observed click history [Chapelle et al., 2009].
These models better capture phenomena such as abandonment after a satisfactory click or
continued searching after unsatisfactory clicks, and they provide a probabilistic foundation
for measures that give high weight to early, highly satisfying results.
    Satisfaction-based models take user satisfaction, rather than clicks per se, as the primary
quantity of interest. Measures such as Expected Reciprocal Rank (ERR) posit a probability
that the user is satisfied at each rank and a stopping probability that depends on satisfaction
[Chapelle et al., 2009]. For example, ERR models the user as browsing the list from top to
bottom, with a probability of stopping at rank i that is proportional to the gain associated
with the document at that rank. The resulting discounting pattern reflects the idea that highly
relevant documents early in the ranking can lead to rapid satisfaction and early stopping.
    These user models motivate the functional forms of discount factors and stopping prob-
abilities that appear in evaluation measures. A logarithmic discount in DCG can be viewed
as a stylised approximation to decreasing examination probability with rank [Järvelin and
Kekäläinen, 2002]; the geometric discount in Rank-Biased Precision (RBP) corresponds to a
user who continues from one rank to the next with constant persistence probability p [Mof-
fat and Zobel, 2008]; ERR’s reciprocal discount emerges from its satisfaction-based stopping
process [Chapelle et al., 2009]. By making the underlying behavioural assumptions explicit,
user models provide a bridge between the design of evaluation metrics and the plausibility
of the user behaviour they intend to approximate.


2.2.3    Measures
Evaluation measures quantify how well a ranking produced by a retrieval system aligns with
the relevance assessments in a test collection. At a high level, they map, for each topic, a
ranked list of documents and their associated relevance labels to a scalar effectiveness score,
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                               14

which can then be aggregated across topics. Measures differ in the assumptions they make
about user behaviour, the extent to which they are sensitive to rank order, and how they
handle graded relevance and incomplete judgments [Saracevic, 1995].
    Set-based measures such as precision, recall, and F-score treat the retrieved set up to a
cutoff as an unordered set, ignoring rank beyond that cutoff [van Rijsbergen, 1979]. Precision@k
measures the proportion of retrieved documents in the top k that are relevant; recall@k mea-
sures the proportion of all relevant documents in the collection that appear in the retrieved
set. F-scores combine precision and recall via a harmonic mean, trading off between retriev-
ing many relevant documents (high recall) and avoiding non-relevant ones (high precision).
While these measures are intuitive and easy to compute, they do not differentiate between
rankings that place the same set of relevant documents at different positions within the top k.
    Ranking-sensitive measures incorporate the order of results. Average Precision (AP), and
its mean across topics (MAP), compute the average of precision values at the ranks where
relevant documents occur, effectively rewarding systems that retrieve relevant documents
early and with few intervening non-relevant documents [Buckley and Voorhees, 2004]. Mean
Reciprocal Rank (MRR) focuses on the first relevant result, computing the reciprocal of the
rank of the first relevant document and averaging across topics; it is particularly suited to
navigational queries where a single highly relevant result suffices [Craswell, 2009].
    For graded relevance, measures based on discounted cumulative gain (DCG) and its nor-
malised variant (nDCG) are widely used. With graded labels ri at rank i and a gain function
g (e.g., g(r) = 2r − 1), discounted gain at cutoff k is defined as

                              k
                                    g(ri )                            DCG@k
                DCG@k = ∑                       with       nDCG@k =          ,           (2.2)
                             i=1 log2 (i + 1)                         IDCG@k

where IDCG@k is the DCG of an ideal ranking that orders documents by decreasing rele-
vance [Järvelin and Kekäläinen, 2002]. The logarithmic discount attenuates the contribution
of lower-ranked documents, reflecting decreasing user attention with depth, while normal-
isation by IDCG@k yields scores in [0, 1] and facilitates comparisons across topics with
different relevance distributions.
    Rank-Biased Precision (RBP) offers an alternative that is explicitly derived from a simple
user model. Assuming a user who continues from one rank to the next with persistence
probability p ∈ (0, 1), RBP is defined as
                                                       ∞
                                  RBP = (1 − p) ∑ pi−1 ri ,                              (2.3)
                                                   i=1

where ri is the (possibly graded) relevance at rank i [Moffat and Zobel, 2008]. The geometric
discount pi−1 corresponds to the probability that the user reaches rank i, and the leading
factor (1 − p) normalises the measure. Small values of p emphasise the very top of the
ranking; larger values place more weight on deeper ranks.
    Satisfaction-based measures such as Expected Reciprocal Rank (ERR) integrate both
graded relevance and a stopping process. ERR assumes that, at each rank, the user becomes
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                                  15

satisfied with probability determined by the gain g(ri ) and stops with that probability; the ex-
pected utility is then a sum over ranks of reciprocal discounts weighted by these satisfaction
probabilities [Chapelle et al., 2009]. Like RBP, ERR admits a user-centric interpretation, but
it emphasises scenarios where users may derive substantial benefit from a small number of
highly relevant documents near the top.

Incompleteness and uncertainty. In Cranfield-style evaluations, measures must contend
with incomplete and uncertain relevance information. Pooling ensures that judged docu-
ments are concentrated among those retrieved by participating systems, but many unjudged
documents remain, and treating them as non-relevant can bias measures in favour of systems
similar to those that contributed to the pool [Buckley and Voorhees, 2004, Zobel, 1998]. Dif-
ferent measures respond differently to unjudged documents: some ignore them entirely (e.g.,
set-based measures applied at a cutoff that lies within the pool), whereas others implicitly
penalise systems that retrieve unjudged documents in high ranks. Measures such as infAP
and bpref have been specifically designed to provide more robust estimates under incom-
plete judgments by accounting for the pool structure [Yilmaz and Aslam, 2006, Buckley and
Voorhees, 2004].
    Evaluation results are therefore subject to sampling variance arising from finite topic
sets, incompleteness, and assessor disagreement. To quantify uncertainty in reported ef-
fectiveness differences, significance testing is commonly applied to per-topic scores. Ran-
domisation (permutation) tests offer a non-parametric approach that makes minimal distribu-
tional assumptions [Smucker et al., 2007]. Paired t-tests are widely used for their simplicity,
though they assume normality of per-topic differences. Bootstrap resampling provides confi-
dence intervals and p-values without parametric assumptions and has been recommended as
a general-purpose approach for retrieval evaluation [Sakai, 2006]. These tests estimate the
probability that observed differences between systems could have arisen by chance under
a null hypothesis of no true difference. Complementing significance tests with effect sizes
(e.g., mean differences, relative improvements) and confidence intervals is recommended
to convey the magnitude and reliability of observed improvements [Carterette et al., 2012].
When multiple systems or metrics are compared simultaneously, corrections for multiple
comparisons, such as Bonferroni adjustment, Holm-Bonferroni step-down procedures, or
hierarchical testing, are required to control family-wise error rates or false discovery rates
[Urbano et al., 2019].


2.3     Language Modelling for Retrieval
A language model in its most general form defines a probability distribution over sequences
of tokens. In the conventional, modern sense, this involves predicting the next token given a
context of preceding tokens [Shannon, 1948, Jelinek and Mercer, 1980], the sense in which
the autoregressive large language models of Section 2.4.4 are meant. In classical informa-
tion retrieval, however, “language modelling” names something narrower and distinct: a
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                                 16

deliberately simplified variant, the unigram or bag-of-words language model, which ignores
word order and treats a document as a multinomial distribution over a vocabulary V [Ponte
and Croft, 1998, Hiemstra, 2001]. Throughout this section “language model” is used in this
classical query-likelihood sense; the term “LLM” is reserved for the neural autoregressive
models discussed later. This simplification sacrifices the sequential structure that makes lan-
guage models powerful for generation, but it gains compatibility with the inverted index
and admits closed-form estimation from term counts alone. The neural language models
discussed in Section 2.4.4 restore the sequential structure through contextualised represen-
tations, but the bag-of-words formulation remains the starting point for understanding how
probabilistic term-level evidence is combined in classical retrieval.
    Under the unigram assumption, a document d is represented solely by its term counts
{c(t, d) : t ∈ V } and length |d| = ∑t c(t, d). The document is assumed to be generated by
repeated independent draws from a multinomial distribution p(· | d) over V . The maximum-
likelihood (ML) estimate of this distribution is

                                                      c(t, d)
                                      pML (t | d) =           ,
                                                        |d|

which assigns probability in proportion to the empirical frequency of t in d [Ponte and Croft,
1998]. This estimator is unbiased but suffers from a critical defect for retrieval: any term not
observed in d receives zero probability, so any query containing such a term is assigned zero
likelihood under pML (· | d).
    To obtain models that can generalise beyond observed counts and support meaningful
comparison between documents, the ML estimate is replaced by a smoothed language model.
Smoothing introduces additional probability mass to unseen or rare events, typically by com-
bining document-specific evidence with a background model that reflects corpus-level statis-
tics. At a high level, smoothing solves two related problems: it prevents zero probabilities
for plausible but unobserved terms, and it regularises the document model for short or sparse
documents.
    Several families of smoothing methods have been developed in statistical language mod-
elling, including additive (e.g., Laplace), absolute discounting, backoff, and interpolation
schemes [Chen and Goodman, 1996]. In information retrieval, two simple yet effective in-
terpolated estimators have been particularly influential. Jelinek-Mercer smoothing forms a
convex combination of the document ML estimate and a collection language model p(t | D ):

                         pJM (t | d) = (1 − λ ) pML (t | d) + λ p(t | D ),

where λ ∈ [0, 1] controls the relative influence of document-specific and corpus-wide evi-
dence [Jelinek and Mercer, 1980, Zhai and Lafferty, 2004]. The collection model p(t | D ) is
usually estimated by aggregating counts over the corpus:

                                               ∑d ′ ∈D c(t, d ′ )
                                   p(t | D ) =                    .
                                                ∑d ′ ∈D |d ′ |
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                                  17

   Dirichlet-prior smoothing instead places a conjugate Dirichlet prior on the multinomial
parameters for each document, with hyperparameter µ > 0 and mean given by the collection
model. The posterior mean under this prior yields

                           c(t, d) + µ p(t | D )     |d|                    µ
          pDir (t | d) =                         =         pML (t | d) +         p(t | D ),
                                  |d| + µ          |d| + µ               |d| + µ

which can be interpreted as a data-dependent interpolation where the mixture weight is de-
termined by document length: short documents (small |d|) are smoothed heavily towards
p(t | D ), while long documents retain estimates closer to their empirical distribution [Zhai
and Lafferty, 2004]. In both Jelinek-Mercer and Dirichlet formulations, smoothing can be
viewed as an instance of Bayesian regularisation or as a bias-variance trade-off. Zhai and
Lafferty [2004] provide a systematic comparison of smoothing methods for retrieval and
demonstrate that the choice of smoothing interacts with query length and collection charac-
teristics.
      An important consequence of these estimators is that they embed a particular notion of
document-length normalisation. In the Dirichlet case, the effective contribution of document
evidence is scaled by |d|/(|d| + µ), so length enters the model directly as a factor controlling
the strength of the prior. Even in fixed-λ interpolation, the denominator ∑t c(t, d) = |d|
influences the ML component. Thus, although framed as probability estimation, classical
language models encode preferences about document verbosity and informativeness through
their smoothing structure. This implicit normalisation will later be contrasted with explicit
length penalties in heuristic ranking functions such as BM25.
      . The bag-of-words language model assumes that documents and queries share a com-
mon vocabulary, but in practice relevant documents often use different terms than the query.
Translation-based language models address this vocabulary mismatch by modelling the
probability of a query term t being generated from a document as a mixture over possible
“source” terms t ′ in the document, weighted by a translation probability p(t | t ′ ) [Berger and
Lafferty, 1999]. This yields a smoothed document model of the form p(t | d) = ∑t ′ ∈d p(t |
t ′ )pML (t ′ | d), which allows a document containing “automobile” to receive non-zero prob-
ability for the query term “car” even if “car” never appears in the document. More recently,
Ganguly et al. [2015] extended this idea by using word embeddings to estimate the trans-
lation probabilities, showing that continuous word representations trained on large corpora
can provide effective soft term-matching within the language modelling framework. These
translation-based approaches can be understood as early instances of the vocabulary mis-
match solutions that neural ranking models later address through dense representations.


2.3.1    Language Models for Ranking
Language modelling becomes a ranking technique when the document language models are
used to assess how likely a document would be to generate the observed query. In the query
likelihood framework, each document d is associated with a smoothed term distribution p(· |
d), and a query q is treated as a short sample generated from that distribution. Under the
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                                  18

unigram assumption and conditional independence of query terms given d,

                                  p(q | d) = ∏ p(t | d)c(t,q) ,
                                             t∈V

where c(t, q) is the query term frequency. Taking logarithms and discarding constants that
do not depend on d yields the scoring function

                       sQL (q, d) ∝ log p(q | d) = ∑ c(t, q) log p(t | d),                 (2.4)
                                                   t∈q


with p(t | d) given by a smoothed estimator such as pJM or pDir [Ponte and Croft, 1998, Zhai
and Lafferty, 2004]. Documents are ranked by decreasing sQL (q, d).
    This formulation has several consequences. First, ranking decomposes into a sum of per-
term contributions, each modulated by both the query count c(t, q) and the document prob-
ability p(t | d). Second, smoothing determines how strongly rare or unseen terms influence
the score: if p(t | d) is close to the collection model for a term, its contribution approximates
log p(t | D ) and thus behaves similarly across many documents. Third, document length
enters implicitly via the estimator p(t | d): under Dirichlet smoothing, the relative effect of
the prior and of the document counts depends on |d|, so shorter documents are effectively
normalised more heavily than longer ones. In this sense, query likelihood exemplifies a
probability-based dynamic ranking model built on top of a static document representation
(term counts and length).
    Alternative derivations connect query-likelihood scoring to expected risk minimisation
and to divergence measures between probability distributions. For instance, one may treat
the query as defining an empirical query model p(t | q) ∝ c(t, q), and interpret retrieval as se-
lecting documents whose language models p(· | d) minimise the divergence between p(· | q)
and p(· | d), e.g., via Kullback-Leibler divergence [Lafferty and Zhai, 2001]. Divergence-
from-randomness (DFR) approaches instead measure how surprising the observed within-
document term statistics are under a random reference model, again leading to additive scor-
ing functions in which each term’s contribution reflects its deviation from corpus-typical
behaviour [Amati and Van Rijsbergen, 2002]. These views highlight that classical term-
weighting schemes can often be seen as approximations to, or reparameterisations of, under-
lying language-modelling principles.
    Within the broader probabilistic relevance framework, the Best Match (BM) family of
models provides a complementary development grounded in term-occurrence probabilities.
The starting point is a binary independence model in which each query term t is associated
with two Bernoulli parameters: the probability of occurrence in a document given relevance,
and the probability given non-relevance [Robertson and Spärck Jones, 1976]. Under con-
ditional independence assumptions, the log-odds of relevance can be expressed as a sum of
per-term contributions, each proportional to a log ratio of these probabilities. This yields
inverse document frequency (IDF)-like weights and motivates an additive scoring function
over query terms.
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                               19

    Early BM formulations (e.g., BM11, BM15) primarily distinguished between presence
and absence of terms. Subsequent refinements incorporated term frequency (tf) effects and
document length into the model to capture the intuition that multiple occurrences should
increase evidence of topicality, but with diminishing returns, and that longer documents
should not be unfairly advantaged simply because they contain more words [Robertson and
Walker, 1994]. This principled progression leads to BM25, whose standard form can be
written as

                                          (k1 + 1) tf(t, d)        (k3 + 1) tf(t, q)
          sBM25 (q, d) = ∑ IDF(t)                              ·                   ,   (2.5)
                         t∈q        k1 (1 − b) + b |d| + tf(t, d) k3 + tf(t, q)
                                                    |d|


with hyperparameters k1 , b, k3 , document length |d|, corpus-average length |d|, and term
frequencies tf(t, d) and tf(t, q) [Robertson and Walker, 1994, Robertson and Zaragoza, 2009].
     Two aspects of BM25 are particularly important for later developments in this thesis. The
first is term-frequency saturation. The factor

                                       (k1 + 1) tf(t, d)
                                         k1 + tf(t, d)

grows with term frequency but at a sub-linear rate, so that the marginal gain from additional
occurrences of a term declines. This encodes a preference for concise, focused evidence
over unbounded repetition. In Chapter 6, we show that this saturation property provides nat-
ural immunity to keyword-stuffing attacks that exploit neural models lacking an equivalent
mechanism.
    The second is the explicit length normalisation term
                                                           !
                                                     |d|
                                    k1 (1 − b) + b
                                                     |d|

in the denominator. When b = 0, the denominator reduces to k1 , so there is no length-based
penalty and documents are compared purely on term frequency. When b = 1, the scaling
becomes proportional to |d|/|d|, which corresponds to a pivoted length normalisation scheme
in which documents longer than the corpus average are penalised and shorter documents
are relatively favoured [Singhal et al., 1996]. Intermediate values 0 < b < 1 interpolate
between these extremes, allowing practitioners to tune the strength of the length penalty to
the collection and task.
    From the perspective of this thesis, BM25 can be viewed as a dynamic scoring function
applied to a static document representation (tf and length) that exposes its length normalisa-
tion design explicitly through b and |d|. In contrast, language-model-based query likelihood
implements a more implicit length normalisation via the dependence of smoothing on |d|.
Despite these differences, both families of models share a common structure: scores de-
compose into per-term contributions that combine corpus-level statistics (IDF or p(t | D )),
within-document term frequencies, and functions of document length.
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                             20

Dynamic Ranking

The scoring functions introduced so far, query likelihood and BM25, are examples of dy-
namic (query-dependent) ranking signals. A dynamic signal is any component of s(q, d)
whose value depends jointly on both the query q and the document d. Formally, given a
collection D and a query q, a ranking function induces an ordering of D according to scores
s(q, d) ∈ R. If s changes when q changes, even though d is fixed, then s is dynamic.
    Dynamic ranking components have several characteristic roles. Matching captures whether
the document contains query terms, with term frequency and inverse document frequency
modulating the strength of evidence [Robertson and Zaragoza, 2009]. Contextualisation ex-
ploits how those terms appear (e.g., proximity, phrase structure, or local context), so that
the same document can be scored differently depending on which aspects the query empha-
sises [Metzler and Croft, 2005, Tao and Zhai, 2007]. Adaptation adjusts to query-specific
phenomena such as rare terms, verbose queries, or different intent types (navigational, infor-
mational, transactional), often through query-dependent weighting schemes [Broder, 2002,
Bendersky et al., 2012].
    Extensions of classical language-modelling and probabilistic frameworks typically en-
rich the dynamic component rather than replacing it. Proximity-aware models augment
s(q, d) with features based on distances between query term occurrences in d, under the
intuition that relevant documents tend to mention related query terms near one another [Tao
and Zhai, 2007]. Term-dependence models such as the Markov Random Field (MRF) frame-
work introduce structured potentials over query unigrams, ordered bigrams, and unordered
windows, yielding dynamic scores that account for simple phrase structure and local co-
occurrence patterns [Metzler and Croft, 2005]. Mixture models treat documents as combi-
nations of topical and background components, allowing s(q, d) to focus on the topical part
most aligned with the query while discounting general or boilerplate text [Wei and Croft,
2006].
    Structured document representations lead to further dynamic variants. Fielded retrieval
models decompose each document into fields (e.g., title, body, anchor text, URL) and learn
per-field weights or priors. A common pattern is to define field-specific language models
p(t | d, f ) and aggregate them into a composite score,

                                 s(q, d) = ∑ α f s f (q, d),
                                           f ∈F


where s f is the dynamic score derived from field f and α f are field weights reflecting the
relative importance of each structure [Robertson et al., 2004, Ogilvie and Callan, 2003].
Although the field weights themselves may be query-independent, each s f (q, d) remains
dynamic because it depends on the interaction between q and the content of that field.
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                                 21

Static Ranking

In contrast, static (query-independent) ranking signals are functions of the document alone.
A static score g(d) is defined independently of any particular query and can therefore be
computed once, at index time, for all documents in the collection. Intuitively, static signals
encode prior beliefs about the usefulness, authority, quality, or other properties of a docu-
ment, before observing a query. When combined with a dynamic score, they modulate the
final ranking in query-independent ways.
    Classical examples of static signals arise from link analysis and temporal features. PageR-
ank, HITS, and related graph-based measures assign higher scores to documents that are cen-
trally positioned in the hyperlink graph or that receive endorsements from other high-scoring
documents [Brin and Page, 1998, Kleinberg, 1999]. Such signals can be interpreted as pri-
ors over document importance or authority, reflecting properties of the collection rather than
any particular information need. Temporal features, such as freshness or recency, provide
another class of static signals: a news article may be more useful when it is recent, regard-
less of the exact query terms used to retrieve it [Li and Croft, 2003, Dai et al., 2011]. Other
static features include source reputation, document type (e.g., homepage vs. deep page), and
coarse-grained quality assessments [Craswell et al., 2005].
    A common abstraction is to decompose the overall ranking score into dynamic and static
components,
                                    s(q, d) = f (q, d) + g(d),                             (2.6)

where f (q, d) is a dynamic score (e.g., query likelihood, BM25, or a neural similarity func-
tion) and g(d) is a static prior capturing query-independent preferences [Craswell et al.,
2005, Robertson and Zaragoza, 2009]. In practice, g(d) may itself be a weighted combi-
nation of multiple static features, and its influence can be tuned by a scaling parameter or
learned jointly with other model parameters.
     Static ranking signals also have important implications for efficiency. Because g(d) is
query-independent, it can be used to organise inverted files or document lists in advance.
For example, postings within each inverted list can be ordered by decreasing g(d), so that
documents with higher prior scores are examined first at query time [Anh and Moffat, 2001,
Ding and Suel, 2011]. This ordering supports early termination strategies in top-k retrieval:
if the dynamic scores for lower-priority documents are unlikely to exceed the current top-
k threshold, the system can safely stop scoring them, reducing query-time cost. Similarly,
static scores can guide pruning decisions, such as discarding documents whose prior quality
is below a threshold, shortening postings lists and reducing index size [Büttcher and Clarke,
2006].


2.4     Learning-to-Rank
The probabilistic and heuristic models discussed so far (e.g., query likelihood, BM25, static
priors) define parametric scoring functions s(q, d) whose parameters are tuned manually or
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                                   22

via simple heuristic calibration. Learning-to-rank (LtR; also referred to as LeTOR in earlier
literature) replaces this manual design with a data-driven approach: it assumes access to
training data that indicate, for many queries q, which documents are preferred (e.g., via
relevance labels or clicks), and it learns a scoring function sθ (q, d), parameterised by θ , that
predicts these preferences. In its most general form, LtR operates on a feature representation
x(q, d) that may combine dynamic and static components:
                                                                
                                x(q, d) = xdyn (q, d), xstat (d) ,

and learns parameters θ so that sθ (q, d) ranks relevant documents ahead of non-relevant ones
according to some loss or utility function [Liu, 2009].
    Training data can come from explicit judgments (as in Cranfield-style collections) or
from implicit feedback, most prominently click logs. Explicit labels are typically of higher
fidelity but expensive to obtain and limited in scale; click data are abundant but noisy and
biased. A central theme in modern LtR is therefore how to exploit large-scale behavioural
data without inheriting their systematic biases, particularly positional bias.


2.4.1    Supervision and Positional Bias
Click logs record which results users click in response to queries. A naïve interpretation
treats clicks as positive labels (relevant) and non-clicked results as negative labels (non-
relevant). However, users do not examine all ranks uniformly. The probability that a docu-
ment at rank i is clicked depends both on its relevance and on whether the user examined that
rank at all. Positional bias refers to the empirical regularity that higher-ranked documents
receive more clicks, even when they are not more relevant, simply because users are more
likely to see them [Joachims et al., 2005, Craswell et al., 2008].
     Formally, let Ei be the event that the result at rank i is examined and Ri be the event that
it is relevant. A simple position-based model assumes

                        Pr(click at rank i) = Pr(Ei ) Pr(click | Ei , Ri ),

with Pr(Ei ) depending on rank (decreasing with i) and Pr(click | Ei , Ri ) depending on rel-
evance but not on position [Richardson et al., 2006]. In historical logs, Pr(Ei ) is typically
unknown and depends on the ranking function that produced those logs. If one directly trains
sθ (q, d) to predict clicks from such data, the learned model may overfit to presentation effects
of the logging system, learning to reproduce its biases rather than to approximate underlying
relevance.
    Counterfactual and propensity-based LtR frameworks make this explicit. Under such
approaches, one estimates a propensity w(q, d) that approximates the probability that (q, d)
would have been observed (examined) under the logging policy, and then reweights observed
clicks by 1/w(q, d) to obtain an unbiased estimator of the target risk [Joachims et al., 2017,
Wang et al., 2016]. This allows learning from biased logs while controlling, at least in
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                                        23

expectation, for positional and other presentation biases.


2.4.2     Learning Objectives
LtR methods are often categorised by the granularity at which they compare predictions to
supervision: pointwise (individual documents), pairwise (pairs of documents), or listwise
(entire ranked lists). Each family defines a surrogate loss for ranking utilities that are typi-
cally non-differentiable and non-convex.

Pointwise. Pointwise methods treat LtR as a regression or classification problem on in-
dividual (q, d) pairs. Given a label y(q, d), which may be binary or graded, one defines
                           
a loss ℓ sθ (q, d), y(q, d) and minimises the empirical risk [Li et al., 2007, Cossock and
Zhang, 2008]. Pointwise approaches are simple, scale well, and can naturally incorporate
heterogeneous features, including static priors and click-derived signals. However, they do
not directly model the relative ordering of documents and are only indirectly aligned with
rank-based utilities such as nDCG or MAP.

Pairwise. Pairwise methods take the ordering of documents for the same query as the prim-
itive object. For each query q, one samples pairs (d + , d − ) such that d + is preferred to d −
according to relevance labels or clicks. The model is then trained to ensure sθ (q, d + ) >
sθ (q, d − ) via a loss on the score difference:

                       Lpair = Eq E(d + ,d − )∼Pq ℓ sθ (q, d + ) − sθ (q, d − ) ,
                                                                               
                                                                                                 (2.7)

where Pq specifies the sampling distribution over preference pairs [Burges et al., 2005, Fre-
und et al., 2003]. Pairwise objectives are more closely aligned with the goal of ranking
relevant documents ahead of non-relevant ones and can be related to the optimisation of
pairwise ranking metrics (e.g., AUC).

Listwise. Listwise methods operate at the level of entire ranked lists. Given a query q
and a set of candidate documents {d1 , . . . , dn } with labels {y1 , . . . , yn }, listwise losses de-
fine a distribution over permutations or over relevance-weighted gains and optimise sθ so
that the induced ranking approximates an ideal one [Cao et al., 2007, Xia et al., 2008].
Examples include ListMLE, which treats the ground-truth permutation as an outcome of a
Plackett-Luce model parameterised by scores sθ (q, di ) [Xia et al., 2008], and LambdaRank
and LambdaMART, which derive per-document gradients approximating the gradient of a
target metric like nDCG [Burges et al., 2006, Burges, 2010].


2.4.3     Model Classes and Feature Integration
Historically, LtR systems have been predominantly feature-based. Engineers design a vector
of features x(q, d) that combines lexical signals (e.g., BM25 scores, language-model scores),
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                                24

link-based features (PageRank, in-link counts), temporal features (age, freshness), and be-
havioural features (click counts, dwell time), together with document-only features capturing
static priors (length, quality scores, authority) [Qin et al., 2010]. Gradient-boosted decision
tree models, especially LambdaMART, became widely adopted in industrial systems due to
their strong empirical performance, robustness to heterogeneous feature scales, and inter-
pretability [Burges, 2010, Wu et al., 2010].
    Modern pipelines increasingly integrate neural representations. Pre-trained language
models provide dense vector encodings of queries and documents, which can be used ei-
ther as additional features in a feature-based LtR model or as the primary input to end-to-end
neural scoring functions [Nogueira and Cho, 2019, Karpukhin et al., 2020].


2.4.4    Neural Language Models for Ranking
Pre-trained neural language models (NLMs) provide contextual representations of text that
can be adapted to ranking. Given an input sequence of tokens, a transformer-style encoder
produces a sequence of contextualised embeddings, from which one can derive representa-
tions for queries and documents [Vaswani et al., 2017, Devlin et al., 2019]. In contrast to
classical bag-of-words models, NLMs encode word order, long-range dependencies, and se-
mantic similarity, making them attractive as components of fθ (q, d) in Eq. (2.1). Training
is typically in two stages: large-scale pre-training on generic language-modelling objectives
(e.g., masked language modelling, next-token prediction), followed by task-specific fine-
tuning for retrieval or ranking [Devlin et al., 2019, Radford et al., 2018].
     Architecturally, neural rankers differ in how they couple q and d within the model and
how the resulting scores are used within the retrieval pipeline. We discuss four prominent
families: cross-encoders, which provide the highest-capacity dynamic scores; bi-encoders,
which trade interaction depth for efficiency; late-interaction models, which occupy a middle
ground; and learned sparse retrievers, which produce vocabulary-indexed weights compati-
ble with classical inverted-file infrastructure. Figure 2.1 sketches the four families side by
side, and Table 2.1 summarises their coupling pattern, document representation, indexing
route, and representative instances.

Cross-encoders. Cross-encoders concatenate q and d and feed the joint sequence through a
transformer, allowing full cross-attention between all query and document tokens [Nogueira
and Cho, 2019]. A typical input format is

                           [CLS] q1 , . . . , qm [SEP] d1 , . . . , dn [SEP],

and the score is derived from the contextualised [CLS] embedding or a pooling over selected
positions. Because q and d are processed together, cross-encoders can model fine-grained
interactions, including phrase alignment, paraphrasing, and contextual disambiguation, lead-
ing to strong effectiveness as re-rankers. However, they are computationally expensive: en-
coding must be repeated for each (q, d) pair, and there is no straightforward ANN index.
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                                                           25


                Bi-Encoder                                            Cross-Encoder
     independent encoding, similarity scoring                     joint encoding, direct scoring

                       s(q, d)                                                      s(q, d)


                                                                                 MLP head
                        ⟨· , ·⟩
                                                                              linear, scalar output



        vq                                   vd                                                  v[CLS]




              Query               Document                                    Cross-Encoder
             Encoder               Encoder                      joint self-attention over all q and d tokens



              query               document              [CLS]    q1      q2      [SEP]     d1     d2      d3   [SEP]
                q                    d


Figure 2.1: The two primary families of neural ad hoc rankers. Cross-encoders pay all of
their cost online; bi-encoders (and variants: late-interaction and learned sparse retrievers)
amortise document-side work into the index. See Table 2.1 for the matched comparison.


Consequently, cross-encoders are typically applied only to a candidate set (e.g., the top-k
from BM25 or a bi-encoder) [Nogueira et al., 2020].
    Sequence-to-sequence models such as monoT5 [Nogueira et al., 2020] adapt the cross-
encoder paradigm to an encoder-decoder architecture. Given the concatenated (q, d) as input,
the decoder is trained to generate a relevance token (e.g., “true” or “false”), and the log-
probability of the positive token is used as s(q, d). Despite the generative framing, this is
functionally a cross-encoder: the model scores a single (q, d) pair at a time using full cross-
attention, and the “generation” is a single token whose logit serves as a scalar score.
    List-wise LLM-based re-rankers such as RankGPT [Sun et al., 2023] and RankZephyr [Pradeep
et al., 2023] extend the cross-encoder paradigm by presenting multiple candidate documents
simultaneously and generating a permutation. These models condition on the relative con-
text of competing documents, which can improve effectiveness but introduces sensitivity to
the input ordering, a form of positional bias that Chapters 5 and 7 investigate.

Bi-encoders (dual encoders). Bi-encoders independently encode queries and documents
using a shared or closely related encoder. Let Eθ denote an encoder that, given a text se-
quence, outputs a sequence of contextualised token embeddings. A query q and a document
d are mapped to sequences

                         Eθ (q) = (q1 , . . . , qm ),     Eθ (d) = (d1 , . . . , dn ),

and a scoring function s(q, d) is defined by applying suitable pooling operations to these
sequences.
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                                     26

between query and document, the form of the stored document representation, the index
structure used for retrieval, and representative models.

                                                                                Contriever [Izacard
