    A common design is to pool each sequence into a single fixed-dimensional vector and
score with a simple similarity. Typical pooling choices include: [CLS] pooling, where
the special classification token prepended to every input during pre-training serves as a
sequence-level summary representation, and its final hidden state is taken as the embedding:
e(x) = xCLS [Devlin et al., 2019, Karpukhin et al., 2020]; mean pooling, which averages
token embeddings: e(x) = L1 ∑Li=1 xi [Reimers and Gurevych, 2019]; and learned pooling, in
which an additional attention or gating mechanism learns to weight token embeddings before
aggregation [Lee et al., 2019].
    With such pooling, we obtain vector representations
                                                                     
                            e(q) = P Eθ (q) ,          e(d) = P Eθ (d) ,

and define the score via the dot product:

                                       s(q, d) = e(q) · e(d),                                 (2.8)

or cosine similarity [Karpukhin et al., 2020, Xiong et al., 2021]. Because e(d) depends
only on d, document embeddings can be pre-computed and indexed. To avoid scoring every
document in the corpus at query time, pre-computed document embeddings are organised
in an approximate nearest neighbour (ANN) index, a data structure that enables sub-linear
retrieval by searching only the regions of the embedding space likely to contain high-scoring
documents, trading a small amount of recall for a large reduction in latency [Johnson et al.,
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                                   27

2021, Malkov and Yashunin, 2020].

Late-interaction models. Single-vector pooling compresses all token-level information
into one representation, which may obscure fine-grained term-level interactions. Late-interaction
models address this by retaining token-level embeddings for documents and performing a
structured interaction at scoring time. The document representation is the full sequence
(d1 , . . . , dn ), and the score is defined via an aggregation such as

                                               m
                                   s(q, d) = ∑ max qi · d j ,
                                              i=1 1≤ j≤n

as in ColBERT [Khattab and Zaharia, 2020]. This approach of treating documents and
queries as bags of vectors and aggregating term-level similarities via max or mean operations
was explored prior to ColBERT by Roy et al. [2016], who showed that such aggregations can
capture fine-grained matching structure. Document token embeddings can be stored in com-
pressed form and indexed for efficient retrieval, preserving more of the matching structure
than a single-vector embedding while remaining cheaper than full cross-attention.

Learned sparse retrievers. Learned sparse retrievers share the dual-tower input layout of
bi-encoders but produce a different output: a high-dimensional sparse vector wd ∈ R|V | over
the vocabulary V , where each component wd,t is a learned weight for token type t in docu-
ment d [Formal et al., 2021, 2022a]. The encoder computes contextual token embeddings,
projects each into vocabulary-sized logits, and aggregates and regularises across positions
using activations and penalties (e.g., log(1 + exp(·)), ℓ1 regularisation) that encourage spar-
sity. The resulting wd can be stored in a standard inverted file, with wd,t filling the role of
term frequency or BM25-style weight, rather than in an ANN structure. Queries are encoded
similarly into sparse vectors wq , and scoring reduces to a weighted dot product

                                    s(q, d) = ∑ wq,t wd,t ,
                                                t∈V

which is efficiently computable via postings lists [Formal et al., 2022a]. Learned sparse
retrievers therefore act as neural generalisations of classical bag-of-words models, where
term importance and query expansion are learned from data but retrieval remains index-
compatible with the inverted-file infrastructure of §2.3.1. Two consequences of this indexing
route are relevant to later chapters. First, learned sparse models internalise document-side
structure (term salience, expansion to related vocabulary) inside the document encoder, so
an external prior placed on top of them is at risk of double counting that structure; this
issue surfaces in Chapter 5. Second, because the document representation is term-indexed,
learned sparse retrievers inherit the auditability of classical lexical models in a way that dense
bi-encoders do not.
    Training for the three dual-tower families (dense bi-encoders, late-interaction, learned
sparse) is typically contrastive. The model is encouraged to assign higher scores to (q, d + )
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                               28

pairs judged or inferred to be relevant than to (q, d − ) pairs. In-batch negatives and mined
hard negatives are central: for each query, the other documents in the minibatch, together
with documents retrieved by strong lexical baselines (e.g., BM25) but judged non-relevant,
serve as negative examples [Karpukhin et al., 2020, Xiong et al., 2021]. Earlier neural in-
teraction models such as DRMM [Guo et al., 2016] and KNRM [Xiong et al., 2017], which
use histogram-based or kernel-based matching over term similarities, established many of
the training patterns that bi-encoder and late-interaction models later adopted.

Beyond the canonical families. A growing body of work sits outside the four-family tax-
onomy above and is worth flagging because it reappears in later chapters. Generative re-
trieval [Tay et al., 2022] bypasses the dot-product between query and document represen-
tations altogether: a sequence-to-sequence model is trained to emit document identifiers
conditioned on the query, so that the retrieval distribution is parameterised directly by the
model’s decoder. Document expansion approaches enrich the document side rather than the
model. Doc2Query [Nogueira et al., 2019] trains a generator to produce queries a document
could answer and concatenates them to the document before indexing, which improves recall
in both classical and learned indexes without changing the scoring function. Query expan-
sion via generation acts symmetrically: HyDE [Gao et al., 2023] prompts a language model
to draft a plausible answer document for the query and encodes that hypothetical document
with a standard bi-encoder, avoiding the query/document encoding asymmetry in zero-shot
settings. Finally, the late-interaction family has been refined beyond the original ColBERT
formulation: token-level vectors can be quantised, residual-compressed, and served from ap-
proximate centroid indexes, with PLAID-style infrastructure scaling the design to corpora at
which the naive max-sim is intractable [Lawrie et al., 2024]. These extensions inherit the of-
fline/online split but redistribute work between the document encoder, the query-time scorer,
and an external generator; the prior-likelihood lens of §2.1 applies to each.

Dynamic Neural Ranking

In the neural setting, the dynamic component fθ (q, d) in Eq. (2.1) is instantiated by the
architectures above. For bi-encoders and late-interaction models, the query representation is
computed at query time, and scores are obtained by comparing it to pre-computed document
representations via Eq. (2.8) or a late-interaction function. For cross-encoders, both query
and document are processed jointly at query time, yielding high-capacity dynamic scores
that capture fine-grained interactions.
    A common multi-stage pipeline illustrates the interplay between these models:

   1. A first-stage retriever (e.g., BM25 or a bi-encoder) retrieves a candidate set of docu-
      ments for q.

   2. A neural re-ranker (often a cross-encoder) computes fθ (q, d) for each candidate and
      reorders them.
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                                  29

   3. Optionally, a downstream generative model consumes the top-ranked documents to
      produce answers, summaries, or further judgments.

In this pipeline, the first-stage retriever emphasises recall and efficiency, while the re-ranker
provides high-precision, query-specific scoring [Nogueira and Cho, 2019, Lin et al., 2021].
    Training objectives for fθ mirror the LtR losses of §2.4.2. Contrastive bi-encoder train-
ing corresponds to pairwise or listwise objectives over (q, d + , d − ) triples, where d + is a
document judged relevant to q and d − is a non-relevant document; cross-encoders are of-
ten fine-tuned with cross-entropy over relevance labels or pairwise margin losses; listwise
neural methods approximate nDCG or related metrics via differentiable relaxations [Bruch
et al., 2019]. Distillation plays a central role: powerful but expensive cross-encoders act as
teachers, providing soft labels or scores that are used to train bi-encoders and late-interaction
models to approximate cross-encoder rankings at much lower cost [Hofstätter et al., 2020].

Static Neural Ranking

Neural language models can also be used to define static priors gφ (d), complementing dy-
namic neural scores. Here, the goal is to learn document-only signals that encode properties
such as quality, authority, topical focus, or risk, which are reusable across queries and com-
putable offline (i.e., at index time, before any query is observed).
   A general template is as follows. A document encoder Eφ maps d to a representation zd ,
and a static scoring head sψ produces a scalar prior:

                              zd = Eφ (d),       gφ (d) = sψ (zd ).

The combined static score gφ (d) = sψ (Eφ (d)) is parameterised by φ (encoder) and ψ (scor-
ing head) respectively. Training signals for gφ can come from several sources.
    Weak supervision from behaviour. Aggregate click-through rates, dwell time, or long-
term engagement statistics provide noisy proxies for document usefulness independent of
any specific query [Dupret and Piwowarski, 2008, Kim, 2014]. By regressing gφ (d) on such
aggregates, the model learns a notion of document quality that can be applied as a static
prior.
    Distilled teacher scores. Dynamic neural or classical rankers produce fθ (q, d) for many
queries; by aggregating these scores across a query distribution, one can derive document-
level targets that capture how consistently d is judged favourably across queries; gφ (d) is
then trained to approximate these teacher-derived scores [Chang et al., 2024].
    Auxiliary document-level tasks. NLMs can be fine-tuned on tasks such as quality es-
timation, factuality detection, spam classification, readability, or topicality; the resulting
predictions define static signals that can be interpreted as priors on the trustworthiness or
usefulness of d [Bendersky et al., 2011, Dai and Callan, 2019].
    The separation between fθ (q, d) and gφ (d) has several practical benefits in neural sys-
tems:
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                                 30

    • Computation and caching. Computing gφ (d) at index time spreads the cost of passing
      documents through expensive neural encoders across all future queries; at query time,
      only fθ (q, d) must be evaluated, and gφ (d) can be added as a cached bias term.

    • Calibration and robustness. Static priors can regularise dynamic scores, dampening
      the influence of spurious matches or adversarial documents; for example, documents
      flagged as low-quality by gφ (d) can be down-weighted irrespective of their dynamic
      similarity to the query [Bendersky et al., 2011].

    • Index organisation. As with classical static signals, neural priors can be used to reorder
      postings, guide pruning, and structure ANN indices so that higher-quality documents
      are considered earlier, improving both effectiveness and efficiency [Tonellotto et al.,
      2018]. This is necessary because query-time latency budgets are fixed: examining
      higher-quality documents first increases the probability that relevant results are found
      within the time budget.

    Chapter 5 exploits this decomposition empirically, showing that a learned static prior
composed with dynamic neural scorers through additive fusion improves effectiveness with-
out additional query-time inference.


2.4.5    Training Neural Ranking Models
Neural ranking models instantiate the dynamic component fθ (q, d) (and, in some cases,
static priors gφ (d)) as parameterised functions trained from data. In contrast to manually
tuned lexical models such as BM25, training involves three interdependent choices: (i) su-
pervision signals and data augmentation strategies, which determine the coverage and fidelity
of the training distribution; (ii) negative sampling schemes, which shape the difficulty and
informativeness of the comparisons the model learns from; and (iii) loss functions, which
define what it means to rank correctly and act as differentiable surrogates for evaluation met-
rics. These choices are not independent: the usefulness of hard negatives depends on the
loss that acts on them, and the effectiveness of a distillation loss depends on the quality of
the teacher whose scores are being approximated. This section covers each in turn before
discussing their interactions.

Data Augmentation and Sampling

The core supervision for ranking consists of preferences between documents for a given
query, derived from explicit relevance labels or implicit feedback (e.g., clicks). For most
collections, such signals are sparse: only a small fraction of query-document pairs are la-
belled, and many queries have few or no explicit positives. Modern training therefore relies
heavily on data augmentation and careful sampling to expose models to informative compar-
isons beyond the raw labels [Hofstätter et al., 2020].
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                                   31

Hard-negative mining. Hard negatives are non-relevant documents that the current model
(or a strong baseline) ranks highly for a query. Training on such negatives encourages the
model to refine its decision boundary close to the positions where mistakes are most conse-
quential (e.g., within the top-k) [Xiong et al., 2021, Zhan et al., 2021]. A typical iterative
hard-negative mining loop proceeds as follows: (1) train an initial model using readily avail-
able negatives (e.g., random negatives or BM25 negatives); (2) periodically freeze the model
and, for each query, retrieve a candidate set of high-scoring documents; (3) from this set,
select documents that are unjudged or judged non-relevant as hard negatives, optionally en-
forcing diversity to avoid near-duplicates; (4) resume training with a mixture of previously
used negatives and newly mined hard negatives. This procedure can be repeated multiple
times as the model improves.
    However, aggressive mining carries a risk of false negatives: documents that are actually
relevant but unjudged in the test collection. To mitigate this, practitioners often filter mined
candidates using a stronger teacher model (e.g., a cross-encoder) that can screen out likely
positives; use label smoothing or soft labels when training on mined sets; or limit mining to
depths where the probability of missing relevant documents is lower [Qu et al., 2021].

In-batch negatives. In-batch negatives exploit the fact that mini-batches typically contain
multiple queries and documents. Under the assumption that queries are sampled approxi-
mately uniformly from a broad query distribution, topical overlap between distinct queries
in the same batch is unlikely, and therefore documents paired with other queries in the batch
can be treated as negatives for a given query [Karpukhin et al., 2020, Henderson et al., 2017].
For a batch of B query-document pairs {(qb , db+ )}Bb=1 , the positive document db+ for query qb
competes against the set {db+′ }b′ ̸=b as negatives. This yields an effective negative set of size
B − 1 per query without additional retrieval.
    In-batch negatives are particularly effective because they provide a large set of negatives
at no additional computational cost beyond what is already required for the forward pass on
the batch. The gradient signal from B − 1 negatives per query is substantially richer than
from a single or small set of negatives [van den Oord et al., 2018], which improves the con-
trastive learning objective’s ability to shape the embedding space. To avoid easy negatives
dominating the loss, it is common to combine in-batch negatives with hard negatives mined
via the iterative procedure described above, and to apply temperature scaling in the softmax
over similarities. Temperature controls the sharpness of the score distribution: lower tem-
peratures concentrate gradient on the hardest negatives, effectively sharpening the decision
boundary, while higher temperatures distribute gradient more uniformly across the negative
set [Xiong et al., 2021].

Training data augmentation via document-to-query generation. Explicit relevance la-
bels are often scarcer on the query side than on the document side: many documents exist
in the corpus without any associated training query. Document-to-query (D2Q) generation
augments training data by learning a model that, given a document (or passage), generates
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                                 32

plausible queries for which that document would be relevant [Nogueira et al., 2019, Bonifa-
cio et al., 2022]. The generated queries, paired with the originating document as a positive,
expand the set of training examples available for contrastive learning. Because generated
queries can be noisy or overly generic, filtering is important: common strategies include per-
plexity thresholds, diversity constraints, and entropy-based filtering that favours queries with
focused term distributions [Wang et al., 2022a].

Loss Functions

Loss functions define the optimisation objective for neural rankers, acting as surrogates for
evaluation metrics such as nDCG or MAP that are non-differentiable and defined over full
rankings. The choice of loss interacts with the architecture (bi-encoder, cross-encoder), with
the sampling strategy (pointwise, pairwise, listwise), and with the nature of supervision (ex-
plicit labels vs. clicks).

Contrastive (InfoNCE-style) objectives. Contrastive losses treat ranking as a discrimi-
nation problem between positive and negative candidates for each query. A widely used
formulation is the InfoNCE loss. Let P denote a set of positive pairs (q, d + ) in a batch, and
for each q let Nq be the associated set of negatives (e.g., in-batch negatives and/or mined
hard negatives). With temperature τ > 0, the loss is

                                                     exp(s(q, d + )/τ)
                     LNCE = −      ∑       log                                .           (2.9)
                                   +
                                (q,d )∈P         ∑d∈Nq ∪{d + } exp(s(q, d)/τ)

This objective encourages the score of the positive document to exceed those of all negatives
by a margin controlled by τ [van den Oord et al., 2018, Karpukhin et al., 2020].

Pairwise and margin-based losses. Pairwise losses operate on ordered pairs (d + , d − ) for
a fixed query, aiming to enforce s(q, d + ) > s(q, d − ). Common instantiations include a logis-
tic loss ℓ(∆) = log(1 + exp(−∆)) with ∆ = s(q, d + ) − s(q, d − ) [Burges et al., 2005], and a
hinge loss ℓ(∆) = max{0, 1 − ∆}.
     Margin-based mean squared error (margin-MSE) becomes particularly useful in distil-
lation settings. Suppose a teacher model provides scores sT (q, d) and we are interested
in preserving score gaps ∆T = sT (q, d + ) − sT (q, d − ). The student model computes ∆S =
sS (q, d + ) − sS (q, d − ), and the loss is
                                                                2
                                 ℓmargin-MSE = ∆S − α ∆T

for some scaling factor α [Hofstätter et al., 2020]. This encourages the student not only to
order pairs correctly, but also to match the teacher’s notion of how strongly one document
should be preferred over another.
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                                     33

Listwise and metric-surrogate objectives. Listwise losses consider entire ranked lists of
candidates for a query. Examples include ListMLE [Xia et al., 2008] and LambdaRank/
LambdaMART [Burges et al., 2006, Burges, 2010]. In LambdaMART, per-document pseudo-
gradients λi are defined as

                         |∆nDCGi j |                                |∆nDCG ji |
 λi =     ∑ 1 + exp sθ (q, di) − sθ (q, d j ) − ∑ 1 + exp sθ (q, d j ) − sθ (q, di) , (2.10)
        j: yi >y j                                  j: y j >yi


where |∆nDCGi j | is the absolute change in nDCG obtained by swapping documents di and d j
in the current ranking [Burges, 2010]. These pseudo-gradients weight pairwise corrections
by their impact on the target metric, combining the tractability of pairwise training with
direct sensitivity to ranking quality. More recent approaches use differentiable relaxations
of ranking operators to define smooth surrogates for rank-based metrics [Bruch et al., 2019,
Cuturi et al., 2019].


2.5     Generalisation of Neural Ranking Models
Generalisation for neural ranking models encompasses several related questions: (i) in-
domain robustness, how stable effectiveness is within the training domain under variations
in topic mix, query formulation, or evaluation conditions; (ii) cross-domain transfer, how
well a model trained on one collection or domain performs on others with different vocab-
ularies, document styles, or relevance notions; and (iii) temporal stability, the rate at which
performance degrades as the underlying distribution over queries, documents, and relevance
labels, P(q, d, r), evolves over time [Thakur et al., 2021].
     Cross-collection evaluations, in which a model trained on one or more source collections
is tested on disjoint target collections, reveal that neural rankers can overfit to the training do-
main when supervision is narrow: models often latch onto characteristic lexical or structural
regularities of the training corpus and fail to generalise to new domains, even when those
domains are closely related [Thakur et al., 2021, Rosa et al., 2022]. This brittleness is exac-
erbated when training relies heavily on labels derived from a single platform, as positional
and presentation biases can be absorbed into the learned scoring function.
     Several strategies have been proposed to improve generalisation. Multi-domain training
exposes the model to a mixture of corpora with diverse topics, styles, and label distributions,
encouraging representations that capture more domain-invariant aspects of relevance [Wang
et al., 2022b]. Lexical grounding, for example via learned sparse representations or hybrid
models that combine dense neural scores with lexical signals, helps preserve sensitivity to
exact term matches and rare vocabulary, which are often underrepresented in dense-only
models trained on limited data [Formal et al., 2022a, Lin et al., 2021]. Teacher diversity
in distillation, using multiple teachers or teachers trained on distinct domains, can reduce
the risk that a student model simply inherits the biases and blind spots of a single teacher
[Hofstätter et al., 2020].
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                                   34

2.5.1    Scaling, Distillation, and Hard Negatives
Scaling the capacity of neural rankers (number of parameters, depth, width) and the size
and diversity of their pre-training data has been one of the most effective ways to improve
zero-shot and few-shot retrieval and re-ranking. Larger pre-trained language models, when
adapted to ranking, generally exhibit better semantic matching, robustness to paraphrasing,
and transfer to new domains [Ni et al., 2022, Neelakantan et al., 2022]. However, returns
from scaling are not monotonic. Improvements can saturate: beyond a certain capacity or
data scale, additional parameters or examples yield diminishing gains for a fixed evaluation
distribution [Kaplan et al., 2020].
     When scaling alone reaches diminishing returns, the quality of supervision becomes the
limiting factor. Two sources of supervision are commonly believed to matter most: distilla-
tion from a strong teacher and the selection of hard negatives. The conventional assumption
is that harder negatives uniformly produce better models, and that a higher-capacity teacher
always yields a stronger student. Chapter 4 challenges both assumptions. It shows that the
effect of negative hardness is substantially smaller than typically assumed, and that what
matters more is the margin distribution of teacher scores: the distribution of score gaps
between positive and negative documents. Several teacher properties modulate student gen-
eralisation, including teacher entropy (overconfident teachers can cause students to overfit to
sharp decision boundaries) and the breadth of the negative candidates used when querying
the teacher [Hofstätter et al., 2020, Xiong et al., 2021].


2.5.2    Continuous Adaptation
Deployed retrieval systems operate in non-stationary environments. The joint distribution
P(q, d, r) evolves over time due to topic drift, vocabulary shift, interface changes, and shifting
user intents [Dalton et al., 2020]. A model that performs well at deployment time may
degrade as the collection grows or as user populations change.
    The ranking-relevant part of Eq. (2.1), s(q, d) = fθ (q, d) + gφ (d) (dropping the per-
query-constant hψ (q)), suggests a natural separation of concerns for adaptation. The static
prior gφ (d) of Eq. (2.1) reflects properties of the documents and the collection and may
be updated offline as the corpus changes: new documents may be scored and indexed, and
temporal decay functions may be recalibrated. Consider, for example, a news retrieval sys-
tem: as new articles are published, their gφ scores can be computed at index time without
reprocessing any historical queries or logs. Because gφ is query-independent, its updates
require no reprocessing of past queries or logs. The dynamic component fθ (q, d) encodes
query-document matching behaviour and is more sensitive to distributional shifts in queries
and user intents. For instance, a model trained on pre-pandemic queries may assign sys-
tematically lower scores to documents about remote working because that vocabulary was
absent from its training distribution; this is a case where fθ requires updating but gφ does not.
Updating fθ should therefore be done more conservatively, with explicit safeguards such as
held-out evaluation on recent query samples and canary deployments [Chapelle et al., 2012].
CHAPTER 2. THE RANKING PROBLEM AND SOLUTIONS                                                       35

           (a) Separable scoring                             (b) Collapsed scoring

                          +        s(q, d)         d                         scorer

                                              negative sampling,

Figure 2.2: Separable versus collapsed scoring. (a) Classical rankers instantiate the decom-
position s(q, d) = fθ (q, d) + gφ (d) explicitly, so the query-independent component gφ can
be estimated once and cached at index time. (b) Neural rankers collapse the separation into a
single monolithic scorer, absorbing query-independent structure implicitly through the train-
ing data distribution, the negative-sampling procedure, and end-to-end optimisation. The
costs of this collapse in efficiency, effectiveness, and robustness structure the contribution
chapters of this thesis.


A canary deployment exposes a small fraction of live traffic to the updated model, enabling
early detection of regressions before the model is rolled out fully [Bakshy et al., 2014]. This
decoupled view supports an operational regime in which gφ is updated frequently to track
corpus evolution, while fθ is updated less often and subject to stricter evaluation, balancing
responsiveness with stability.


Summary
The ranking-relevant part of Eq. (2.1), s(q, d) = fθ (q, d) + gφ (d) (dropping the per-query-
constant hψ (q)), provides a unifying lens on the material surveyed in this chapter. Classi-
cal term-weighting functions such as BM25 and query likelihood instantiate both compo-
nents explicitly: the likelihood captures how well a document’s language matches a query,
while length normalisation, smoothing, and IDF weights encode stable beliefs about docu-
ments that hold across queries. Neural ranking models collapse this separation. Whether
implemented as bi-encoder similarity, cross-encoder re-ranking, or learned sparse retrieval,
contemporary models absorb query-independent structure into their parameters through the
training distribution, the negative sampling procedure, and end-to-end optimisation (Fig-
ure 2.2).
    This entanglement imposes costs along three axes that structure the rest of the thesis.
It reduces effectiveness, because modelling capacity is spent recovering structure that is
stable across queries and estimable at index time (Chapter 5). It reduces efficiency, because
the pipelines that produce neural rankers may be partially redundant if the same signals
are available more cheaply from classical supervision, and the evaluation used to justify
them may be unreliable (Chapters 3 and 4). And it reduces robustness, because rankers that
associate textual patterns with relevance can be manipulated by content that inflates scores
without genuine gain, which separable components can detect (Chapters 6 and 7).
Chapter 3

Evaluation Reliability and Benchmark
Saturation

   Hypothesis H1.1 (tested in this chapter)
   Inter-annotator agreement under graded relevance is insufficient to reliably discrimi-
   nate between closely performing neural ranking systems on widely used test collec-
   tions, limiting the practical value of marginal effectiveness gains from increasingly
   expensive non-decomposable models.

     The introduction (Chapter 1) argued that the field’s investment in increasingly expensive,
non-decomposable neural rankers rests on two premises: that the marginal effectiveness
gains reported for each generation of models are real, and that the elaborate pipelines pro-
ducing them are necessary. This chapter examines the first premise. The decomposition
s(q, d) = fθ (q, d) + gφ (d) frames the thesis at the level of modelling; this chapter steps back
to the level of measurement and interrogates the aggregate score s(q, d) itself. If the com-
ponents of a ranking pipeline are to be evaluated independently, a prior question must be
answered first: how reliable is the infrastructure on which such evaluation depends?
     The dominant trajectory in neural ranking research is one of escalating expense. Each
generation of models is trained with more data, distilled from larger teachers, and deployed
with greater inference-time computation. First-stage retrievers are initialised from billion-
parameter language models and fine-tuned with multi-stage negative mining cascades that
themselves require training and querying multiple intermediate models. Re-rankers are dis-
tilled from proprietary LLMs, a process that requires the teacher to be queried once for every
training query in order to label that query’s candidate list [Pradeep et al., 2022, Sun et al.,
2023, Pradeep et al., 2023]. The cost is therefore driven by the size of the training set, not
by any single query: competitive students are distilled from tens of thousands of training
queries, each carrying up to a hundred candidate documents, so the teacher is invoked on
the order of tens of thousands of times, and hundreds of thousands of documents are scored,
during data collection alone. This expense is incurred once at training time and amortised
over inference, but because the teacher is typically a proprietary model behind a paid API
it remains a barrier to reproduction. The resulting systems are then evaluated against static

                                               36
CHAPTER 3. EVALUATION RELIABILITY AND BENCHMARK SATURATION                                                37

test collections where effectiveness differences of a few hundredths of an nDCG point are
reported as meaningful progress. This trajectory rests on the assumption that the evaluation
methodology reliably measures differences between systems at the margins where expensive
models operate, that a reported improvement of ∆nDCG@10 = 0.02 reflects a genuine differ-
ence in ranking quality rather than an artefact of annotator subjectivity, judgment variability,
or benchmark saturation.
    We test this assumption directly. We conduct a large-scale re-annotation of the TREC
Deep Learning 2019 test collection, a passage-ranking collection built on the MSMARCO
corpus that serves as a standard target for the expensive re-rankers under study, using eight
retrieval academics as secondary annotators. We quantify inter-annotator agreement under
graded relevance, a setting that has not been studied in prior re-annotation work, and measure
the stability of system orderings under judgment variation. We find that agreement is lower
than commonly assumed [Voorhees, 2000, Bailey et al., 2008]: Fleiss’ κ values [Fleiss,
1971] approach random under four-grade relevance, and while system orderings are broadly
stable, the current state-of-the-art re-rankers, both those that prompt large language models
directly and those distilled from them, are precisely those whose relative rankings are most
sensitive to annotator variation. We establish an empirical upper bound on the collection
by treating human annotators as oracle systems. State-of-the-art re-rankers have reached or
are approaching parity with this bound. This suggests that further improvements measured
on this collection may reflect overfitting to the specific opinions expressed in its judgments
rather than genuine advances in ranking quality.
    These findings bear on the separability thesis in two ways. First, they constrain the epis-
temic weight that can be placed on benchmark-derived evidence for any system comparison,
including the comparisons reported in other chapters of this thesis. We return to this point
in the conclusions of this chapter, where we distinguish the claims in this thesis that sur-
vive annotation noise from those that are best interpreted as directional trends. Second, they
identify a structural advantage of modular systems over monolithic ones: when a pipeline
is composed of separable components, each component’s contribution can be evaluated, ab-
lated, and stress-tested independently. When a pipeline is monolithic, only the aggregate
outcome can be assessed, and this chapter shows that aggregate assessment is unreliable at
the margins where the most expensive models operate.
    The remainder of the chapter is organised as follows. Section 3.1 reviews prior re-
annotation studies, agreement metrics for graded relevance, the role of query narratives,
and benchmark saturation. Section 3.5 reports the re-annotation of TREC DL’19, the inter-
annotator agreement and system-ordering analyses, and the oracle upper-bound study, and
closes with the verdict on H1.1 and its consequences for the evidential weight of benchmark
comparisons elsewhere in the thesis.1
1 The test collections, systems, software, and compute underpinning this study are consolidated with those of

 the other chapters in Appendix A.
CHAPTER 3. EVALUATION RELIABILITY AND BENCHMARK SATURATION                                    38

3.1     Evaluation Reliability
This section reviews prior work on annotation reliability and test collection reusability that
directly informs our re-annotation study. We cover prior re-annotation studies (Section 3.1.1),
agreement metrics for graded relevance (Section 3.1.2), the role of query narratives (Sec-
tion 3.1.3), and the concept of benchmark saturation (Section 3.1.4).


3.1.1    Prior Re-Annotation Studies
The question of whether relevance judgments are reliable enough to support comparative
evaluation has been investigated in a small number of influential studies spanning several
decades.
    Lesk and Salton [1968] conducted one of the earliest agreement studies, examining medi-
cal documents under binary relevance. They reported overlap of approximately 0.30 between
independent assessors, establishing that substantial disagreement exists even in relatively
constrained domains with well-defined information needs. Burgin [1992] extended this to
an IR test collection, again under binary relevance, and found somewhat higher agreement
(overlap ≈ 0.49), suggesting that agreement varies with domain, document length, and the
specificity of relevance criteria.
    The most influential re-annotation study, and the one this chapter adapts to a modern set-
ting, is that of Voorhees [2000], who investigated TREC-4 and TREC-6 test collections. For
each topic, two secondary assessors independently judged samples of 200 relevant and 200
non-relevant documents drawn from the primary assessor’s pool. Under binary relevance on
newswire documents with full topic descriptions and narratives, Voorhees observed overlap
of approximately 0.36 and system ranking correlation τ ≈ 0.89. The central finding, that
assessor disagreement is substantial at the document level but washes out at the system level,
preserving relative system orderings, became a foundational justification for the Cranfield
paradigm and has been cited to support the reusability of test collections ever since.
    Table 3.1 contrasts these studies with our adaptation. Three differences are critical. First,
modern collections such as TREC DL’19 use short keyword queries without descriptions or
narratives, removing the interpretive constraints that helped assessors in earlier studies agree
on what constitutes relevance. Second, documents in TREC DL’19 are short passages (∼30
words on average) rather than full-length articles of the kind found in earlier collections such
as newswire, scientific articles, web pages, and legal or biomedical text [Voorhees, 2000,
Harman, 1993], potentially increasing ambiguity about whether a passage fully satisfies an
information need. Third, relevance in TREC DL’19 is assessed on a four-grade scale (0-3),
whereas all prior re-annotation studies used binary judgments. Graded relevance introduces
finer distinctions that may amplify disagreement, particularly at intermediate grades where
the boundary between “relevant” and “highly relevant” is subjective.
CHAPTER 3. EVALUATION RELIABILITY AND BENCHMARK SATURATION                                        39

3.1.2    Inter-Annotator Agreement Metrics
Agreement between annotators can be quantified at several levels.
    Let A = {a1 , . . . , aK } be a set of K annotators, D = {d1 , . . . , dN } a set of N documents,
and ri j ∈ {0, 1, . . . , G − 1} the relevance grade assigned by annotator ai to document d j over
a scale of G grades.
    Overlap (also called raw agreement or percent agreement) between two annotators ai and
ak is the proportion of documents on which they assign the same label:

                                                 1 N               
                               OVL(ai , ak ) =     ∑   1 ri j = rk j .                         (3.1)
                                                 N j=1

This measure was used by Voorhees [2000] and provides an intuitive but chance-uncorrected
baseline.
    Cohen’s κ [Cohen, 1960] corrects for the agreement expected by chance under the as-
sumption that each annotator’s marginal label distribution is fixed. Let po = OVL(ai , ak ) be
the observed agreement and pe = ∑G−1g=0 p̂ig p̂kg the expected chance agreement, where p̂ig is
the empirical proportion of grade g assigned by annotator ai . Then

                                                      po − p e
                                      κ(ai , ak ) =            ,                               (3.2)
                                                      1 − pe

with κ = 0 indicating chance agreement and κ = 1 indicating perfect agreement.
    For more than two annotators, Fleiss’ κ [Fleiss, 1971] generalises Eq. (3.2) to K-way
agreement. Let n jg be the number of annotators who assign grade g to document d j , and
           1
let p̄g = KN ∑Nj=1 n jg be the overall proportion of assignments to grade g. The observed
                                                                ∑g n jg (n jg −1)
agreement averaged over documents is p̄o = N1 ∑Nj=1                K(K−1) , and the expected chance
agreement is p̄e = ∑g p̄2g . Fleiss’ κ is then

                                                  p̄o − p̄e
                                         κF =               .                                  (3.3)
                                                   1 − p̄e

Both measures are defined for nominal categories and can be applied to binarised or multi-
grade relevance scales, but they behave differently: under four-grade relevance, κ values
are typically lower than under binarised relevance because there are more opportunities for
disagreement.
    At the system level, agreement is assessed through ranking correlation measures. Kendall’s
τ [Kendall, 1938] counts the proportion of concordant and discordant system pairs, providing
a measure of how consistently two sets of judgments order systems. Spearman’s ρ operates
on rank differences and is sensitive to large rank changes. Rank-biased overlap (RBO) [Web-
ber et al., 2010] extends these by emphasising agreement at the top of the system ranking,
which is often the region of greatest practical interest. We report all three in our analysis.
    An important subtlety is that high system-level correlation can coexist with low document-
level agreement: if disagreement is roughly uniform across documents and systems, the av-
eraging inherent in system-level metrics smooths out individual discrepancies. The question
CHAPTER 3. EVALUATION RELIABILITY AND BENCHMARK SATURATION                                   40

we investigate is whether this smoothing remains effective when the systems being compared
are closely spaced in effectiveness, as is increasingly the case with modern neural rankers,
and when the disagreement is graded rather than binary.


3.1.3    Narrative and Intent Ambiguity
Classical TREC topics typically include three components: a short title (the query), a de-
scription (a sentence-length statement of the information need), and a narrative (a paragraph
specifying what constitutes a relevant document and what does not). Narratives serve as an-
notation guidelines, constraining assessor interpretation and reducing ambiguity about bor-
derline cases [Voorhees, 2001].
    Modern test collections have largely abandoned descriptions and narratives. The MS-
MARCO collection [Nguyen et al., 2016], from which TREC DL’19 draws its queries, pro-
vides only short keyword queries without any accompanying description of intent. This shift
reflects the practical reality of web search, where users issue terse queries and the system
must infer intent. However, for evaluation purposes, the absence of narratives means that as-
sessors must independently interpret the query’s intent, potentially arriving at different rele-
vance standards. Bailey et al. [2008] showed that providing narratives significantly increases
agreement, and that the effect is larger for ambiguous queries where multiple reasonable in-
terpretations exist. In TREC DL’19, such ambiguity is the norm rather than the exception: a
query like “what is a corporation” could be interpreted as seeking a legal definition, a general
explanation, or historical context, and each interpretation implies a different set of relevant
documents.


3.1.4    Benchmark Saturation and Collection Expiry
The iterative use of a fixed test collection to develop and compare systems raises concerns
about overfitting to the specific opinions expressed in its judgments. If each generation of
systems is optimised against the same collection, and if successful design choices propagate
through the community (via shared pre-trained models, training data, and evaluation prac-
tices), then measured improvements may increasingly reflect alignment with the collection’s
particular annotators rather than genuine advances in ranking quality. This phenomenon has
been characterised as “bias by research design”: the influence of previous works implicitly
biases new findings towards a common benchmark [Armstrong et al., 2009].
    If state-of-the-art systems have reached or are approaching parity with the collection’s
own assessors, that is, if system effectiveness approaches the level achievable by a human
annotator operating under the same guidelines, then further measured improvements may re-
flect overfitting to specific annotation decisions rather than improvements in retrieval quality
that would generalise to other assessors or collections. This chapter investigates precisely
this question, treating human annotators as oracle systems and establishing an empirical up-
per bound on the collection.
CHAPTER 3. EVALUATION RELIABILITY AND BENCHMARK SATURATION                                              41

3.2     Iterative Data Augmentation under Test Collections
TREC-style test collections enable the evaluation and, thereby, the development of retrieval
systems. Making these collections robust and reusable is not trivial, partly because of the
inherent subjectivity of relevance [Taube, 1965]. Assessing the reliability of relevance judg-
ments involves assessing inter-annotator agreement and is comparably expensive [Lesk and
Salton, 1968, Burgin, 1992, Voorhees, 2000]. Consequently, only a few studies have in-
vestigated how the variability of relevance judgments affects the evaluation of retrieval sys-
tems. However, those findings were highly important for the design of retrieval test collec-
tions as they showed that even when relevance is subjective and therefore annotators dis-
agree [Schamber et al., 1990, Soboroff, 2025], subsequent aggregated effectiveness evalua-
tions [Lesk and Salton, 1968, Burgin, 1992] and system rankings [Voorhees, 2000] are stable,
ensuring the reusability of test collections.
    Contrasting older collections, topics in modern test collections often do not have de-
scriptions or narratives [Craswell et al., 2019], which is known to affect agreement [Rees
and Schultz, 1967, Voorhees and Harman, 1997], documents are often shorter, and relevance
is assessed on a graded scale, which has not been investigated in re-annotation studies. Ta-
ble 3.1 contrasts previous studies and the properties of the corresponding test collections
with the TREC Deep Learning 2019 [Craswell et al., 2019] (DL’19) test collection we use
in our reproducibility work. The changes in modern collections potentially increase assess-
ment variability because an assessor can freely interpret the query intent and context that
may be missing from the document to assess its relevance on a fine-grained scale. In this
reproducibility study, we investigate if the robustness of classical test collections still holds
for modern test collections under these new conditions.
    These shifts in test collection design coincide with advances in retrieval methodologies;
retrieval pipelines increasingly comprise components where neural language models esti-
mate relevance. These systems have continuously improved over multiple iterations through
improved training processes [Pradeep et al., 2022], through distillation from large language
models [Pradeep et al., 2023, Schlatt et al., 2025], or by directly using large language models
for ranking [Sun et al., 2023]. We hypothesise that iterative model improvements on static
benchmarks may overfit to the specific opinions expressed in static relevance judgments, in-
CHAPTER 3. EVALUATION RELIABILITY AND BENCHMARK SATURATION                                       42

troducing systemic bias, which can be characterised as “bias by research design” in which
the influence of previous works can bias new findings implicitly towards a common bench-
mark [Hovy and Prabhumoye, 2021]. The TREC DL’19 track remains a commonly used
standard for benchmarking systems, being cited and most likely evaluated hundreds of times
(Cited 32 times at ECIR’24 and SIGIR’24 alone). Craswell et al. [2021] alluded to this point,
warning against the iterative design processes employed in ad-hoc neural ranking.
    Thus, we look to replicate the classic setting for evaluating the robustness of collections
proposed by Voorhees [2000] for modern collections, with the additional hypothesis that
bias in the system orderings may be introduced through model iteration in addition to human
disagreement. Since a single expert human assessor’s relevance grades on a given topic are
subjective and “equally plausible” as another assessor’s, we can compare the effectiveness
of systems using multiple “equally correct” sets of relevance judgments. If a system’s rela-
tive effectiveness degrades when evaluated using alternative judgments, it may indicate that
models overfit the preferences expressed by the collection’s official judgments.
    In this paper, we conduct a re-annotation of TREC DL’19 using two “secondary” anno-
tators for every topic. Based on our new relevance judgments, we first provide a qualitative
analysis of this process and validate the findings of previous work in a modern setting. That
is, we observe low agreement among assessors but system rankings remain stable. Secondly,
treating human annotators as manual TREC runs, we find that one can no longer mean-
ingfully discriminate between the state-of-the-art and human judges on this collection. A
reasonable “upper bound” effectiveness is around nDCG@10 of 0.81 (due to ambiguities in
relevance assessment). We suggest that reported results that are higher than this bound on
DL’19 may already overfit to the relevance labels. Note that we are not aware of any exist-
ing system reporting results exceeding this bound.2 However, the leading systems today are
very close, with RankZephyr [Pradeep et al., 2023] and RankGPT [Sun et al., 2023] reaching
between 0.78-0.80 nDCG@10 with a sufficiently strong first stage. We additionally find that
closed-sourced large language models and systems distilled from them can fluctuate greatly
in relative effectiveness, often degrading system rank on our new relevance judgments.
    Our reproducibility effort spawned from the ECIR 2024 Collab-a-thon on Neural IR [MacA-
vaney et al., 2024]. A subset of the IR community discussed that the current generation of
retrieval models may have reached a practical upper bound on existing test collections, but
we were unsure when to consider a test collection to be saturated or expired. Our study re-
produced prior findings on the inherent subjectivity and variability of relevance judgments
(especially in the absence of a well-defined narrative), and it established an empirical upper
bound on TREC DL’19. Therefore, we encourage caution when attempting to maximise a
given metric as we approach and exceed human bounds on relevance estimation. Exceeding
human effectiveness, given the subjectivity of relevance, may not lead to an optimal system
but instead stagnation of progress in the field. The code and relevance judgments produced
for this study are released to support reproduction and replication.
2 We do not consider large ensemble systems, given their risks [Amatriain and Basilico, 2017].
CHAPTER 3. EVALUATION RELIABILITY AND BENCHMARK SATURATION                                       43

3.3     Evaluating Reliability through Re-Annotation
The reliability of a test collection, given the subjectivity of relevance, is often validated by re-
annotation [Lesk and Salton, 1968, Burgin, 1992, Voorhees, 2000]. Modern IR models are
often optimised to mimic previously effective systems through data-driven processes. We
examine how these models, designed after a collection’s release, perform on new judgments
following the process of Voorhees [2000].


3.3.1    Source Study and Its Adaptation
We describe the original annotation process applied by Voorhees [2000]. This work was
carried out as a component of the curation of the TREC-4 [Harman, 1995] and TREC-
6 [Voorhees and Harman, 1997] corpora. As is standard in the curation of pooled TREC
corpora, one “primary" annotator was assigned to each topic, subsequently judging each
document in the pool. Two additional annotators were assigned to each topic to validate the
effect of re-annotation on depth-pooled corpora. A sub-sample of judgments was taken from
the pool and re-annotated. In TREC-4, a maximum of 200 relevant documents per topic
were taken as a secondary pool for re-annotation. Additionally, 200 non-relevant documents
were sampled per topic. A binary judgment was assigned to each query-document pair, rel-
evant or non-relevant. The texts constituting the primary annotation pool, left unjudged in
the secondary pool, are included in all system evaluations, maintaining identical pooling bi-
ases. The comparison of “secondary" annotations was carried out to assess hypotheses that
resurface frequently in IR literature. This work focuses on the subjectivity of relevance and
how pooling affects this subjectivity. Thus, experiments were conducted over a depth-pooled
corpus under construction at the time. This process produces a subset of judgments where
each query-document pair was re-annotated twice.
    Where possible, we follow the evaluation process of Voorhees [2000], quantifying the
probability that two systems swap in relative effectiveness when the assessor set changes.
We define, within an annotation set A, an annotation a ∈ A as a tuple {q, d, r} of query q,
document d, and judgment r ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3}; each set contains one such tuple for every judged
query-document pair. In this study we collect n = 3 annotation sets per topic group, denoted
Ai for i ∈ {1, . . . , n}: the original DL’19 judgments and two secondary re-annotations.
    Assembling a complete judgment set amounts to choosing, independently for each of the
m queries, which of the n annotation sets supplies that query’s judgments. There are therefore
nm distinct judgment sets that the annotation process could have produced; with n = 3 this is
the 3m figure noted by Voorhees [2000]. This count is astronomically large for any realistic
m and cannot be enumerated, so we instead draw s combinations A∗ uniformly at random
(s = 10,000 throughout) and evaluate every system against each, estimating the distribution
of evaluation outcomes over the assessor assignments the process could have yielded.
    To localise instability to individual system comparisons rather than summarise it in a
single number, we measure the pairwise swap rate following Voorhees [2000]. For a set of
systems S we form a matrix B ∈ Z|S|×|S| in which B[i, j] counts the sampled judgment sets
CHAPTER 3. EVALUATION RELIABILITY AND BENCHMARK SATURATION                                              44

under which system Si scores higher than S j on a measure f . The swap rate for the pair (i, j)
is min(B[i,sj],B[ j,i]) , the fraction of assessor assignments under which the pair’s ordering flips
against its majority direction; by the symmetric treatment of the comparison it is bounded in
[0, 0.5]. We use this pairwise quantity, rather than a single rank correlation such as Kendall’s
τ, because τ summarises agreement over the entire system ordering in one number, whereas
the swap rate isolates which specific leaderboard conclusions would fail to survive a change
of assessors. The two views are complementary: τ can be recovered by aggregating the
pairwise disagreements, and we report it (with Spearman’s ρ and RBO) alongside the swap-
rate analysis.


3.3.2     Re-Annotation of a Modern Collection
While the original study focused on binary judgments, our work adapts this methodology
to graded relevance and neural systems under a modern evaluation setting; we describe it as
an adaptation rather than a reproduction, since the collection, the relevance scale, and the
systems under study all differ from the original. Our adaptation is conducted on the TREC
Deep Learning 2019 track (DL’19) [Craswell et al., 2019]. Specifically, we use the passage
collection, which is generally more popular. The proposal of more granular measures that
explicitly consider the ordering of a ranking, such as discounted cumulative gain, as opposed
to set-based measures, such as Precision@k, generally requires graded relevance as opposed
to the binary relevance grades of older collections. DL’19 was annotated under this setting
with four grades of relevance: ‘perfectly relevant,’ ‘highly relevant,’ ‘related’, and ‘non-
relevant.’ This distinction leads to some key differences in our process and analysis.
    We treat the original judgments of DL’19 as those of the “primary” annotator. In our
study, two annotators from a pool of 8 retrieval academics3 were assigned to each topic
and acted as “secondary” annotators (corresponding to 4 topic groups), aligning with the
original study in that each topic was annotated by two new annotators; the original study
does not specify the number of total annotators. The size of the annotator pool used in this
work is similar to that of TREC collections [Soboroff, 2025], albeit with a reduced total
annotation budget due to cost and following the sampling setting of Voorhees [2000]. We
created a secondary pool composed of all documents in the pool with a grade of 1 or more,
and sampled 100 documents per annotator from the non-relevant documents of the primary
pool. We reduced the number of non-relevant judgments from the pool, motivated by the
hypothesis that agreement is far greater over what is non-relevant; for set-based measures,
this choice leads to a balance between relevant and non-relevant texts and has minimal effect
on nDCG, which under depth-k pooling treats documents outside the judged set as non-
relevant regardless of how many non-relevant texts are sampled (see the depth-k pooling
construction of §2.2.1).
    Our pool-creation process used all judged documents at least related to a topic and as-
signed topics to annotators in a round-robin fashion. The round-robin balanced two quanti-
3 These academics contributed to subsequent analysis and write-up in this work.   This decision has minimal
 effect on our findings; nevertheless, we acknowledge this overlap.
CHAPTER 3. EVALUATION RELIABILITY AND BENCHMARK SATURATION                                 45

ties at once: the annotation load carried by each annotator, and the coverage of every topic
by exactly two annotators. Topics were ordered by their number of judged documents and
assigned largest first, so that a priority queue of annotator pairs, ordered by current load,
received the next-largest topic in turn; a second round-robin pass then swapped large topics
onto the least-loaded annotators to even out the totals. Non-relevant texts were then assigned
to each annotator pair, giving a mean annotation load of 1125 pairs per annotator. Balanced
assignment matters because it prevents any single annotator from dominating any one topic,
which would otherwise confound the inter-annotator agreement estimates; the largest-first
ordering serves only to make this greedy load balancing effective and does not affect the
two-annotators-per-topic coverage property, which holds for any topic order.

Annotator Instruction.

Each annotator was provided with annotation guidelines, including the original relevance
grade descriptions from DL’19. Annotators were made aware that instances had been pre-
viously annotated for DL’19; however, they were not provided with relevance grades or a
grade distribution. Due to the specialist knowledge required to assess relevance in, for ex-
ample, medical topics, annotators were allowed to familiarise themselves with topics at their
discretion. To facilitate reproducibility and prevent exposure to discussion and instances
of DL’19 relevance judgments, which may be indexed on common search engines, we in-
stead enforce the use of the ChatNoir search endpoint [Potthast et al., 2012], which indexes
ClueWeb22 [Overwijk et al., 2022] and retrieves using BM25.


3.3.3    Further Investigation of Annotator Disagreement
Beyond our reproduction setting, we utilise re-annotated query-document pairs to investigate
other aspects of collection reliability.

Judgment Aggregation

Voorhees [2000] apply several aggregation operations including combination as described in
Section 3.3.1 and aggregate operators. We perform maximum, minimum, and mean pooling
over judgments. In the case of mean pooling, we do not perform further quantisation; for
example, three texts of grades 1, 1.5, and 2, where the second text was annotated 1 and 2 by
different annotators, would be compared in that order.

Absent Narratives.

The absence of narratives or larger descriptions for the TREC Deep Learning collections
allows an additional focus for our investigation: Annotators were prompted to note cases of
ambiguity and, more generally, their difficulties in annotation. Furthermore, annotators were
asked to create their narratives for each query so that agreement among annotators could
be investigated with more granularity in the presence of a natural language description of
CHAPTER 3. EVALUATION RELIABILITY AND BENCHMARK SATURATION                                    46

their thought process during annotation. In doing so, we measure query intent as a factor in
agreement.
    To ablate this factor, we fix narratives after the curation of judgments to assess how
narrative interpretation can affect agreement and downstream measures of effectiveness. We
take three topics of low, median, and high agreement measured via Fleiss κ between the
primary and secondary annotators; half of the annotators reference one narrative, and half
reference the other. We can then further isolate sources of disagreement to query intent and
intrinsic ambiguity of a query title, both qualitatively and quantitatively.

Annotators as Oracles.

User models underlie many common IR measures such as discounted cumulative gain [Järvelin
and Kekäläinen, 2002] and mean reciprocal rank (@k) [Chapelle et al., 2009]. In performing
offline evaluations of search systems using these measures, we aim to model effectiveness via
user behaviour approximated by relevance judgments. An optimal system would approach
a score of 1 on a normalised metric when k relevant documents exist. However, given the
subjective and variable nature of relevance under different query intents, such effectiveness,
though possible, may not be indicative of a truly effective system. Human annotators outside
the original annotation setting may represent strong search systems as they have exhaustively
annotated available documents to some cutoff k. As such, we measure the performance of
each annotator under original relevance judgments as an indicator of how non-pooled modern
systems are performing relative to a more realistic oracle. As in ideal discounted cumulative
gain, judgments are sorted by relevance grade after aggregation. Though graded relevance
lacks granularity in contrast to scalar relevance scores, through in-grade permutations (shuf-
fling), we validate that variations in downstream measures of effectiveness are minimal (SD
< 0.0001).


3.4     Evaluation
This section establishes the empirical findings of the re-annotation: the level of inter-annotator
agreement observed under graded relevance, the stability of system orderings under judg-
ment variation, and the standing of automatic systems relative to human annotators treated
as oracle runs.


3.4.1      Experiment Design
Following the re-annotation process of Voorhees [2000], we observe the impact of re-annotation
on the DL’19 test collection, using combinations to assess modern system bias.

Dataset.

The MSMARCO passage corpus is a collection of around 8.8 million segmented documents
covering a broad range of ad-hoc search topics [Nguyen et al., 2016]; this collection and
CHAPTER 3. EVALUATION RELIABILITY AND BENCHMARK SATURATION                                   47

Table 3.2: Modern systems included in our analysis. Rightmost columns are the source of
labels for semi-supervised learning; neural is any encoder-type model, and LLM is a Large
Language Model. Absence of both indicates binary labels.

                                                                                  Distillation
Model                                      Type                                   Neural LLM
ColBERT [Khattab and Zaharia, 2020]        Late interaction bi-encoder             ×        ×

monoT5 [Nogueira et al., 2020]             Seq2Seq cross-encoder                   ×        ×
MonoELECTRA [Pradeep et al., 2022,         BERT-based cross-encoder                ✓        ✓

RankGPT [Sun et al., 2023]                 Decoder-only list-wise cross-encoder    ?        ?


accompanying training topics were mined and annotated from Bing query logs. We re-
annotate the TREC Deep Learning 2019 test collection [Craswell et al., 2019], composed of
43 topics. Unlike the development split released originally with MSMARCO, the DL’19 test
set uses densely annotated depth-pooled topics taken from the associated TREC track that
year.

Pool Description.

The DL’19 track primarily focused on the comparison of neural systems in ad-hoc ranking.
For equivalence to the re-ranking task, the top 100 texts of all submitted systems from both
re-ranking and retrieval tasks were included in our annotation pool. The top 10 texts from
each of the 75 systems (44% Neural LM, 27% Neural, 29% lexical/traditional) were added
to our pool, and HiCal [Abualsaud et al., 2018] is used to collect an additional 100 texts per
topic. Our approach does not annotate all texts and thus uses a subset of this pool.

Models.

We compare several neural architectures and training settings. In addition to models that
contributed to the original annotation pool, we include systems that use other models for
either data augmentation or semi-supervised training via distillation. Table 3.2 illustrates
the degree of teacher distillation used in each architecture; ColBERT and SPLADE act as
end-to-end retrieval or first-stage retrievers, whereas all others re-rank BM25 [Robertson
et al., 1995] or ColBERT as noted. For monoT5, monoELECTRA, and SetEncoder, we
include multiple model sizes, base and large for BERT/ELECTRA variants and base and 3B
for T5. For monoELECTRA, we do not use the original checkpoints released by Pradeep
et al. [2022]; we instead use distilled checkpoints released by Schlatt et al. [2025] as we are
interested in systems that use modern processes. When using RankGPT, we employ GPT-4,
GPT-4 Turbo, and GPT-4o.
CHAPTER 3. EVALUATION RELIABILITY AND BENCHMARK SATURATION                                   48

Measures.

Voorhees [2000] measure inter-annotator agreement in a pairwise setting using the overlap of
judgments defined as the number of common judgments divided by the total number of judg-
ments. We additionally use Cohen’s κ over both 4-grade and binarised relevance and Fleiss’
κ, allowing for comparisons between 3 annotators (1 primary, 2 secondary) simultaneously.
Voorhees [2000] reported MAP because TREC-4 and TREC-6 used binary relevance with
MAP as the primary measure; the TREC Deep Learning tracks use four-grade relevance
and nDCG@10, so this study reports nDCG@10. Aligned with Voorhees [2000], we mea-
sure downstream system order correlation between judgment sets using measure Kendall’s
τ; additionally, we measure Spearman’s ρ and RBO.

System Order Robustness.

As we have multiple annotators per query, we can observe pooled judgments across several
hypothetical sets; we take all possible combinations of annotators and evaluate both pooled
and new models to observe the stability of system order under new judgments. For a given
judgment set, we produce a ranking of systems by a target metric (i.e., nDCG@10) and com-
pare this system order to the system ordering under the original judgments. We aggregate
rank ∆ for all possible natural combinations over each system to observe how frequently a
system degrades over other assessed systems. We measure significant differences in retrieval
effectiveness via a paired t-test with Bonferroni correction for different annotator combina-
tions. We measure significant changes in system rank by a Wilcoxon signed test comparing
annotator combinations with the system order by official TREC judgments.


3.4.2    Pilot Study Findings
We conducted a pilot study over the top 10 pooled texts for 10 randomly sampled topics.
Table 3.3 reports the grade distribution observed in the pilot. We observe that the prevalence
of grades 1 and 3 is inflated in these annotations compared to the original annotations. In
discussions with annotators, there was ambiguity about what separates a relevant text from a
highly relevant text. Additionally, cultural context leads to ambiguity in interpreting a query;
for example, a query “dog day afternoon meaning” was difficult to interpret without broader
context, as a film exists with that name. The query could be interpreted as the meaning of the
phrase or the film. From these ambiguities, we allowed annotators to use a controlled search
engine to familiarise themselves with the topic. Annotators not from the USA generally
noted the difficulty in USA-centric topics; nationality-conditioned bias and query difficulty
fall outside the scope of this chapter’s hypotheses and are not pursued here.


3.4.3    Core Assessment of Agreement
Quantification of relevance is confounded by many factors with several works reporting what
is generally considered low agreement among annotators [Lesk and Salton, 1968, Burgin,
1992, Voorhees, 2000]. Voorhees [2000] report overlap among annotators as a measure of
agreement, observing values around 0.4. In Table 3.4, we present agreement measurements

over both 4-grade and binarised relevance. We observe that under 4-grade relevance, all
agreement values degrade relative to those stated in previous studies. Fleiss’ κ measured
over primary and secondary annotators indicates near-random agreement but generally im-
proves by around 10 points in all cases over binary judgments. Overlap values over binary
judgments generally correspond to those observed by Voorhees [2000], and over solely sec-
ondary annotators, we observe high overlap values, suggesting that while disagreement stems
from the subjectivity of relevance and the annotation setting, the process of annotating over
4 grades is generally more variable regardless of topic granularity. In the third group, we
find the lowest values in both 4 grade and binary relevance, which is again reflected in low
overlap and Cohen’s κ values, potentially suggesting diverging query intents or that this al-
location of queries was particularly ambiguous. However, we see increases in overlap values
when measured solely over secondary annotators, again suggesting that the annotation set-
ting under which judgments are collected contributes to the improved agreement, as noted
by Cuadra and Katter [1967] and Regazzi [1988] in previous annotation studies. Values
of κ are low in all cases, suggesting that generally quantifying relevance over 4 grades is
more challenging for annotators than the re-annotation setting of Voorhees [2000]. The lack
of pre-defined narratives clarifying intent means annotators could interpret a query as they
please, potentially resulting in three diverging topic interpretations.
    In Figure 3.1, observe a Sankey flow diagram between the original relevance grade as-
signed by primary annotators and the new grades by secondary annotators. Like previous
work, a secondary annotation setting often leads to the reduction of relevance grades, e.g., 1
CHAPTER 3. EVALUATION RELIABILITY AND BENCHMARK SATURATION                                 50




    Figure 3.1: Transitions in relevance grades from primary to secondary annotations.

to 0 or 2 to 1. Validating our decision to sample a subset of non-relevant judgments, 13% of
800 non-relevant texts change grade, with 77% being a change to a grade of 1; we analyse
outlier cases (providing a grade of 2 or more) in Section 3.4.4. Measuring frequencies ag-
nostic of previous judgments, grades 1 and 2 are annotated similarly across annotators, while
grades 0 and 3 were annotated with greater variance. This variance in the case of grade 0 is
generally consistent across annotators, and we provide a deeper analysis of grade 3 due to
varying notions of relevance below.


3.4.4    Narratives, Intent, and Relevance
The observations of grade transitions in our work and generally low agreement noted by
prior work, even in a binary setting, may be attributed to several factors noted in Section
3.1. Though we cannot fully isolate factors in the subjectivity of relevance, we choose to
investigate the effect of narratives on inter-annotator agreement, given that recent corpora
often do not have fixed narratives [Craswell et al., 2019, 2020], unlike those used in prior
re-annotation studies [Harman, 1995]. We select topics by median, minimum, and max
Fleiss’ κ values from the annotation pool and consider where disagreement occurs and how
narratives differ. Chosen examples illustrate different aspects of agreement, differing query
intents, and a narrative’s specificity.
     The first, taken from Group 1, is a technical query in the medical domain with a Fleiss κ
of 0.06, indicating disagreement near random guessing. The query “types of dysarthria from
cerebral palsy” was described by one annotator as a query from a sibling of someone with
cerebral palsy looking to understand how their sibling would be affected by their condition
in the future. The other annotator described a medical context in which the definition of
all conditions is known, and therefore, solely the connection between dysarthria and cerebral
CHAPTER 3. EVALUATION RELIABILITY AND BENCHMARK SATURATION                                        51

palsy was required. The second annotator stated a requirement that relevance was determined
by the types and specificity of provided types of dysarthria connected to cerebral palsy,
with all other documents being non-relevant. Though both are valid information needs, they
represent vastly different query intents, with one annotator’s grade frequency matching the
overall annotation pool and the other stricter narrative leading to near-total non-relevance.
     The second, taken from Group 4, is a more general query, “is cdg airport in main paris”
with a Fleiss κ of 0.08, again indicating low agreement. However, both annotators described
a need for exact distances from the airport to either the centre of Paris or, more generally,
the city of Paris. Both annotators required a succinct description of the distance or travel
times to the airport from Paris to be considered perfectly relevant. To be highly relevant,
both considered any mention of either travel time or distance measurements. Intents then
diverged for partially relevant documents, with one annotator allowing for the mention of
costs and other aspects of travel to and from the airport. In contrast, the other annotator re-
quired the airport’s position, at minimum, mentioning that it is situated with respect to Paris.
Non-relevance was denoted by any other mention of the airport or mentions of distances to
destinations near Paris. We find larger differences in lower relevance grades, with one an-
notator considering the majority of pooled documents to be non-relevant when there is no
mention of travel details. In contrast, the other considers them to be relevant because they
mention that the airport is situated near Paris in a generic manner. The specificity of a query
generally leads to lower agreement in our observations. While queries in domains such as
law or medicine have a lower overall agreement, we still find cases where generic queries
have low agreement due to the interpretations of information needs.
     We analyse outlier cases of texts initially labelled non-relevant that, when evaluated un-
der an alternative query intent, were deemed perfectly relevant. In the 9 cases of this, there
are cases where a reference is made to the entity of focus, for example “goldfish” in the
query, “do goldfish grow” but only implicitly, for example “it” or “they”. The query was sat-
isfied by a given document if the context is inferred by the surrounding spans of text or from
knowing that the document has been retrieved by some system (this is inevitable under pool-
ing), but the information is not self-contained. In some cases, annotators explicitly stated
that they considered a document relevant, but when the information is not self-contained, it
cannot be annotated as relevant; others took this approach without note.
     Overall, many annotation disagreements can occur not only in the high-level query intent
of the narrative but also in descriptions of relevance, suggesting that diagnostic tooling and
query-specific grade specifications would tighten future re-annotation passes on this collec-
tion.


3.4.5    Collecting Narratives for Ambiguous Queries
Given the lack of public narratives provided in the release of TREC Deep Learning 2019, it
is infeasible to isolate query intent as a factor in reliability without first collecting narratives.
In fixing narratives, we produce an additional set of relevance judgments for queries of low,
