median, and high agreement by Fleiss κ, including the primary annotator. In Figure 3.2, We
classify these queries as difficult, median and easy with respect to query intent determination
and measure Cohen’s κ between the judgments of both narrative groupings. It is clear that
a fixed narrative reduces ambiguity with Cohen’s κ, improving by 24% and 46% for each
annotation group between the difficult and median query; however, between the median and
easy query, a decrease of 8% and an increase of 19% is observed.
    In several cases, high agreement across different narrative groups occurs; in each case,
narratives are of similar detail, defining what intent the user had in searching as well as
what should be labelled relevant; we consider that one factor in relevance annotation beyond
random noise may therefore be interpretation of a narrative which in the case of a primary
annotator is of minimal concern but in human-in-the-loop annotation with generative sys-
tems may lead to implications for control as ultimately we can only express our need or a
demonstration to the system in natural language. When computing global improvements in
agreement, we measure a 79% increase in agreement between the difficult and median query
and an additional 1% increase comparing the median and easy query. From these obser-
vations, we propose that the greatest factor in disagreement is simply the ambiguity of the
natural language used to express a query regardless of the narrative applied. Whether or not
this is only inherent in pooled corpora would require further investigation as in a setting such
as that of TREC DL’19, the ad-hoc realisation of a query intent will be biased by the top-k
texts chosen by pooled systems.


3.4.6         Core Assessment of System Order
The core objective in re-annotation studies is to validate that downstream evaluation is not
compromised by human bias in subjective relevance judgments. By measuring the stability
of system order and the correlation of system order by target metrics, we can assess col-
lection reliability. Following Voorhees [2000] and as described in Section 3.3.1, we sample
combinations of relevance judgments and observe the stability of pairwise system compar-
isons in Figure 3.3. Comparisons closest to the top right of the figure represent those where

Figure 3.4: Variance in nDCG@10 over annotator combinations. Effectiveness values on
new annotations are translucent. Colour of points indicates model type.



In Figure 3.5, we combine all possible combinations of annotators across annotation pairs.
We measure changes in system order under these combinations and aggregate by system.
Many lexical systems remain stable in ordering (∆ ≈ 0). Older neural approaches in the
original system pool display higher variance but can improve or degrade in rank without
a significant difference. When considering only modern systems, we observe that smaller
models trained with supervised and semi-supervised approaches consistently improve system
rank. Within this group, however, larger semi-supervised models are more robust than their
smaller counterparts under new judgments. List-wise encoders generally degrade in rank.
These models use an interaction token to facilitate inter-document attention, assisting in out-
of-domain effectiveness [Schlatt et al., 2024]. Changes in effectiveness are more pronounced
and are significant in smaller distilled language models. This is notable as though we see
continuing improvement in smaller models out-of-domain through distillation. One should
be cautious that in-domain effectiveness may be less robust than benchmarks indicate, which
for many applications may be more important than robust effectiveness across heterogeneous
corpora.
    Runs that are either zero-shot or distilled from LLM re-rankers show the highest vari-
ance in relative effectiveness even within a sub-group such as those based on GPT-4 end-
points. GPT-4o-based runs significantly degrade in system rank when re-ranking BM25
but are more stable under a neural first-stage ColBERT. This is most likely caused by posi-
tional bias found in list-wise ranking towards already precise first-stage rankings [Tang et al.,
2024, Parry et al., 2024b]. This hypothesis is validated by the frequent rank improvement of
RankZephyr (the left-most model in Figure 3.4), which was trained explicitly to reduce this
bias [Pradeep et al., 2023]. Overall, we consider that though high-quality data (i.e., granular
training data from strong models) is important in improving the robustness of neural models,
these findings would suggest that smaller models in our current training strategies may not
have the capacity to robustly generalise even in-domain.

3.4.7                  Systems versus Humans
Recall our argument that a “perfect” score on a normalised metric may not be a feasible
or desirable goal in ranking evaluation; as such, we measure the effectiveness of human
annotators aggregated in multiple forms outlined in Section 3.3.3. In Table 3.6, see that
systems can exceed or reach parity with secondary annotators, particularly with respect to
nDCG@10 and MRR@10, with multiple systems, both LLM and encoder-based, exceed-
ing annotators. When excluding unjudged documents (which is known to overestimate the
retrieval effectiveness [Fröbe et al., 2023, Sakai, 2008]), annotator performance measured
by precision can be exceeded by neural systems across nDCG, P, and MRR; however, recall
remains lower in all settings. Given our findings in Figure 3.4, an observation that systems
are better than humans should be considered cautiously. However, the robustness of the
LLM-based RankZephyr observed both by system order robustness over new judgments and
its parity with human annotators suggests that with sufficient capacity, a smaller system can
be distilled from larger systems while remaining robust. Observing similar effectiveness to
humans and confidence intervals providing no way to discriminate systems, we consider that
achieving significant improvements would suggest far exceeding humans, which may not be
possible in a way conducive to improving information access and may solely serve to im-
prove precision on specific topics and intents. The ability of distilled systems to reach parity
with human effectiveness across multiple annotator combinations while obtaining largely
reduced recall in a judged setting, even when re-ranking a neural first-stage retriever, is un-
desirable for several downstream tasks. Although precision is approaching or overtaking the
effectiveness of an oracle, recall remains a challenge in densely annotated collections.

3.5     Conclusions
The re-annotation study reveals that inter-annotator agreement on TREC DL’19 is substan-
tially lower than prior re-annotation studies observed on older collections. Under four-grade
relevance, Fleiss’ κ values between primary and secondary annotators approach random
agreement; under binarisation, values remain below those reported by Voorhees [2000] on
newswire collections with full narratives. This degradation follows from graded relevance
scales, shorter documents, and the absence of narratives constraining annotator interpreta-
tion, but its implications have not been absorbed by the community.
     System orderings are broadly stable, replicating the classical finding that Cranfield-style
evaluation is robust to annotator disagreement. However, this robustness has limits that
are concentrated where they matter most. Systems at the current Pareto frontier of neu-
ral ranking, those based on or distilled from large language models, exhibit the highest
variance in relative effectiveness under judgment perturbation. The Pareto frontier of the
swap-probability analysis is populated by LLM-neural comparisons: system pairs where
large nDCG differences coincide with high probabilities of rank reversal under alternative
judgments. Closed-source LLM-based re-rankers show the highest variance even within
subgroups, and smaller distilled models, though improved by semi-supervised training, gen-
eralise poorly even in-domain.
     When human annotators are treated as oracle systems, state-of-the-art re-rankers reach or
approach parity with annotator performance across nDCG, Precision, and MRR, while recall
remains substantially lower. This suggests that the collection may be approaching saturation:
further gains may reflect alignment with the particular annotator opinions encoded in the
judgments rather than improvements that generalise to other assessors or collections.
CHAPTER 3. EVALUATION RELIABILITY AND BENCHMARK SATURATION                                     57

Verdict on H1.1. Recall that H1.1 held that inter-annotator agreement under graded rel-
evance is insufficient to reliably discriminate between closely performing neural rankers,
limiting the practical value of the marginal gains claimed for increasingly expensive models.
H1.1 is supported, with one caveat. Under four-grade relevance on TREC DL’19, inter-
annotator agreement approaches chance, and the system pairs whose orderings are most
sensitive to judgment variation are precisely those at the neural Pareto frontier, where LLM-
based and LLM-distilled re-rankers are separated by small nDCG margins. For these com-
parisons, the marginal gains reported for increasingly expensive models fall within the range
of annotation variability. The caveat is one of scope: large-effect comparisons (neural versus
lexical) remain stable under re-annotation, so the claim concerns fine-margin discrimination
on saturating graded collections, not benchmark evaluation in general.

Epistemic implications for this thesis. The findings of this chapter constrain the eviden-
tial weight of benchmark-derived comparisons throughout the thesis. We distinguish three
levels of conclusion that TREC DL-style evaluation can support under the annotation vari-
ability documented here. Large-effect conclusions, such as the effectiveness of the document
prior in Chapter 5, survive annotation noise: the effect sizes are large relative to the variabil-
ity observed under re-annotation, and orderings at this granularity are stable. Equivalence
conclusions, such as the equivalence of BM25-sampled and expensively mined negatives in
Chapter 4, are strengthened by annotation variability: if the benchmark cannot discriminate
the two, the expensive option has no measurable advantage. Fine-margin conclusions, such
as comparisons between closely spaced neural re-rankers, are directional trends rather than
definitive rankings. We are explicit throughout about which level each result supports.

Implications for separability. The structural advantage of modular systems under evalu-
ation uncertainty is that components can be evaluated independently. If a monolithic system
achieves ∆nDCG = 0.02 over a baseline, the re-annotation findings show this gain may not be
robust to annotator variation. If a modular system achieves the same gain but its components
can be ablated individually, the attribution of that gain is more informative and more testable
than the aggregate comparison alone. This does not resolve the measurement problem, but it
reduces its impact: separable systems provide more evaluation surface than monolithic ones.
    These findings bear directly on how training pipelines are designed and justified, a ques-
tion taken up in the next chapter.
Chapter 4

Revisiting Term-Weighting as a Data
Source

   Hypothesis H1.2 (tested in this chapter)
   BM25-sampled negatives provide sufficient supervision for effective ranking distil-
   lation without the multi-stage mining cascades that dominate current practice; under
   minimally sufficient locality and teacher effectiveness, neural model-based data aug-
   mentation does not improve effectiveness beyond this baseline.

    Chapter 3 established that the evaluation methodology used to benchmark neural rank-
ing models is less reliable than commonly assumed. Under graded relevance, inter-annotator
agreement on TREC Deep Learning 2019, the most widely used passage ranking benchmark,
falls close to chance: Fleiss’ κ between primary and secondary annotators approaches zero
under the four-grade scale. The most effective re-rankers, namely LLM-based and LLM-
distilled systems, are precisely those whose relative orderings are most sensitive to annotator
variation: under re-annotation, the probability of a rank swap between adjacently ranked
LLM-based systems approaches one half, while it remains far lower for comparisons in-
volving lexical or non-LLM neural systems. State-of-the-art re-rankers have reached parity
with human annotator performance on TREC DL’19, indicating that this collection may be
saturated.
    These findings raise a question about the training pipelines that produce these systems.
If the evaluation methodology cannot reliably discriminate between a system trained with
BM25-sampled negatives and a system trained with an elaborate multi-stage negative min-
ing cascade, then the marginal effectiveness gain attributed to the more expensive pipeline
cannot be reliably measured under current benchmarks. The gain may be genuine but small,
or it may be an artefact of the judgment variability and benchmark saturation documented in
Chapter 3; the thesis does not claim to settle which, only that the expense is not justified by
the available evidence. The field has invested considerable effort in progressively more com-
plex training regimes on the assumption that measured improvements on static benchmarks
reflect genuine advances in ranking quality. The preceding chapter calls that assumption into
question by showing that, at the fine margins separating these systems, agreement between

                                              58
CHAPTER 4. REVISITING TERM-WEIGHTING AS A DATA SOURCE                                       59

annotators approaches chance and the strongest re-rankers reach parity with the collection’s
own assessors.
    This chapter tests a single claim: that ranking-distillation generalisation is governed by
quantities the elaborate mining pipelines do not change. If the claim holds, the pipelines are
not paying their cost. The claim bears directly on the separability thesis of this dissertation
(§1.4), which holds that query-dependent and query-independent evidence can be identi-
fied, estimated, and composed as distinct components. State-of-the-art training pipelines are
tightly coupled by construction: each stage depends on the output of the previous stage, and
the contribution of any single component cannot be isolated without retraining the entire
cascade. The alternative investigated here is separable supervision: using a classical term-
weighting retriever, BM25, as the sole source of negative examples, decoupled from any
intermediate neural model. If separable supervision produces models of equivalent effec-
tiveness, the computational and engineering cost of tightly coupled pipelines is not justified.
    We provide a theoretical analysis grounded in density-ratio adjustment and PAC-style
generalisation bounds, showing that the factors governing ranking distillation effectiveness
are the teacher’s pairwise entropy and the essential diameter of the query manifold, not the
expense of the negative mining process. We validate this analysis empirically, training both
bi-encoders and cross-encoders under systematically varied supervision conditions, and find
that BM25-sampled negatives, the cheapest and simplest option, produce models whose in-
domain and out-of-domain effectiveness is statistically equivalent to those trained on expen-
sively mined data under semi-supervised criteria.
    In terms of the decomposition s(q, d) = fθ (q, d) + gφ (d), this chapter concerns the cost
of producing the dynamic component fθ : it asks what supervision is actually required to train
it well, rather than how to compose it with a separable prior. Section 4.1 situates the chapter
against negative-mining, distillation, and reproducibility practice. Section 4.3 presents the
density-ratio and generalisation analysis, the empirical ablation over supervision conditions
for both bi-encoders and cross-encoders, and closes with the verdict on H1.2 and its scope.


4.1     Background: Negative Mining and Distillation

4.1.1    Negative Mining: From BM25 to Multi-Stage Cascades
The training of state-of-the-art neural ranking models has become increasingly involved.
Early work fine-tuned pre-trained language models on MSMARCO with BM25-sampled
negatives and binary cross-entropy, achieving strong effectiveness with a simple pipeline
[Nogueira and Cho, 2019]. Subsequent work introduced hard-negative mining from the
model’s own retrieval [Xiong et al., 2021], distillation from cross-encoder teachers [Hof-
stätter et al., 2020], and iterative cycles of mine-retrain-mine that progressively tighten the
negative distribution [Zhan et al., 2021]. A widely used end point of this trajectory is the
SentenceTransformers cascade [Reimers and Gurevych, 2019], in which twelve cross-trained
models produce candidates that are filtered by a classifier before a single student is trained.
CHAPTER 4. REVISITING TERM-WEIGHTING AS A DATA SOURCE                                             60




    (a) BM25 negatives (b) CE-filtered negatives (c) Iteratively mined   (d) Ensemble negatives
       Relevant   Non-relevant Decision boundary

Figure 4.1: Conceptual view of the negative distribution under successive mining stages.
Relevant documents (green) occupy a shared region; non-relevant documents (red) are drawn
progressively closer to the class boundary as mining tightens. (a) BM25 negatives are spread
widely and mostly far from the boundary, carrying little discriminative signal. (b) Filtering
candidates through a cross-encoder concentrates the negatives into a tighter ring nearer the
boundary. (c) Iterative mining pulls them onto the boundary itself, and (d) the full ensem-
ble collapses them almost entirely onto it. Across (a) to (d) the empirical class boundary
becomes sharper while its support shrinks; this chapter tests whether that sharpening is nec-
essary once supervision comes from a teacher rather than from binary labels.


    Figure 4.1 makes this trade-off concrete. Reading the panels from (a) to (d), each suc-
cessive mining stage draws the negatives closer to the class boundary: BM25 negatives are
diffuse and largely uninformative, cross-encoder filtering concentrates them, and iterative
and ensemble mining collapse them onto the boundary itself. Each stage therefore buys
sharper supervision at the price of coverage, since the negatives it retains describe an ever-
smaller region of the input space. The question this chapter asks is whether that trade is
worth making once the supervision signal is a teacher’s graded score rather than a binary la-
bel: if a graded teacher already communicates how negative each candidate is, the additional
sharpening bought by expensive mining may be redundant.
    The historical trajectory illustrates a steady growth in cost: from BM25 negatives (cheap,
deterministic) to single-model neural negatives (moderate cost) to cross-encoder-filtered neg-
atives (expensive) to ensemble negatives (very expensive) to multi-stage cascades (extremely
expensive). At each step, the assumption is that harder negatives, drawn from progressively
tighter distributions around each query, yield better models. This chapter tests that assump-
tion directly.
    A parallel line of work refines which negatives are selected rather than how many stages
produce them. Because contrastive objectives become more discriminating as candidates
grow more similar, Gao et al. [2021] showed that sampling negatives from more precise
rankings, and sampling more of them, yields further effectiveness gains. Active-learning
approaches instead select the negatives a model finds most challenging, though they require
a substantial pool of annotated queries to be effective [Althammer et al., 2023]. A recurring
difficulty is that as the source of negatives grows stronger, label sparsity makes it increasingly
likely that a sampled negative is in fact relevant; this motivated de-noised negative selection
[Qu et al., 2021], which has itself grown increasingly elaborate [Reimers and Gurevych,
2019].
CHAPTER 4. REVISITING TERM-WEIGHTING AS A DATA SOURCE                                        61

4.1.2    Distillation: Labelling Strategy versus Selection Strategy
Knowledge distillation transfers task effectiveness from one model to another, replacing hard
labels with the soft targets produced by a teacher [Bucila et al., 2006, Ba and Caruana,
2014, Hinton, 2015]. In ranking, distillation has served two roles: as a weak-supervision
signal for bootstrapping early pre-trained language model rankers, and as a means of training
lightweight rankers that approach the effectiveness of far larger teachers [Lin et al., 2020,
Hofstätter et al., 2020, Qu et al., 2021, Xiao et al., 2022, Pradeep et al., 2022].
    A distinction that matters for the analysis in this chapter is between distillation as a la-
belling strategy and distillation as a selection strategy. As a labelling strategy, distillation
replaces binary relevance labels with the teacher’s soft targets, changing what the student
is trained to predict. As a selection strategy, the teacher is used to choose which candi-
dates enter the training set at all, changing which examples the student sees. Contemporary
pipelines conflate the two, using a single teacher both to score and to select. This is undesir-
able because it confounds attribution: when such a pipeline reports a gain, that gain cannot
be assigned to the labelling signal or to the selection of which candidates the student sees, so
neither mechanism can be credited or ruled out. The generalisation analysis in Section 4.2
separates them.
    Early accounts of why distillation works were largely qualitative [Ba and Caruana, 2014,
Hinton, 2015]. More recent work has sought principled explanations: the effectiveness of a
teacher-student pair has been related to intrinsic task difficulty [Ji and Zhu, 2020] and to the
degree to which the student follows the teacher’s distribution [Nagarajan and Kolter, 2019],
with evidence that exactly matching the teacher’s densities is not necessary for generalisa-
tion, consistent with ranking methods that ignore the precise densities of teacher rankings
[Pradeep et al., 2023, Schlatt et al., 2025]. The most directly comparable prior result is that
of Hsu et al. [2021], who derive a uniform-convergence generalisation bound for a distilled
predictor: with probability at least 1 − δ , the student’s population risk exceeds its empiri-
               p                         
cal risk by O (C(H) + log(1/δ ))/n , where C(H) is a capacity term for the student class
H and n the number of distillation examples. Their analysis is teacher-centric: distillation
serves as a lens to bound the risk of the teacher’s own predictor. The bound developed in
Section 4.2 (Theorem 4.2.1) is instead student-centric and miner-aware: it bounds the stu-
dent’s excess pairwise risk under a possibly biased mining policy and isolates the geometry
of the query manifold and the mining bias as separate terms that the teacher-centric setting
does not expose.


4.1.3    Efficiency and Reproducibility Concerns
The growing investment in negative-mining and distillation pipelines carries costs that are
independent of any effectiveness gain. The cascades described in Section 4.1.1 can involve
a dozen or more cross-trained models together with classifier filters, and each additional
model in the cascade must be trained and queried at scale, inflating computational cost and
the associated CO2 footprint [Strubell et al., 2019, Scells et al., 2022].
CHAPTER 4. REVISITING TERM-WEIGHTING AS A DATA SOURCE                                         62

    These pipelines also hinder reproducibility. The precise sequence of checkpoints, fil-
tering thresholds, and sampling decisions that produces a state-of-the-art model is difficult
to replicate, because these decisions are numerous, interdependent, and rarely reported in
full: critical settings such as the number of mining iterations, the filtering threshold, and the
teacher checkpoints are frequently recoverable only from released code rather than the paper
text, when they are recoverable at all [Lin, 2022, Wang et al., 2022b]. When a system that
combines expensive negative mining, multi-teacher distillation, and iterative hard-negative
selection reports a new result, the marginal contribution of each component is confounded
with the others. This chapter asks whether that expense is warranted, that is, whether the fac-
tors that govern ranking distillation effectiveness are the factors these pipelines are designed
to improve.


4.2     Term-Weighting as an Efficient Data Source
The trajectory of growing mining cost described in Section 4.1.1 rests on an intuition about
hardness: negatives drawn from progressively tighter distributions around each query present
a harder classification task and therefore yield better rankings. The intuition is that when
candidates cluster tightly around the query, the model must separate documents that are
nearly identical in representation space, including relevant and non-relevant documents on
the same topic, rather than discarding obviously unrelated text; the decision boundary it must
learn is correspondingly finer. This intuition is seldom stated as a falsifiable claim. Modern
distillation compounds the difficulty, since it operates over explicit teacher rankings rather
than positive/negative pairs, so the notion of a negative, and with it the notion of negative
hardness, is no longer well defined. The interaction between negative mining and distillation
is nonetheless applied in many systems with little or no ablation [Xiao et al., 2022, Ren et al.,
2021, Song et al., 2023, Wang et al., 2024]. This section makes the assumption precise:
it develops a generalisation analysis of ranking distillation that isolates the quantities on
which effectiveness depends, and then tests empirically whether expensive mining pipelines
improve them.
    To see why hardness loses its meaning under distillation, consider a teacher that assigns
five candidate documents the soft scores g = (0.91, 0.87, 0.83, 0.40, 0.10). Under a pairwise
objective such as MarginMSE the student is trained to reproduce the score differences gi − g j
rather than a binary label: the third document is a near-tie with the top two yet sits a wide
margin above the fourth. “Negative” is therefore not a property a document has or lacks, but
a position in a continuous ordering; the same document is a hard negative relative to the top
of the ranking and an easy one relative to its tail.
    In information retrieval, hard negatives are typically sampled from candidate sets scored
by one or more initial models [Karpukhin et al., 2020, Gao et al., 2021] as indicated in Figure
4.2. In contrastive objectives, increasing the locality of (reducing the distance between) the
candidate set creates a more challenging classification task [Gutmann and Hyvärinen, 2010,
Ceylan and Gutmann, 2018], often improving downstream effectiveness. Distillation, in turn,
CHAPTER 4. REVISITING TERM-WEIGHTING AS A DATA SOURCE                                                  63




                        BM25 TAS-B       v3   v3   v2       v1      v1        v1   v1   v1   v1   v2


                            Supervised


                            Unsupervised                Hard Negative Miner




                                                            Your Model


Figure 4.2: The negative-mining pipeline used in the widely adopted SentenceTransformers
configuration [Reimers and Gurevych, 2019]. Each row is a successive mining stage, read top
to bottom in the order models are trained and applied; each column is a candidate retriever
or scorer, with TAS-B shown as the representative first-stage dense retriever that seeds the
candidate pool. Solid edges denote candidates passed forward to the next stage, and dotted
edges denote candidates removed by a cross-encoder filter. The figure makes one point:
producing “hard” negatives already requires training and running a cross-encoder, so the
supervision the pipeline is meant to provide cheaply is in fact a prerequisite for building it.


replaces binary labels with soft targets from a teacher [Bucila et al., 2006, Ba and Caruana,
2014, Hinton, 2015], allowing students to match or surpass larger estimators [Pradeep et al.,
2022, Xiao et al., 2022, Pradeep et al., 2023]. Contemporary systems couple the two: teach-
ers are employed both to score and to label documents, producing multistage cascades of
models that must be trained and queried at scale to collect training data. A common exam-
ple is the five-stage SentenceTransformers pipeline whose twelve cross-trained models are
further filtered by a classifier [Reimers and Gurevych, 2019], sometimes upon previous iter-
ations of each other. Such complexity is problematic. Each additional model inflates compu-
tational cost and CO2 footprint [Scells et al., 2022] and hinders reproducibility [Wang et al.,
2022b]. From a theoretical standpoint, negative-selection heuristics influence only which
instances are labelled, not the diameter ∆Q or entropy H(g) terms that govern generalisation
(see Section 4.2). Existing work on increasing the “hardness” of negatives often focuses
solely on the domain of a ranking [Karpukhin et al., 2020, Gao et al., 2021, Qu et al., 2021],
either using expensive ensembling approaches or iteratively choosing challenging domains
based on heuristics such as model uncertainty [Xiong et al., 2021, Zhan et al., 2021]. This
focus on reducing epistemic uncertainty may yield a highly confident model while neglect-
ing the irreducible aleatoric uncertainty inherent in relevance labels. The noise-contrastive
and distillation objectives referred to here are defined in the background (Chapter 2) and
formalised for the present analysis in Section 4.2. Distillation, by contrast, allows multi-
ple relevant documents and degrees of relevance to be considered within a single training
instance, reflecting real search scenarios.
    This analysis controls, both theoretically and empirically, for the heuristics governing
example locality and the entropy of target distributions. Intuitively, the bound depends on
how far apart the candidate documents for a query can lie (their geometric spread), on how
CHAPTER 4. REVISITING TERM-WEIGHTING AS A DATA SOURCE                                       64

decisively the teacher orders a typical pair of them (its confidence), and on how aggressively
the miner concentrates on a narrow slice of the candidate space. The generalisation bound
over ranking distillation developed in Section 4.2 makes this precise: its bias term depends on
three quantities: the diameter ∆Q of the query manifold, the teacher’s pairwise entropy H(g),
and the miner condition number κQ . The first two are unchanged by additional negative-
mining stages; the third penalises miners that concentrate aggressively on a narrow region of
the candidate space.
    Concretely, the analysis and ablation show that when distilling from a strong teacher,
extensive hard negative mining typically yields effectiveness at parity with simpler negative
mining from sources like BM25 [Robertson et al., 1995]. This is important because simpler
negative mining substantially reduces the cost and complexity of training ranking models.
    We now formalise the contribution of data augmentation to ranking model optimisation.
We provide a generalisation bound for ranking distillation that decomposes the excess risk
of a learned student into three independent levers: the resolution of the teacher, the geom-
etry of the query manifold, and the bias of the mining policy. Each lever corresponds to
a quantity we estimate empirically in Section 4.2.4, and each maps to a distinct interven-
tion a practitioner might consider. Our central claim, made precise below, is that traditional
negative-hardness reasoning conflates these levers and obscures which of them is actually
doing the work. A full notation table and all proofs are given in Section 4.4.1.


4.2.1    Preliminaries
The ranking problem and the cross-encoder / bi-encoder taxonomy are defined in §2.1 and
summarised in Table 2.1; this chapter follows the notation introduced there. As in §2.3.1,
a ranking model f : X → R scores query-document inputs X ≡ (Q, D) as an unnormalised
estimator of p(Q | D) [Robertson et al., 1995], with D denoting one or more texts depending
on the architecture. The analysis below focuses on the two architectures that admit distil-
lation from a teacher: cross-encoders [Nogueira et al., 2020] and bi-encoders [Karpukhin
et al., 2020].
    Training Ranking Models Let Q denote a set of training queries. For each Q ∈ Q
we observe a finite candidate list DQ commonly treated as pairs XQ = {(Q, Di ) : ∀Di ∈
                                                                                     |X |
DQ }. Each element of XQ can be assigned a binary relevance label YQ = {yi }1 Q , y ∈
{0, 1}. Commonly, solely relevant candidates are explicitly annotated (i.e., y ← 1), forming
a labelled set L. All other elements are sampled from a larger set of similar documents
selected by a heuristic, forming a negative set U that is assumed to be non-relevant. In
distillation settings, targets YQ are instead determined by an existing teacher model g such
that YQ = {g(Q, D), ∀(Q, D) ∈ XQ }, the logits yielded by g may in turn be transformed into
explicit labels depending on optimisation criteria.
    Choosing Pairs It is common to condition the sampling of negative examples on some
existing scorer [Karpukhin et al., 2020, Qu et al., 2021]. Formally, let µQ define a query-
specific reference measure over X under which we wish to evaluate a ranker, and let πQ
CHAPTER 4. REVISITING TERM-WEIGHTING AS A DATA SOURCE                                          65

denote a possibly biased mining policy from which training pairs are actually drawn. Recent
approaches use an ensemble of systems [Qu et al., 2021, Song et al., 2023] inducing πQ ,
which, in contrastive settings, can be further filtered by a model g to ensure that ρ(Xi , X j ) >
ε, ∀X j,i̸= j ∈ X . This assumes that Xi can be a labelled positive for the contrastive objective.
This step, given its expense and implied confidence in the discriminative ability of g, would
imply that one should learn an approximation of the teacher model g in a semi-supervised
setting as opposed to solely filtering a candidate distribution. Nevertheless, this approach of
ensembling and filtering is frequently employed, and thus we consider it for completeness.
     Problem Setting The subjectivity of relevance and the scale of modern web-scale cor-
pora make estimation of a ranking intrinsically subjective [Voorhees, 2000, Parry et al.,
2025a]: for most queries, we neither observe a complete ordering of candidates nor can
we assume perfect recall in finding relevant documents, thus there can be many relevant doc-
uments beyond the single-relevant document constraint of contrastive learning. We therefore
often work with a) a sparse set of human judgments and noisy negative examples, or b) a
teacher model g : X → R trained on auxiliary data. The objective of student f : X → R is to
rank elements from a structured space X equipped with metric ρ. Given a query Q, the goal
is to learn a scoring function that produces rankings aligned with a teacher model g.
     Recall that for each query Q ∈ Q we have candidate pairs XQ = {(Q, D1 ), . . . , (Q, DmQ )}
together with labels YQ ∈ {y0 , y1 , . . . , ymQ } ∪ {∅}. A student ranker f : X → R outputs
real-valued scores whose descending order defines the predicted ranking. Our loss criteria
contrast query-document pairs such that we look to minimise the pairwise risk of misordering
two pairs compared to their order under model g. Metric structure matters fundamentally
because our student learns in a setting where available training data represents a limited and
often biased sample from the true space of relevant elements around an anchor query. The
geometry constrains how knowledge can transfer between observed pairs.
     Our problem then lies in the contribution of data augmentation to the effectiveness of
ranking models. Distillation through criteria such as RankNet [Burges, 2010] blurs the
boundary between classical notions of contrastive learning and knowledge distillation. No
score values are used, and technically, contrastive objectives do the same, albeit with arbi-
trary negative ordering, both conditioned on some teacher (or negative miner) g. Negative
sampling and knowledge distillation can be seen as orthogonal: sampling provides suitable
observations given a downstream task and requires some labelling process for optimisation;
distillation provides the labelling process for optimisation, but does not provide an explicit
selection process for observations.


4.2.2    Three Quantities
Assuming student model f and teacher model g, we use the output of model g as targets Y .
Our analysis isolates three quantities, each of which has a clear interpretation in isolation
and each of which is estimable from training data. Together they will fully account for the
excess risk of a distilled student.
CHAPTER 4. REVISITING TERM-WEIGHTING AS A DATA SOURCE                                           66

    We define locality in terms of a query-specific measure µQ over our input space X mod-
elling the geometry of relevant elements conditioned on Q. Formally, let (X , ρ, µQ ) be a
metric-measure space with complete space X , ground metric ρ (we use cosine distance over
fixed latent representations), and measure µQ conditional on Q. The essential diameter of
this space is
                     ∆Q = ess sup ρ(x, x′ ) with respect to µQ ⊗ µQ .                  (1)
                              (x,x′ )∈X 2

∆Q measures the geometric spread of the query-conditional support: how far apart can two
points drawn from µQ be? It is the natural quantity to control how much score variation a
Lipschitz Bayes scorer can produce on the manifold the student must learn.
    We work with pairwise Bregman surrogates because they are prevalent in the empiri-
cal ranking literature (RankNet [Burges, 2010], MarginMSE [Hofstätter et al., 2020], KL-
divergence [Kullback and Leibler, 1951]) and admit clean theoretical analysis. Define the
pairwise risk of f as:

Definition 4.2.1 (Pairwise Risk). For a scorer f and query measure µQ , the pairwise risk is
the probability of misordering:

                                             [ f (x) < f (x′ )] = Eµ ⊗2 1{ f (x) < f (x′ )} .
                                                                                          
                RµQ ( f ) =       Pr                                                            (2)
                              (x,x′ )∼µQ⊗2                          Q



Definition 4.2.2 (Bregman Distillation Loss). For convex potential φ , student f , and teacher
g, the distillation loss on pair (x, x′ ) is

                       ℓ( f , g; x, x′ ) = Dφ f (x) − f (x′ ) g(x) − g(x′ ) ,
                                                                           
                                                                                                (3)

where Dφ (a ∥ b) = φ (a)−φ (b)−φ ′ (b)(a−b) is the Bregman divergence. Writing δx,x′ (h) :=
h(x) − h(x′ ) for the score difference under scorer h, this is equivalently

                                 ℓh (x, x′ ) = Dφ δx,x′ ( f ) ∥ δx,x′ (g) ;
                                                                         


we use whichever form is clearer in context.

     The semi-supervised losses used in our experiments are all pairwise Bregman surro-
gates. For RankNet, writing si j = f (xi ) − f (x j ) and pi j = σ (si j ) and taking the poten-
tial φ (u) = u log u + (1 − u) log(1 − u) on (0, 1), the divergence Dφ (a∥b) = a log ab + (1 −
        1−a
a) log 1−b   recovers the RankNet loss against the teacher-derived label yi j = 1{g(xi ) > g(x j )}
as Dφ (yi j ∥pi j ). For MarginMSE, taking φ (u) = u2 gives Dφ (a∥b) = (a − b)2 , and the loss
is Dφ f (x) − f (x′ ) g(x) − g(x′ ) directly. The link between Bregman divergences and
                                   

exponential-family conditional expectations is due to Banerjee et al. [2005] and explains
why both losses share the same surrogate framework.
     Hypothesising that higher-entropy ranking targets encode additional useful information,
similarly to the propositions of Hinton [2015], we model the entropy of a teacher ranking
under pairwise preferences:
CHAPTER 4. REVISITING TERM-WEIGHTING AS A DATA SOURCE                                          67

Definition 4.2.3 (Query Entropy). For teacher g and query measure µQ , let px,x′ = Pr[g(x) >
g(x′ )]. The query entropy is
                                                                           
        H(g) = E(x,x′ )∼µ ⊗2 −px,x′ log px,x′ − (1 − px,x′ ) log(1 − px,x′ ) ∈ [0, log 2].    (4)
                           Q


     H(g) is small when the teacher confidently picks an order on most pairs and large when
its pairwise verdicts are close to a coin flip. It is a property of the teacher and the evaluation
measure jointly. When H(g) is close to log 2 no student can do meaningfully better than
guessing on a typical pair; when H(g) is small the teacher provides a sharp signal that the
student can match. Following Painsky and Wornell [2020], we relate this entropy to teacher
misordering through Pinsker’s inequality, in the direction relevant to bounding the gap from
the teacher to Bayes:

Definition 4.2.4 (Misordering bound from entropy (Proof in §4.4.1)). For teacher g with
pairwise entropy H(g), the deviation of the teacher’s pairwise distribution from uniform is
bounded by                                     q
                             η H(g) := 2 − log 2−H(g)
                                           1
                                     
                                                      2    +
                                                             ,                          (5)

where (·)+ denotes the positive part. As H(g) → log 2 the teacher becomes uninformative
and η → 0; as H(g) → 0 the teacher is sharp and η → 12 .

    Training pairs are drawn from a possibly biased mining policy πQ with supp πQ ⊆ supp µQ ,
while risk is always evaluated under µQ . Define the importance ratio wQ = dµQ /dπQ and its
essential supremum, the miner condition number:

                                κQ = ess sup wQ (x) ∈ [1, ∞].                                 (6)
                                       x∈supp πQ


Unbiased mining (πQ = µQ ) gives κQ = 1. Aggressive miners that concentrate on a narrow
region of X inflate κQ , increasing the variance of the importance-weighted empirical risk
and slowing convergence.
    We control the capacity of the student through the empirical Rademacher complexity
Rn (L) of the surrogate class L = {ℓ(h, g; ·, ·) : h ∈ H} on n pairs, treated as a black box that
decays at the standard O(n−1/2 ) rate. This holds for linear classes with bounded norm, kernel
classes with bounded RKHS norm, and, under standard implicit-regularisation assumptions,
for the trained-network classes used in our experiments. Working through Rademacher com-
plexity rather than dimension counting lets the bound apply to neural rankers without an
artificial combinatorial proxy.


4.2.3    Generalisation under Data Augmentation
We now state our main result: a generalisation bound for ranking distillation whose bias term
decomposes additively into the three quantities defined above, plus a capacity term.
CHAPTER 4. REVISITING TERM-WEIGHTING AS A DATA SOURCE                                               68

Theorem 4.2.1 (Excess risk of distilled ranking under biased sampling (Proof in §4.4.3)).
Let (X , ρ, µQ ) be a metric-measure space for query Q, let H be a hypothesis class such
that the Bayes scorer f ⋆ = arg minh∈H R µQ (h) is L-Lipschitz with respect to ρ, and let fb
minimise the importance-weighted empirical Bregman loss with convex potential φ over n
pairs drawn i.i.d. from πQ⊗2 . Then for every confidence level δ ∈ (0, 1), with probability at
least 1 − δ ,
                                                                 q                       q
                                                                                                  )
RµQ ( fb)− RµQ ( f ⋆ ) ≤                                   + C κQ Rn (L) L ∆Q + C κQ L ∆Q log(1/δ
                                                                                 ′
                                            
                              ζφ L ∆Q η H(g)                                                  n     ,
                              |      {z      }               |     {z       } |         {z        }
                           teacher resolution × geometry      miner bias × capacity    confidence
                                                                                  (7)
                                                           √
where ζφ depends only on the divergence potential φ (ζφ = 2 for RankNet, ζφ = 2 for
MarginMSE), and C,C′ > 0 are absolute constants. In the unbiased case πQ = µQ we have
κQ = 1.

Corollary 4.2.1 (Unbiased mining). When πQ = µQ , we have κQ = 1 and the bound of
Theorem 4.2.1 reduces to
                                                q                     q
                                                                               )
   RµQ ( f ) − R µQ ( f ) ≤ ζφ L ∆Q η H(g) + 2ζφ Rn (L) L ∆Q + ζφ L ∆Q log(2/δ
                       ⋆

                                                                           n     .

Corollary 4.2.2 (Standard rate). If Rn (L) = O(n−1/2 ), the capacity and confidence terms of
Theorem 4.2.1 are O(n−1/4 ) and O(n−1/2 ) respectively, and the bound takes the schematic
form                                                                    
                RµQ ( fb) − R µQ ( f ⋆ ) ≤ ζφ L ∆Q η H(g) + O κQ n−1/4 .
                                                         


Reading the bound. The bound has three terms, each governed by one of the three quan-
tities. The teacher resolution term ζφ L∆Q η(H(g)) does not vanish with n: it is the floor
on excess risk imposed by teacher uncertainty, scaled by the largest score gap a Lipschitz
Bayes scorer can produce on the manifold. The capacity term scales the Rademacher com-
plexity by the miner condition number κQ and vanishes with n. The confidence term is the
standard high-probability price, again multiplied by κQ . In particular, no choice of mining
policy can affect H(g): the teacher entropy is the binding constraint when teacher quality is
the bottleneck.


4.2.4    Implications
Theorem 4.2.1 reframes negative mining away from the language of hardness and toward the
three concrete levers it identifies. Each lever maps to a distinct intervention.

Geometry, not hardness, governs the bias term. The bias term scales with the diameter
∆Q , not with any nominal notion of how challenging the negatives are. A miner that produces
“hard” candidates without tightening ∆Q relative to the evaluation manifold cannot reduce
this term. Conversely, a simple miner that happens to produce a tighter ∆Q achieves the
same geometric improvement at a fraction of the cost. Our empirical ∆Q in Table 4.2 varies
CHAPTER 4. REVISITING TERM-WEIGHTING AS A DATA SOURCE                                           69

modestly across mining domains, which the bound predicts and the in-domain effectiveness
pattern in Table 4.1 corroborates.

Teacher entropy is untouchable by mining. No mining strategy enters the η(H(g)) term.
Improving the bound through this channel requires a sharper teacher, not better negatives.
This explains why expensive ensemble miners often plateau in effectiveness once a teacher
is fixed: they cannot move the term that the teacher controls.

Miner bias is a multiplicative cost, not a benefit. The miner condition number κQ mul-
tiplies both the capacity and confidence terms. A miner that concentrates aggressively on a
narrow region of X inflates κQ , slowing convergence even when the per-pair signal looks
strong. The familiar in-domain success of cascade miners is consistent with this: their κQ
is small relative to in-domain µQ , but the same miners suffer out-of-domain because κQ ex-
plodes when evaluated against a different target measure. Empirical values of H(g), ∆Q , and
κQ across mining domains are reported in Table 4.2 and explain the pattern of in- versus
out-of-domain effectiveness in Section 4.2.4.
    The practical recommendation is to measure these three quantities directly when compar-
ing miners, rather than reasoning about hardness as a single axis. A miner is worth its cost
only when it materially reduces ∆Q or holds κQ near unity under the evaluation distribution
of interest, since neither H(g) nor any term in the bound rewards hardness for its own sake.
Having established theoretical factors in ranking distillation, we now investigate common
settings from literature.


4.2.5    Experimental Setup
Datasets and Metrics We assess in-domain effectiveness on the TREC Deep Learning
2019 [Craswell et al., 2019] and 2020 [Craswell et al., 2020] test collections, retrieving
from the MSMARCO passage collection [Nguyen et al., 2016]. To assess out-of-domain
effectiveness, we use all public test collections comprising the BEIR benchmark [Thakur
et al., 2021]. We report dataset statistics in Appendix A and full out-of-domain results in
Section 4.4.6. All reported test collections apply normalised discounted cumulative gain
(nDCG) [Järvelin and Kekäläinen, 2002] as their primary measure, and we report this value
at a rank cutoff of 10, as is standard, to provide insight at greater recall values, we also report
mean averaged precision (MAP) over full rankings. We use a TOST (Two One-Sided T-test)
to assess statistical equivalence with α = 0.05 and means bounded at 1%. Further details are
provided in Section 4.4.5.
    Models We train both cross-encoders and bi-encoders to assess how representation ca-
pacity affects investigated training settings. All cross-encoders are initialised from an ELEC-
TRA checkpoint [Clark et al., 2020], as this model has been shown empirically to yield
greater effectiveness than a standard BERT model for cross-encoder initialisation [Pradeep
et al., 2022]. We initialise all bi-encoders from the original BERT checkpoint [Devlin et al.,
2019]. Each model is a standard transformer encoder with twelve layers and six attention
CHAPTER 4. REVISITING TERM-WEIGHTING AS A DATA SOURCE                                             70

heads per layer. To ensure reproducibility and clear attribution of effectiveness, we train a
cross-encoder following Pradeep et al. [2022] using the ELECTRA architecture trained for
one epoch with BM25 localised negatives on the MSMARCO-passage training set. Under
distillation, it is generally unnecessary [Althammer et al., 2023, Schlatt et al., 2025] and
computationally infeasible in our study to train all model variants at this scale; thus, this
model acts as a teacher trained in a data-rich environment.
    Loss Criteria As a supervised criterion, we employ localised contrastive estimation
(LCE) [Gao et al., 2021], similar to infoNCE [Gutmann and Hyvärinen, 2010] and more
generally model-conditioned NCE [Ceylan and Gutmann, 2018]. Writing Xi for a known
positive,                                                               

                    ℓLCE (X ; f ) = − log                                             .
                                          exp f (Xi )/τ + ∑m−1

                                                             j=1, j̸=i exp f (X j )/τ

    We employ three semi-supervised criteria prevalent in neural ranking literature. The first,
MarginMSE, aims to reduce the effect of differences in ranking modes by optimising the
margin between positive (Xi ) and negative (X j ) elements instead of pointwise scores [Hof-
stätter et al., 2020]:
                                            m h                                        i2
                 ℓmarginMSE (X ; f , g) = ∑        f (Xi ) − f (X j ) − g(Xi ) − g(X j ) .

increasing application with increasing precision of modern ranking models as it optimises
all x, x′ interactions agnostic of human labels:

 where si j = f (Xi ) − f (X j ), σ (z) = 1+e1 −z , and yi j ∈ {0, 1}. Finally, KL divergence is com-

and Welling, 2014]. It assumes, much like RankNet, that a reference distribution, in this case

identifications for RankNet and MarginMSE are given alongside the Bregman distillation
loss in §4.2.2.
    Implementation Details All models observe a total of 12M documents from the MSMARCO-
passage collection [Nguyen et al., 2016], providing approximately equal computational bud-
get across all training settings with a fixed batch size of 128 pairs. We follow Pradeep et al.
[2022] in setting the learning rate of cross-encoder runs to 1e−5 and Hofstätter et al. [2020]
for bi-encoders, setting the learning rate to 7e−6. We apply a learning rate warmup for 0.1
epochs and then linear decay with an AdamW optimiser using default hyperparameter set-

inference and evaluation [Macdonald and Tonellotto, 2020].
    Negative Sampling Sources We consider four sampling sources in our investigation,
aligning with those applied in literature. The first is uniform selection from the training
corpus (Random). The second is a lexical heuristic BM25 (k1 = 1.2, b = 0.75) [Robertson
et al., 1995], a lightweight retrieval model. The third is our teacher model, a cross-encoder
(CE). Finally, we apply the ensemble pipeline shown in Figure 4.2 (Ensemble). We use the
rankings supplied, but filter them by our teacher model to ensure fairness across settings. We
outline all models contained within this ensemble in Section 4.4.5.


4.2.6     Discussion
In Table 4.2 we report empirical estimates of the three quantities H(g), ∆Q , and κQ across
mining domains, alongside the effectiveness results in Table 4.1. We use these together to
interpret each empirical observation against the bound.
    Effectiveness under Different Empirical Distributions Our ablation of sampling distri-
butions under semi-supervision shows multiple cases where investing computational budget
in a strong estimator is unnecessary to improve generalisation both in- and out-of-domain.
In Table 4.1, rows 1-4 show effectiveness in a supervised setting using a contrastive objec-
CHAPTER 4. REVISITING TERM-WEIGHTING AS A DATA SOURCE                                         72

Table 4.2: Empirical Values of teacher entropy, the relative density ratio of each sampling
domain and empirical measures of the query diameter. Each is taken at the 95th percentile
(either max or mean) for robust estimates. Entropy is measured in Nats via Shannon entropy
over each ranking. We note that these are approximations of the theoretical aspects discussed
in this work.

                  Source            bν (g)
                                    H                κbQ             ∆
                                                                     bQ

                  Random         6.62±0.127 14.202±556.251 10.448±0.044
                  BM25          4.973±1.978 12.747±461.588 9.862±0.205
                  Cross-Encoder 4.068±0.930 11.116±353.018 9.593±0.251
                  Ensemble      3.973±0.838 8.276±165.234 9.546±0.233


tive. In-domain, it is clear that localised sampling by heuristics can be effective as there
is a clear trend in effectiveness as “hardness” and thus computation is expended. Out-of-
domain, observe that generally, where a single estimator-induced distribution is insufficient
to cover the query manifold, a random or ensemble sample yields greater robustness. We find
that, though in explicit contrastive learning, there is a clear trend that the tighter a sampling
distribution is, the more model effectiveness can continue to improve; we find that under all
semi-supervision settings, effectiveness plateaus once a minimal locality is enforced (BM25)
with inconsistent effectiveness improvements suggesting that heuristics such as mining from
rankings are insufficient to explain effectiveness gains.
     Though bias induced by sampling is indeed reduced by ensembling approaches, the em-
pirical diameter ∆Q does not shrink across mining domains, as shown in Table 4.2; this
explains the minimal change in out-of-domain effectiveness, since the bias term in the bound
is largely unchanged. Aligning with the κQ term in Theorem 4.2.1, we see that when den-
sity ratios are minimised across our settings under an ensembling approach, a Bi-Encoder
continues to improve, suggesting this term can have a larger effect; nevertheless, we do not
find settings where a statistically differentiable positive effect is found across multiple do-
mains (e.g. helping both in- and out-of-domain) when applying computationally expensive
data selection strategies.
     Effectiveness under Different Entropy Having controlled for entropy in different train-
ing settings, we now fix the sampling domain πQ , choosing BM25 and select rankings based
on where they lie within the sampled teacher entropy distribution by quartile. In Table 4.3,
we see that once locality is established, one can further improve effectiveness in-domain
by selecting examples conditioned on ranking entropy. Even under a contrastive setting we
find that sub-selection by some teacher (significantly less expensive than ensembling before
similarly filtering) can further improve performance, reducing the gap observed in our main
results under a contrastive objective. We see that choosing the central mass of the entropy dis-
tribution (inner quartiles) is most effective and can be contrasted with selection in the outlier
quartiles in which effectiveness degrades. Generally we observe that the upper quartile will
yield greater effectiveness over the lower quartile, coupled with the reduced effectiveness of
selection via outlier quartiles we consider that a balance must be struck between capturing

high entropy examples for the purposes of generalisation. We note that all cases degrade out-
of-domain potentially suggesting that choosing examples by these criteria induces overfitting
to the particular cases within the training domain. We observe that increased mean entropy
leads to reduced effectiveness; considering Table 4.2, the diameter ∆Q is insufficiently small
to compensate for the high η(H(g)). Out-of-domain, the same selection criteria fail because
κQ inflates under domain shift, dominating the bound and erasing any locality benefit.


4.3     Conclusions
The density-ratio analysis and empirical ablation demonstrate that the factors governing
ranking distillation effectiveness are teacher entropy and the geometry of the query mani-
fold, not the expense of the negative mining process. BM25-sampled negatives are statis-
tically equivalent to expensively mined negatives under all semi-supervised criteria tested,
across both in-domain and out-of-domain evaluation. The multi-stage cascades that domi-
nate current practice, ensembling multiple retrievers, filtering by a cross-encoder, iterating
over increasingly hard negatives, do not tighten the essential diameter of the query space in a
way that justifies their computational cost. Under distillation, the choice of sampling domain
affects score distributions and model confidence but does not yield statistically distinguish-
able improvements in ranking effectiveness.

Verdict on H1.2. H1.2 is supported within a clearly bounded scope. For distillation on
the MSMARCO passage collection, evaluated on the associated TREC DL and BEIR bench-
marks, BM25-sampled negatives are statistically equivalent to expensively mined negatives
under every semi-supervised criterion tested, for both bi-encoders and cross-encoders, in-
domain and out-of-domain. The density-ratio analysis explains why: the operative factors
are teacher pairwise entropy and the essential diameter of the query manifold, neither of
which the mining cascade tightens enough to register. The verdict is not claimed beyond this
CHAPTER 4. REVISITING TERM-WEIGHTING AS A DATA SOURCE                                         74

regime. It is established under a distillation objective with an effective teacher; it is not es-
tablished for contrastive training without a teacher, for recall-sensitive tasks where candidate
diversity dominates, or for domain-specific corpora where BM25’s term statistics are poorly
calibrated. These boundaries are the subject of a forward direction in Section 8.2.2.

Scope and limitations. Three limitations bound the claims above. First, the analysis and
experiments consider uniform sampling from a candidate list; this is the most common set-
ting, but top-k sampling is becoming more prevalent and is not analysed here. Second, when
examples are selected by teacher entropy, some overfitting may occur that an explicit reg-
ulariser could control; we do not pursue this, since the experiments are intended to expose
entropy as a lever on ranking behaviour rather than to optimise it. Third, although the study
spans the common cross-encoder and bi-encoder architecture classes, the set of architectures
and teachers examined is not exhaustive.
    These findings should be scoped precisely. The equivalence demonstrated here holds un-
der distillation on the MSMARCO passage collection and its associated TREC DL and BEIR
evaluation benchmarks. The chapter does not establish the equivalence under contrastive ob-
jectives without a teacher, on recall-sensitive tasks where candidate diversity matters more,
or on domain-specific corpora where BM25’s term statistics are poorly calibrated; Chapter 8
takes up these conditions as part of the broader programme this thesis opens (§8.2.1, §8.2.2).
    The results complement those of Chapter 3 at two levels. At the empirical level, that
chapter showed that the benchmarks used to justify expensive models cannot reliably mea-
sure the marginal gains those models claim; this chapter shows that the training pipelines
producing those marginal gains are unnecessary under distillation. At the structural level,
both chapters illustrate an advantage of separable components: BM25 negatives are a sep-
arable, interpretable, and reproducible source of training signal whose contribution can be
evaluated independently of any intermediate neural model. The tightly coupled alternatives,
multi-stage cascades whose components cannot be isolated, are more expensive, less repro-
ducible, and, as shown here, no more effective.


4.4     Proofs and Additional Results
This section provides the proofs of the generalisation bound stated in §4.2 and the com-
plete per-dataset out-of-domain results. The notation, formal definitions, loss criteria, and
corollaries are given inline earlier in the chapter.


4.4.1    Preliminaries
We work over a metric-measure space (X , ρ, µQ ) where X is the joint query-document
space, ρ is a ground metric (in our experiments, cosine distance over a fixed pretrained
embedding), and µQ is the query-conditional reference measure under which we evaluate
risk. Training proceeds over pairs (x, x′ ) drawn from a possibly biased mining policy πQ
CHAPTER 4. REVISITING TERM-WEIGHTING AS A DATA SOURCE                                         75

whose support lies in that of µQ .
   The student f is trained against a fixed teacher g via a pairwise Bregman surrogate; the
population quantity of interest is the pairwise misordering risk

                                                                  f (x) < f (x′ ) .
                                                                                
                                R µQ ( f ) =       Pr
                                               (x,x′ )∼µQ⊗2


    Our analysis isolates three quantities, each of which is interpretable in isolation and each
of which is estimable from data: the teacher’s pairwise entropy H(g), the essential diameter
∆Q of the query manifold, and the miner condition number κQ . The main theorem states that
the excess risk of an empirical risk minimiser decomposes additively into three terms gov-
erned by exactly these three quantities, plus a sample-size term controlled by the surrogate’s
Rademacher complexity.

Pairwise Bregman surrogates

Definition 4.4.1 (Bregman divergence [Bregman, 1967]). For a strictly convex C1 potential
φ : R → R, the Bregman divergence is

                           Dφ (a∥b) = φ (a) − φ (b) − φ ′ (b)(a − b).

Definition 4.4.2 (Pairwise surrogate). Writing δx,x′ (h) := h(x)−h(x′ ), the per-pair surrogate
is
                             ℓh (x, x′ ) := Dφ δx,x′ (h) ∥ δx,x′ (g) .
                                                                    

    The losses used in this chapter are all pairwise Bregman surrogates. RankNet cor-
responds to φ (u) = u log u + (1 − u) log(1 − u) applied to sigmoid-transformed margins;
MarginMSE corresponds to φ (u) = u2 applied directly to margins; the KL form follows
by the same construction with the same potential as RankNet but applied to teacher-induced
soft targets. The explicit identifications are given earlier in §4.2.2.

The three quantities

Definition 4.4.3 (Pairwise teacher entropy). Let px,x′ := Pr[g(x) > g(x′ )] where the probabil-
ity is taken over any teacher randomness (deterministic teachers give p ∈ {0, 1} pointwise).
The pairwise teacher entropy is
                                                                            
        H(g) := E(x,x′ )∼µ ⊗2 −px,x′ log px,x′ − (1 − px,x′ ) log(1 − px,x′ ) ∈ [0, log 2].
                            Q


    H(g) is small when the teacher confidently picks an order on most pairs and large when
the teacher’s pairwise verdicts are close to a coin flip. It is a property of the teacher and the
evaluation measure jointly, not of any specific student.
Definition 4.4.4 (Essential diameter).

                  ∆Q := inf M ≥ 0 : µQ⊗2 {(x, x′ ) : ρ(x, x′ ) > M} = 0 .
                                                                  
CHAPTER 4. REVISITING TERM-WEIGHTING AS A DATA SOURCE                                          76

    ∆Q measures the geometric extent of the support: how far apart can two points drawn
from µQ be? On a unit sphere with cosine distance the worst-case diameter saturates near
2, but the finitary essential diameter on a query-conditional support is informative; in our
experiments it varies systematically with the mining domain (see the diameter estimates
reported earlier in this chapter).

Definition 4.4.5 (Miner condition number). With wQ = dµQ /dπQ the Radon-Nikodym deriva-
tive,
                              κQ := ess sup wQ (x) ∈ [1, ∞].
                                        x∈supp πQ

    κQ is the worst-case factor by which the miner under-represents a region that the evalua-
tion measure cares about. Unbiased mining gives κQ = 1; aggressive miners that concentrate
on a narrow slice of X give large κQ .

Capacity via Rademacher complexity

We control the capacity of the student class through the Rademacher complexity of the in-
duced surrogate class, which lets us state the bound for any architecture for which such a
complexity is finite without committing to combinatorial dimension counting.
                                                                                2
Definition 4.4.6 (Empirical Rademacher complexity). For a class F ⊆ RX of functions on
pairs and an i.i.d. sample S = {(xs , xs′ )}ns=1 ,

                                                       n
                            b n (F ; S) := Eε sup 1 ∑ εs f (xs , x′ ),
                            R                                     s
                                              f ∈F n s=1


where εs are i.i.d. Rademacher signs. Its expectation Rn (F ) := ES R
                                                                    b n (F ; S) is the popula-
tion version.

    We assume Rn (L) → 0 for the surrogate class L := {ℓh : h ∈ H} at the standard rate
O(n−1/2 ). This holds for linear classes with bounded norm, kernel classes with bounded
RKHS norm, and (under standard implicit-regularisation assumptions) for the trained-network
classes used in our experiments.


4.4.2    The Three Levers, Informally
Before the proofs, we describe the three forces that govern excess risk and how each cor-
responds to one of the quantities above. The reader can take this section as a roadmap;
everything stated here is made precise in §4.4.3.
    The first lever is teacher resolution. If the teacher’s pairwise verdicts are close to uniform
on most pairs, no student can do better than guess on those pairs. This is captured by H(g):
high entropy means the teacher offers little signal. The student’s risk against the Bayes
order is bounded below by something that grows with H(g), and the contribution of teacher
uncertainty to the student’s excess risk is correspondingly bounded above by a function of
H(g).
CHAPTER 4. REVISITING TERM-WEIGHTING AS A DATA SOURCE                                              77

    The second lever is geometry. Even a perfect teacher cannot transfer information about
pairs that are far apart in (X , ρ) to pairs that are close, unless we have some regularity. We
express this regularity by assuming the Bayes scorer f ⋆ is Lipschitz in ρ. The geometric
extent over which the student must agree with the Bayes order is then controlled by ∆Q : a
tight query manifold means small score variation across pairs, hence sharper learnability.
    The third lever is miner bias. Training pairs are drawn from πQ , but risk is measured
under µQ . The price for this mismatch is a multiplicative factor κQ in the variance of any
unbiased estimator, which propagates to the rate.
    The decomposition we prove in §4.4.3 reads, schematically,

                  ζφ L∆Q η(H(g))               +     C κQ Rn (L)           + |confidence term},
                  |     {z     }                     | {z }                      √ {z
               teacher resolution + geometry       miner bias + capacity       O(   log(1/δ )/n)


with η an explicit, decreasing function. Each of the three quantities H(g), ∆Q , κQ enters
exactly once, in a place where its meaning makes intuitive sense.


4.4.3    Main Result
We now assemble the bound. The argument follows the standard structure of a calibration-
based excess-risk decomposition: a teacher-Bayes gap controlled by the entropy term, a
student-teacher gap controlled by the surrogate excess, and a uniform deviation argument
that reweights for the miner.

Teacher resolution: from entropy to misordering

The first move is to control how far the teacher itself can be from the Bayes order on a
typical pair. The right direction of Pinsker’s inequality, and the right primitive to use, is the
following.

Lemma 4.4.1 (Teacher misordering from pairwise entropy). For any pair (x, x′ ), write p =
Pr[g(x) > g(x′ )] and let H(p) = −p log p − (1 − p) log(1 − p). Then

Reading. Lemma 4.4.1 bounds how far the teacher’s pairwise distribution is from uniform.
When H(g) is close to log 2 the teacher is uninformative and the bound is near zero; when
H(g) is small the teacher is sharp and the bound is close to 12 . This is the right direction for
our purposes because the surrogate excess risk we will bound below is multiplied by L∆Q ,
which sets a scale for how much score-space movement the geometry permits; together they
say “the teacher can only mis-rank pairs by at most this much, scaled by how far apart they
can be.”

Surrogate calibration: from Bregman excess to misordering excess

We use the standard classification-calibration result of Bartlett et al. [2006], instantiated for
our two surrogates. We state it without reproof.

Lemma 4.4.2 (Surrogate calibration; instance of Bartlett-Jordan-McAuliffe). Let φ be ei-
ther the negative-binary-entropy potential (RankNet) or the squared potential (MarginMSE).
There exists a constant ζφ > 0 such that for any scorer h,
                                                        q

For RankNet, ζφ =     2; for MarginMSE, ζφ = 2.

Reading. Calibration says that any surrogate excess we control gets translated into mis-
ordering excess at the price of a square root and a known constant. The RankNet con-
stant matches the constant we would have obtained directly from Pinsker on the condi-
tional Bernoulli, but Bartlett-Jordan-McAuliffe gives the result cleanly without a tedious
sign-boundary argument.

We replace the VC argument of the previous version with a one-line symmetrisation bound.
This is what lets the result apply to neural ranking models without an artificial dimension
count.

Reading. The bound depends on the model class only through Rn (L), which we leave
