4.4.4    Reading the Bound
The boxed bound of Equation (4.3) in Theorem 4.4.5 has three terms, each governed by
exactly one of the three quantities introduced in §4.4.1. Throughout, n denotes the number
of training pairs drawn from the mining policy πQ , as fixed in assumption 4; statements about
a term “vanishing” refer to its behaviour as n grows.

The teacher term. The first summand of Equation (4.3), ζφ L∆Q η(H(g)), does not vanish
as n → ∞: it contains no n at all. It is the floor on excess risk imposed by teacher uncer-
tainty, scaled by the largest score gap the Bayes scorer can produce on the query-document
manifold. That scale is L∆Q because of assumption 1, which asks f ⋆ to be L-Lipschitz with
respect to the ground metric ρ: for every pair of query-document points x, x′ ∈ X ,

                                | f ⋆ (x) − f ⋆ (x′ )| ≤ L ρ(x, x′ ).                                 (4.10)

In words, the Bayes scorer cannot assign wildly different relevance scores to two points that
the metric considers close; L caps how fast relevance may change as one moves through the
space. Combined with assumption 2, which caps ρ itself at ∆Q , no pair can be separated by
more than L∆Q in score. This is the standard Lipschitz smoothness condition used to relate
metric structure to risk in statistical learning theory [Bartlett and Mendelson, 2002, Bartlett
et al., 2006].
    Negative mining cannot reduce this term because mining only changes which pairs are
drawn, that is, the policy πQ , and πQ does not appear in ζφ L∆Q η(H(g)). The quantities that
CHAPTER 4. REVISITING TERM-WEIGHTING AS A DATA SOURCE                                       82

do appear are properties of the teacher (H(g)), of the Bayes scorer (L), of the evaluation
geometry (∆Q ), and of the surrogate (ζφ ) — none of which a sampler can alter. Sharpening
the teacher does reduce the term, because η is increasing in H(g): a teacher whose pairwise
preference distribution is closer to deterministic has lower entropy, so η(H(g)) shrinks and
with it the floor. In the limiting case of a perfectly confident and correct teacher, H(g) = 0
and the term vanishes entirely; in the opposite case of a teacher that is indifferent on every
pair, H(g) = log 2 and the floor is at its worst.
                                                                         q
The capacity term. The second summand of Equation (4.3), 2ζφ κQ2 Rn (L)L∆Q , van-
ishes with n at whatever rate Rn (L) decays, but is multiplied by the miner condition number
κQ . The intuition for why a large κQ slows convergence is a reweighting argument. Training
pairs arrive from πQ , but the quantity we care about is risk under the evaluation measure µQ ;
to correct for the mismatch each observed pair is reweighted by wQ = dµQ /dπQ , and κQ is
the largest such weight. When the miner concentrates on a narrow region of X , the regions
it under-samples must be assigned large weights to compensate, so a handful of rare draws
come to dominate the weighted average. The estimator remains unbiased, but its effective
sample size falls: informally, n pairs drawn under a miner with condition number κQ carry
roughly the information of n/κQ2 pairs drawn under µQ . More draws are therefore needed
to reach the same accuracy, which is exactly the κQ2 inflation visible in Lemma 4.4.4 and
Equation (4.2).
    This is the only place in the bound where the choice of πQ enters, and that localisation is
the practical payoff of the analysis. It means the mining strategy is a pure sample-efficiency
lever: a better miner buys faster convergence towards the teacher, never a better asymptote.
Any hope of surpassing the teacher-imposed floor must come from a different intervention,
and the bound says which one — reducing H(g) or tightening the geometry — rather than
from further engineering of the negative sampler.

                                                                           p
The confidence term. The third summand of Equation (4.3), ζφ κQ L∆Q log(2/δ )/n, is
the standard price paid for a high-probability guarantee rather than a guarantee in expecta-
tion. The probability in question is over the random draw of the n training pairs, not over
document relevance: the bound holds for at least a 1 − δ fraction of training samples one
might have drawn, and δ is the tolerance for the unlucky remainder. Demanding greater
certainty (smaller δ ) costs only logarithmically. This term is again scaled by κQ , for the
same effective-sample-size reason as the capacity term, so a concentrating miner widens the
confidence interval as well as slowing convergence; the two effects compound, and a miner
with large κQ is penalised twice over.

What this says about negative mining. The traditional rationale for hard-negative mining
is that harder negatives make a more informative training signal. Theorem 4.4.5 gives a
more nuanced picture: a miner is helpful precisely insofar as it does not inflate κQ , and
a miner that aggressively concentrates on hard candidates may actually slow convergence
CHAPTER 4. REVISITING TERM-WEIGHTING AS A DATA SOURCE                                       83

even if it appears to improve loss values during training. The diameter ∆Q matters too:
a miner that produces a tighter ∆Q relative to the evaluation manifold reduces both bias
terms simultaneously. Crucially, no amount of mining touches H(g). The teacher entropy
is the binding constraint when teacher quality is the bottleneck, as it is in the contemporary
distillation setups that train a bi-encoder or late-interaction student against a fixed cross-
encoder teacher: MarginMSE and Topic-Aware Sampling distillation from a BERT cross-
encoder [Hofstätter et al., 2020], the cross-encoder re-ranker distillation used in RocketQA-
style pipelines [Qu et al., 2021], and the sequence-to-sequence teachers of monoT5 and its
successors [Pradeep et al., 2022]. In each case the student is trained to reproduce a teacher
whose own pairwise preferences are uncertain, so H(g) bounds what any amount of sampling
can achieve.

What this says about model behaviour. Excess risk decomposes additively, so the em-
pirical question is which of the three summands of Equation (4.3) — teacher, capacity, or
confidence — dominates at the sample sizes used in practice. The empirical lever esti-
mates reported in this chapter show that ∆Q varies modestly across mining domains while
κQ varies by orders of magnitude in worst-case behaviour and H(g) varies by roughly a fac-
tor of two between random and ensemble miners. Theorem 4.4.5 predicts that effectiveness
gains across mining domains under distillation should track η(H(g)) when the capacity term
is dominated, and that the residual variation should track κQ . This is consistent with the em-
pirical pattern in the experiments section of this chapter: BM25, CE, and Ensemble miners
produce comparable in-domain effectiveness once locality is established, with differences
explained by H(g) rather than nominal hardness.

What this says about evaluation. Out-of-domain effectiveness is governed by the same
three quantities evaluated under the out-of-domain µQ . A miner that produces small in-
domain κQ may produce large out-of-domain κQ , which is exactly the failure mode visible in
our BEIR results: the ensemble miner is competitive in-domain but loses to simpler miners
on several BEIR splits where the implicit miner-induced measure no longer matches the
evaluation measure. The bound predicts this directly.


4.4.5    Additional Experimental Details
Empirical Approximations

We apply Monte-Carlo sampling to provide empirical estimates of theoretical values outlined
in Section 4.2, our sample size is equal to our training corpus (maximising possible samples
under our setting). We compute Hν (g) over teacher scores of training data observed during
the training of each model.
    As our true measure µQ over X is latent (or infeasible to compute with standard bench-
marks due to query mismatch), we provide an approximation of κQ , the density ratio bridging
our biased measure νQ to our unbiased risk. We do so by assuming a uniform chance of sam-
CHAPTER 4. REVISITING TERM-WEIGHTING AS A DATA SOURCE                                       84

pling all documents as opposed to a rank-biased sample taking 1/g(Q, D), ∀D ∈ XQ , κQ is
then taken as the supremum of these values.
    To compute an empirical diameter ∆ bQ , we apply cosine distance over representations
from an existing embedding model (we use RetroMAE [Xiao et al., 2022], a strong embed-
ding model based on MAE pre-training) as our ground metric ρ and compute the supremum
over Monte-Carlo samples from our training data over each query.

TOST test

A two one-sided t-test (TOST) determines if the means of two populations are equivalent
based on independent samples from each population, in our case, the query-level effective-
ness of two models. For means µ1 , µ2 and confidence bound θ = |µ2 − µ1 | · ε (ε is a per-
centage bound parameter), we assess two hypotheses, that µ2 − µ1 lies above θ and below
θ using one-sided t-tests with confidence 1 − 2α compensating for multiple hypothesis test-
ing. Thus, within an ε bound with confidence 1 − α, we can say that µ1 , µ2 are statistically
equivalent.


4.4.6    Out-of-domain Effectiveness
Results for all BEIR splits are reported across three tables per architecture, grouped by
dataset family; the CQADupStack tables are scaled down so that all thirteen sub-forums
fit across the text width. For bi-encoder models, Table 4.4 covers the CQADupStack sub-
forums, Table 4.5 the question-answering and entity-retrieval collections, and Table 4.6 the
argument, claim, and scientific collections. The corresponding cross-encoder results are Ta-
bles 4.7, 4.8, and 4.9.


4.4.7    Additional Figures
Figure 4.3 reports the training dynamics behind these results, plotting the exponential mov-
ing average of the distillation loss and of the student gradient norm for each sampling regime.
    As a qualitative way to discriminate between domains, observe across Figure 4.4 the
increasing alignment of model scores to a power law as harder negatives are applied. Under
increasingly difficult negatives, the model’s score distribution stretches into a heavy-tailed,
near-power-law form: only a few distractors receive high scores, while other documents are
driven sharply downward. The slope of this tail offers a simple, domain-agnostic measure of
how confidently the model assigns relevance. Importantly, when evaluated out-of-domain,
this power-law alignment vanishes entirely, potentially indicating overfitting, as indicated by
the reduced out-of-domain effectiveness reported earlier in this chapter.
    Chapters 3 and 4 have examined the internal validity of neural ranking: whether the eval-
uation methodology measures what it claims to measure and whether training pipelines are
efficiently designed. Chapter 5 then asks what separability buys in effectiveness terms, be-
fore Chapters 6 and 7 turn to external validity: whether the systems we build are trustworthy

Chapter 5

Effectiveness through Decomposed
Structure

   Hypothesis H2 (tested in this chapter)
   A query-independent document-quality prior, learned offline and composed with ex-
   isting rankers through score fusion, improves both first-stage retrieval and downstream
   re-ranking effectiveness without increasing query-time cost.

    The two preceding chapters examined the internal validity of the neural ranking pipeline:
Chapter 3 showed that benchmark evaluation cannot reliably discriminate the expensive
models at the frontier, and Chapter 4 showed that the elaborate cascades producing those
models are unnecessary under distillation. Both are arguments about cost and measurement,
the dynamic component fθ (q, d) and the infrastructure around it. This chapter turns to what
separable structure positively contributes. It reintroduces the query-independent document
prior gφ (d) that classical probabilistic retrieval maintained and that neural systems collapsed,
and asks whether estimating and composing it explicitly improves effectiveness.
    Classical probabilistic retrieval models maintained an explicit separation between the evi-
dence that must be resolved at query time and the evidence that can be established in advance.
Under the query-likelihood formulation, relevance estimation decomposes as p(d|q) ∝ p(q|d)p(d),
where the likelihood p(q|d) captures how well a document’s language matches a particular
query and the prior p(d) encodes stable beliefs about the document (quality, authority, struc-
tural completeness, or domain suitability) that hold regardless of what is being searched for.
This decomposition was not merely notational: it separated the scoring work that must be
repeated for every query from the scoring work that can be performed once, offline, for every
document in the collection.
    Neural ranking models abandoned this separation. The contrast is clearest when the two
scoring regimes are written side by side. The classical decomposition assigns each source of
evidence its own term,

                       Ssep (q, d) =      f (q, d)         +       gφ (d)      ,        (5.1)
                                          |θ {z }                  | {z }
                                       query-conditioned       query-independent



                                                89
CHAPTER 5. EFFECTIVENESS THROUGH DECOMPOSED STRUCTURE                                     90

whereas a contemporary neural ranker exposes only a single scoring function,

                                   Scol (q, d) = sθ (q, d),                             (5.2)

in which no syntactic component corresponds to gφ . Whether implemented as bi-encoder
similarity, cross-encoder re-ranking, or learned sparse retrieval, such models absorb document-
side structure into the parameters θ of Equation (5.2) through training data distributions,
negative sampling procedures, and end-to-end optimisation.
    The distinction between Equations (5.1) and (5.2) is one of accessibility rather than
of content. MacAvaney et al. [2022a] show through prompt-adaptation experiments with
monoT5 that a substantial fraction of a neural ranker’s scoring behaviour is attributable
to query-independent document properties rather than to query-document interaction; the
query-independent evidence is still being used, and sθ still behaves in part as though it con-
tained a gφ term. What has disappeared is not the prior itself but its separate existence as
an addressable component of the model: in moving from Equation (5.1) to Equation (5.2) it
has been marginalised into the learned representation, entangled with query-dependent sig-
nal in a way that prevents its separate estimation, reuse, or control. This entanglement has
a concrete effectiveness cost. Modelling capacity that could be allocated to resolving query
intent is instead spent recovering structure that is stable across queries and could have been
estimated once offline. A cross-encoder that must simultaneously learn that a document is
well-written, structurally coherent, and from an authoritative source; properties that hold for
every query, has less capacity remaining to learn that this particular document addresses this
particular query’s specific informational need. Relevant documents whose utility is medi-
ated by document-side properties may be systematically underranked when those properties
compete for representational resources with query-dependent matching. In this chapter, we
formalise this argument by treating retrieval as approximate posterior inference and extend-
ing the classical prior-likelihood decomposition to contemporary learned systems. We show
that a query-independent document-quality prior, learned entirely offline from standard rel-
evance corpora, can be composed with existing retrieval and re-ranking pipelines through
simple additive score fusion to improve ranking effectiveness. The prior requires only a
cached score lookup at query time introducing zero additional neural inference and can be
updated, audited, or replaced independently of the deployed dynamic model. Our investiga-
tion is deliberately minimal in its assumptions. We do not calibrate fusion weights; we do not
jointly train the prior and the likelihood; we do not assume access to any signal beyond what
is already available in standard training corpora. The fusion is an additive interpolation with
equal weighting. If even this uncalibrated, independently trained prior yields measurable
gains, the implication is that the entanglement neural systems introduce is not cost-free, and
the principle of separation that classical IR maintained remains productive. The calibrated,
jointly optimised composition that this minimal setting deliberately defers is developed as a
thesis-level direction in Section 8.2.1.
    Section 5.1 reviews document-quality estimation and static priors, score and rank fu-
CHAPTER 5. EFFECTIVENESS THROUGH DECOMPOSED STRUCTURE                                                      91

sion, and prior-likelihood composition in adjacent domains. The body of the chapter then
formalises retrieval as approximate posterior inference, develops the prior and its offline es-
timation, reports the first-stage and re-ranking experiments under additive fusion, and closes
with the verdict on H2, including the one setting in which the uncalibrated prior does not
help.


5.1      Related Work
This section reviews work directly relevant to the effectiveness contribution of this chap-
ter: document quality estimation and learned static priors (Section 5.1.1), score and rank
fusion methods (Section 5.1.2), and the use of prior-likelihood composition in related fields
(Section 5.1.3).


5.1.1     Document Quality Estimation and Static Priors
The idea that document-level properties can improve retrieval independently of any particu-
lar query has a long history. PageRank [Brin and Page, 1998] demonstrated that link-based
authority, computed entirely offline, could substantially improve web search when combined
with lexical matching. Subsequent work incorporated freshness, source reputation, and doc-
ument type as static features in learning-to-rank pipelines, where they were treated as com-
ponents of xstat (d) alongside dynamic query-document features [Craswell et al., 2005, Qin
et al., 2010]. In these systems, static priors improved effectiveness precisely because they
captured relevance-correlated properties that dynamic scoring alone could not recover from
term overlap.
    Neural approaches to document quality estimation have extended this principle. Ben-
dersky et al. [2011] showed that document-level quality features, readability, authority, and
structural completeness, could be predicted from text alone and used to improve ad hoc
retrieval when combined with query-dependent scores. More recently, neural quality classi-
fiers have been trained to predict document utility without access to any query. The QualT5
models used in this chapter [Chang et al., 2024] exemplify this approach: they are T5-based
encoder-decoder models trained with binary cross-entropy to distinguish relevant from non-
relevant documents using only the document as input, with hard negatives drawn from BM25
retrieval.1 Functionally, this training is identical to that of monoT5 [Nogueira et al., 2020]
but with the query removed from the input context, isolating the query-independent compo-
nent of what monoT5 learns.
    A related line of work has made explicit the observation that neural rankers learn sub-
stantial query-independent behaviour even when trained with query-conditioned objectives.
MacAvaney et al. [2022a] demonstrated through prompt-adaptation experiments that a sig-
nificant fraction of monoT5’s scoring behaviour is attributable to document-side properties
1 Model checkpoints, datasets, software, and compute used across the contribution chapters are consolidated in

 Appendix A.
CHAPTER 5. EFFECTIVENESS THROUGH DECOMPOSED STRUCTURE                                        92

rather than to query-document interaction. This finding motivates the present chapter: if
neural rankers already learn an implicit prior, there may be gains from learning it explicitly
and composing it with the dynamic scorer in a controlled way.
     Document-level signals can also be derived by aggregating query-conditioned scores
across a query distribution. If a document receives high scores from a cross-encoder across
many diverse queries, this consistency itself constitutes a query-independent quality signal.
Chang et al. [2024] formalised this view and showed that such aggregated scores can be dis-
tilled into a document-only model that serves as a static prior. The priors used in this chapter
can be understood as instances of this broader family: they estimate gφ (d) from data without
requiring any query at inference time.


5.1.2    Score Fusion and Rank Fusion
Separating a static prior from a query-conditioned likelihood raises an immediate question
that the preceding sections have left open: once the two components are estimated apart, how
should they be recombined into a single ranking? This is not a new problem. Retrieval has
combined evidence from multiple sources for decades, and the prior-likelihood composition
used in this chapter is a particular instance of that older practice. This section therefore re-
views the fusion literature in order to place the composition operator of Equation 2.1 within
it, and to make precise which design choices this chapter inherits and which it fixes deliber-
ately.
     Combining multiple retrieval signals into a single ranking is a classical problem in infor-
mation retrieval. Score-level fusion methods combine the numeric scores produced by differ-
ent systems for the same document. CombSUM and CombMNZ [Shaw and Fox, 1994] are
early examples: CombSUM adds normalised scores across systems, while CombMNZ addi-
tionally weights by the number of systems that retrieved the document. Log-linear combina-
tion of scoring potentials provides a probabilistic interpretation: under a product-of-experts
view, each system contributes a factor to a joint scoring function, and the combined score is
their sum in log-space [Hinton, 2002].
     Rank-level fusion methods operate on the rank positions rather than raw scores, avoiding
the need for score normalisation. Reciprocal rank fusion (RRF) [Cormack et al., 2009] de-
fines the fused score as a sum of 1/(k + ri ) across systems, where ri is the rank assigned by
system i and k is a constant. RRF has become widely adopted in practice due to its simplic-
ity, robustness, and strong empirical performance, particularly when combining lexical and
neural retrieval [Lin et al., 2021].
     The fusion used in this chapter is additive interpolation,

                                S(q, d) = sθ (q, d) + γ uφ (d),                           (5.3)

which can be interpreted as a special case of log-linear combination where the dynamic
scorer and the static prior each contribute a factor. This differs from typical learning-to-rank
feature combination in two respects. First, the two components of Equation (5.3), uφ (d)
CHAPTER 5. EFFECTIVENESS THROUGH DECOMPOSED STRUCTURE                                         93

and sθ (q, d), are functionally orthogonal: the utility model sees strictly less input than the
relevance scorer (no query), so gains cannot stem from complementary data access but must
reflect document-level regularities that sθ can represent in principle but struggles to dis-
entangle from query-dependent signal during joint optimisation. Second, the prior can be
computed offline and cached, contributing zero marginal query-time cost and admitting in-
dependent update, audit, or replacement.
    Having fixed the form of the composition in Equation (5.3), one further choice remains:
how the weight γ is set. The distinction here is between calibrated and uncalibrated com-
position. Calibrated fusion learns the combination weights (α, γ) from held-out data, po-
tentially jointly optimising the prior and likelihood components. Uncalibrated fusion fixes
these weights (e.g., equal weighting) and relies on the complementarity of the components
without adaptation. This chapter deliberately operates in the uncalibrated setting: if gains
are observed without calibration, the implication is that the prior provides complementary
signal in principle, with calibrated composition as an avenue for further improvement.


5.1.3    Priors in Related Fields
The prior-likelihood decomposition investigated in this chapter has parallels in recommender
systems and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG).
    In collaborative filtering, user-item interaction is decomposed into user priors (prefer-
ences, demographics), item priors (popularity, quality), and their interaction [Koren et al.,
2009]. Matrix factorisation models explicitly separate user and item biases from the latent
interaction factor, and these biases serve precisely the role of gφ (d) and hψ (q) in Equa-
tion 2.1: they capture stable properties of users and items that hold across interactions. The
analogy is instructive: just as recommender systems benefit from explicit bias terms that pre-
vent the interaction model from wasting capacity on recovering popularity effects, retrieval
systems may benefit from explicit document priors that prevent the query-conditioned scorer
from wasting capacity on recovering quality effects.
    In retrieval-augmented generation, the generator must select context from a set of re-
trieved documents. List-wise re-rankers such as RankGPT [Sun et al., 2023] and RankZephyr
[Pradeep et al., 2023] can be understood as performing a form of posterior refinement: given
a candidate set and a query, they reorder documents to maximise the probability that the
top-ranked documents are relevant. When an LLM-based generator subsequently consumes
these documents, it performs a further refinement step, selecting which passages to attend to
and how to integrate them into the generated response. This multi-stage process of retrieval,
re-ranking, and generation is an instance of the staged posterior refinement formalised in this
chapter as p(0) (d) → p(1) (d|q, x1 ) → · · · → p(k) (d|q, x1:k ). Document priors can enter this
pipeline at any stage, providing a query-independent regularising signal that improves the
initial ordering presented to downstream components.
    Agentic RAG systems [Schick et al., 2023, Yao et al., 2023], in which an agent iteratively
retrieves, reasons, and re-retrieves, extend this pattern further. Each retrieval-reasoning cycle
CHAPTER 5. EFFECTIVENESS THROUGH DECOMPOSED STRUCTURE                                          94

introduces a new evidence factor conditioned on accumulated context, progressively sharp-
ening the system’s belief about which documents are relevant. The prior-likelihood frame-
work provides a natural vocabulary for reasoning about which factors should be resolved at
which stage and under what computational budget.


5.2     Towards a Neural Relevance Posterior
Relevance in information retrieval reflects the degree to which a document satisfies an infor-
mation need Saracevic [2007]. This quantity is not directly observed [Belkin, 1980, Mizzaro,
1997], but inferred from partial evidence about the query, the document, and the retrieval
context Robertson and Spärck Jones [1976]. In modern neural retrieval systems, this infer-
ence is operationalised as a single scalar score computed at query time for each candidate
document [Nogueira and Cho, 2019, Karpukhin et al., 2020], implicitly committing sys-
tems to resolving nearly all relevance-related uncertainty during query-document interaction
under strict latency constraints Dean [2009]. Yet some beliefs about documents are stable
across queries Azzopardi and Vinay [2008], Chang et al. [2024] and can be formed in ad-
vance and reused across interactions Peng et al. [2008].

Perspective. We treat retrieval as approximate inference over a relevance posterior. Un-
der this view, modern pipelines implement staged posterior refinement: (i) offline, query-
independent components form reusable document-side beliefs (prior terms) Robertson et al.
[1995], Peng et al. [2008], Azzopardi and Vinay [2008], and (ii) online, query-conditioned
components contribute evidence that resolves intent (likelihood-like interactions). This re-
finement is mediated by approximation operators that trade computation for posterior fidelity,
including candidate truncation Nogueira and Cho [2019], approximate nearest-neighbour re-
call Malkov and Yashunin [2020], Johnson et al. [2021], and bounded context budgets in
retrieval-augmented generation Lewis et al. [2020], Guu et al. [2020]. This lens clarifies
which features can be amortised offline, which must remain online, and how they should
be composed within deployment budgets. We illustrate the benefits of this formulation in
Figure 5.1.
    Classical probabilistic retrieval models explicitly distinguish between query-dependent
evidence and query-independent document effects, often via document priors Robertson
et al. [1995], Peng et al. [2008]. Importantly, alternative decompositions of relevance are
possible without altering the underlying ranking objective, leveraging both query-document
interactions and features of documents computed offline. The neural setting alters the prac-
tical character of this distinction in two related ways. First, powerful pretrained language
models can serve as offline document scorers, running once at index time and caching their
output for reuse across all queries at a cost that is amortised over the full query workload.
Whereas classical document priors were largely restricted to corpus statistics and link-graph
signals, learned neural priors can capture semantic quality, authority, and utility at a represen-
tational depth that heuristic signals cannot reach, while remaining strictly query-independent
CHAPTER 5. EFFECTIVENESS THROUGH DECOMPOSED STRUCTURE                                                                       95

                                         Dense Retrieval             Re-Ranking     RAG




                                         Likelihood                            Prior
                                      Query-Conditional                  Interaction-Free
                                      Document-Interactions              User / Topic Affinity
                                      Computed Online                    Computed Offline




                         Efficiency                     Effectiveness                       Control

Figure 5.1: An illustration of prior-likelihood decomposition and its downstream benefits
with respect to three pillars: Efficiency, Effectiveness and Control. Our empirical investiga-
tion targets both efficiency and effectiveness by reusing a static pruning model to improve
ranking precision.
CHAPTER 5. EFFECTIVENESS THROUGH DECOMPOSED STRUCTURE                                        96

by construction. Second, this offline capacity is rarely exploited in contemporary pipelines.
Instead, end-to-end gradient-based training tends to absorb stable document-side regularities
into the same parameters that resolve query intent, because both signals are present in rele-
vance supervision and neither is architecturally isolated Braga et al. [2025]. Feature-based
learning-to-rank exhibits a related entanglement, but the neural case is sharper in degree: rep-
resentations are shared across query and document sides, supervision is end-to-end, and the
resulting model cannot be decomposed into reusable offline and online components with-
out retraining. The combination of these factors, namely the availability of strong offline
scorers and the tendency of online models to recover document-side structure that need not
be re-estimated per query, motivates treating the prior-likelihood split as an explicit design
variable rather than an artefact of inference.
    Neural rankers do not model query-document interactions in isolation. In practice, they
jointly model query-specific signals and corpus-level regularities arising from training data
distributions, negative sampling procedures Karpukhin et al. [2020], and document prop-
erties that correlate with relevance across many queries such as readability or general util-
ity Zamani et al. [2018], Geva et al. [2021]. This phenomenon has been isolated, explicitly
showing that much of the optimisation effort within a neural model is in fact learning charac-
teristics of a relevant document a priori of a particular query Braga et al. [2025], even when
the optimisation objective does not explicitly consider a prior over documents. As a result,
evidence of relevance that can be captured offline is conflated with evidence that must be re-
solved at interaction time. Crucially, this entanglement incurs an efficiency cost: modelling
effort and computation are repeatedly expended at query time to recover structure that could
be learned once offline and reused across interactions.
    Query-independent document utility (quality, authority, or expected utility marginalised
over the query distribution Peng et al. [2008], Chang et al. [2024]) provides a concrete
instance of such offline modelling. Because these properties do not depend on a specific
query Peng et al. [2008], they can be learned and cached entirely offline. However, docu-
ment utility is only one instance of a broader class of priors that may also condition on users,
domains, temporal context, or system-level constraints.
    Within this perspective, our contributions are threefold. 1) We frame and advocate for
modern learned information access systems using an explicit prior-likelihood decomposi-
tion, providing a concrete language for reasoning about how relevance estimation is dis-
tributed across offline and online components. 2) We show empirically that learning a query-
independent prior offline can compensate for biases and limitations in initial retrieval with-
out increasing query-time cost. 3) We outline future research directions on how offline priors
and query-time models should be composed to balance effectiveness, efficiency, and control
under modern deployment constraints.
CHAPTER 5. EFFECTIVENESS THROUGH DECOMPOSED STRUCTURE                                        97

5.3     Neural Retrieval and Document Priors
Neural information retrieval has largely centred on query-conditional ranking. Whether
through cross-encoder re-ranking MacAvaney et al. [2019], Nogueira and Cho [2019], dual-
encoder retrieval Karpukhin et al. [2020], or learned sparse representations Formal et al.
[2021], a common pattern persists: a lightweight retriever generates candidates that are pro-
gressively refined by increasingly expensive interaction Pradeep et al. [2021]. Improvements
in effectiveness have coincided with increased interaction complexity Khattab and Zaharia
[2020] and higher serving cost, and while distillation reduces online burden Hofstätter et al.
[2020], Lin et al. [2020], re-ranker capacity continues to grow Pradeep et al. [2023], Parry
et al. [2025a]. Some architectures shift computation offline: bi-encoders precompute whole
or per-token document representations Karpukhin et al. [2020], Khattab and Zaharia [2020],
and document expansion generates surrogate queries offline Nogueira et al. [2019], but these
strategies target the likelihood (improving query-document matching) rather than leveraging
this offline computation to model document utility independently.

Learning to Rank. Learning-to-rank (LTR) methods combine heterogeneous document
features, including query-independent signals such as PageRank Page et al. [1999], doc-
ument length Robertson et al. [1995], and spam scores Cormack et al. [2011], within a
supervised framework Liu [2009]. LTR is therefore the most direct precedent for incor-
porating static document signals into ranking. However, LTR treats all features as inputs to
a single monolithic scorer trained end-to-end; the distinction between query-dependent and
query-independent evidence is not preserved in the learned model and cannot be exploited
at deployment time. Once trained, an LTR model requires all features at inference, and up-
dating a single signal (e.g., a freshness estimate) necessitates retraining or recalibrating the
full model. By contrast, the prior-likelihood decomposition maintains query-independent
signals as separable, reusable components that can be updated, audited, or replaced without
modifying the query-time model. The distinction is architectural rather than representa-
tional: both approaches can, in principle, access the same information, but the decompo-
sition exposes modularity that monolithic scoring obscures. Critically, this modularity is
operationally meaningful only in the neural setting: classical LTR feature pipelines required
all signals at serving time regardless, whereas a cached neural prior contributes to scoring at
zero marginal document-side cost.

Priors in Information Retrieval. Query-independent document signals have long played
a central role in large-scale search. PageRank Page et al. [1999] and HITS Kleinberg [1999]
established that link-graph authority, computed entirely offline, substantially improves re-
trieval when combined with content-based matching. Document quality estimation Chang
et al. [2024], spam detection Cormack et al. [2011], and readability scoring have similarly
been computed offline and incorporated as document priors Peng et al. [2008]. These signals
shape which documents are indexed, retrieved, or promoted, providing a reusable layer of
corpus-level modelling independent of individual queries.
CHAPTER 5. EFFECTIVENESS THROUGH DECOMPOSED STRUCTURE                                        98

    Recent work has revisited static document modelling in neural IR, particularly for static
corpus filtering Cormack et al. [2007], Chang et al. [2024]. Neural quality estimators can
remove large fractions of a corpus while preserving downstream effectiveness, reducing in-
dexing cost, storage, and latency. Crucially, these models run entirely offline and can lever-
age powerful architectures (including large language models) without affecting query-time
performance Chang et al. [2024].
    However, current practice largely treats static scores as binary gating decisions applied
during indexing Chang et al. [2024]. Once pruning is complete, the learned document signal
is discarded and does not participate in ranking. This creates a disconnect between offline
document modelling and online ranking, despite both targeting the same relevance objective.
A key distinction of our perspective is lifecycle and composition: rather than treating offline
scores as gates (index inclusion) or as additional features inside a monolithic ranker, we treat
them as first-class priors that persist throughout the pipeline.

Topic Models and Latent Structure. Latent variable models have long separated corpus-
level structure from query-specific evidence. Probabilistic latent semantic analysis Hofmann
[1999] and latent Dirichlet allocation Blei et al. [2003], Wei and Croft [2006], Ganguly et al.
[2013a] model documents as mixtures of topics estimated offline and reused across queries,
capturing corpus-wide regularities that would otherwise be rediscovered at query time. This
convention aligns with the view of retrieval as an interaction between an information need
and a latent information space Belkin [1980], in which queries are partial expressions of an
underlying need and latent variables capture structure not directly observable in the query.
Modern neural systems rarely use topic models directly but rely on the same principle: rele-
vance is mediated by latent corpus structure learnable offline. Neural representations absorb
this structure implicitly through pretraining, but the separation between corpus-level regular-
ities and query-specific evidence remains fundamental. Our work extends these notions by
arguing that systems can benefit from reintroducing explicit document-side components.

Retrieval-Augmented Generation. RAG systems Lewis et al. [2020] introduce a quali-
tatively different approximation boundary: rather than ranking over candidates, the system
selects evidence under a hard context budget. Recent work has shown that retrieval quality
directly governs generation quality Shi et al. [2023], and that LLM generators are sensi-
tive to document ordering, redundancy, and noise in the retrieved context Liu et al. [2024].
These findings suggest that document-level properties such as reliability, conciseness, and
answerability are primary factors affecting relevance in RAG Tian et al. [2025], yet are rarely
explicitly modelled. Under a prior-likelihood decomposition, such properties are naturally
represented as prior terms that can filter or reweight candidates before context construction
Chandra et al. [2026], reducing the burden on the generator to identify useful evidence from
noisy retrieval.
CHAPTER 5. EFFECTIVENESS THROUGH DECOMPOSED STRUCTURE                                        99

5.4     Bridging Probabilistic IR to Learned Systems
We first outline the connection between classical probabilistic term modelling and how the
formal language of those models can be helpful in reasoning over modern learned systems.

Classical Probabilistic Retrieval We consider ad-hoc retrieval over a corpus C . Classi-
cal probabilistic retrieval models rank documents (d) given a query (q) according to poste-
rior probability of relevance, following the Probability Ranking Principle (PRP) Robertson
[1977]. In language-model-based formulations, this is expressed through a query-likelihood
decomposition:
                                              p(q | d) p(d)
                                   p(d | q) =               ,                         (5.4)
                                                  p(q)
where the constant marginal p(q) = ∑d∈C p(q | d) need not be explicitly computed as it does
not affect the relative ordering of the query-conditional document estimates.
    In Equation 5.4, p(d) is a query-independent document prior and p(q | d) denotes a
query-dependent likelihood. Under a multinomial unigram language model with the term
independence assumption Hiemstra [2001], Ponte and Croft [1998], the query likelihood
factorises over query terms and is typically smoothed against the collection:

                     P(q | d) = ∏ P(t | d)                                                (5.5)
                                 t∈q

                              = ∏ (λ PMLE (t | d) + (1 − λ )PMLE (t | C ))                (5.6)
                                 t∈q
                                                         f (t, C )
                                                                  
                                     f (t, d)
                              =∏ λ            + (1 − λ )             ,                    (5.7)
                               t∈q   f (·, d)              f (·)

where (a) PMLE (t | d) and PMLE (t | C ) respectively denote the maximum likelihood esti-
mates of sampling t either from the document d or from the collection, (b) f (·, d) = |d| =
∑w∈d f (w, d) represents the document length and f (·) denotes the cardinality of the vocabu-
lary multiset (i.e. the collection size), and (c) λ controls the mixture weight on the document-
conditional sampling probability relative to the background collection model.
    Historically, this factorisation was rarely treated as a modelling decision to be optimised.
The likelihood received primary attention Robertson et al. [1995], while the prior was instan-
tiated through heuristics: document length normalisation Robertson et al. [1995], collection
statistics Ponte and Croft [1998], or uniformity assumptions Zhai and Lafferty [2004]. This
parallels latent-variable models in IR: probabilistic latent semantic analysis Hofmann [1999],
Ganguly et al. [2015] and latent Dirichlet allocation Blei et al. [2003], Wei and Croft [2006],
Ganguly et al. [2013a,b], where corpus-level structure is estimated offline and reused across
queries. The separation between corpus-level regularities and query-specific evidence has al-
ways been present; what remained unexplored was how these components should be learned,
composed, and reused.
    Modern neural IR systems collapse this decomposition into a single query-conditioned
scoring function. Whether implemented as bi-encoder similarity, cross-encoder re-ranking,
CHAPTER 5. EFFECTIVENESS THROUGH DECOMPOSED STRUCTURE                                        100

or learned sparse retrieval, contemporary models absorb corpus-level effects into parameters
through training data, negative sampling, and optimisation dynamics. The document prior
has not disappeared; it has been marginalised into the learned representation space, thereby
implying a change in inference strategy rather than in the learning objective. This marginali-
sation is a consequence of joint optimisation: because query-independent and query-dependent
signals are both present in relevance supervision, gradient flow entangles them within shared
parameters in a way that classical term-weighting models, which never shared parameters
across these two roles, did not. The practical implication is that structure which could be
estimated once offline must instead be recovered at query time, repeatedly, across every in-
teraction.

Conditioning as a Design Variable Conditioning choices determine not only which infor-
mation sources are used but when they enter inference. Writing relevance as a marginalisa-
tion over latent factors z (corpus structure Robertson and Spärck Jones [1976], user popula-
tions He et al. [2017], task constraints or similar semantics Ganguly et al. [2015]),

                               p(d | q) = ∑ p(d | q, z) p(z | q),                          (5.8)
                                             z

practical systems must choose a subset x ⊂ z to condition on explicitly, marginalising the
rest. Conditioning on x yields

                               p(d | q, x) ∝ p(q | d, x) p(d | x),                         (5.9)

committing the system to resolving x at inference time. Each commitment carries a cost.
The central design question is therefore which variables to retain at each pipeline stage
given a fixed computational budget. Variables whose influence on document ordering is sta-
ble across queries (quality, authority, domain suitability) are natural candidates for offline
resolution via priors; variables whose influence is query-specific (topical match, facet selec-
tion) must remain online. In neural systems this distinction is particularly consequential: a
bi-encoder document embedding, for instance, already amortises document-side computa-
tion offline, but the training objective entangles prior-like corpus utility with likelihood-like
query-predictive signal within the same representation, preventing either from being inde-
pendently updated, audited, or reused across pipelines.
    Conditioning on a variable x that is not document-dependent preserves the ranking be-
cause:
                     if x ⊥⊥ d | q, arg max p(d | q) = arg max p(d | q, x).                (5.10)
                                         d                    d
This holds whenever x is a property of the query, session, or retrieval context that does not
induce a document-dependent score transformation (e.g., latency budget, user locale, task
type). By contrast, conditioning on a document-dependent variable does change the ranking,
precisely the mechanism exploited by priors.
CHAPTER 5. EFFECTIVENESS THROUGH DECOMPOSED STRUCTURE                                         101

Priors as Amortised Inference Amortised inference refers to the strategy of performing
expensive computation once offline and reusing the result across many future instances,
rather than repeating the computation each time. PageRank Page et al. [1999], which es-
timates document authority once and reuses it across all queries, is a canonical example
of amortised inference in retrieval. The neural setting extends this principle qualitatively:
whereas PageRank is a graph statistic, a neural quality estimator can capture semantic utility,
structural completeness, and domain reliability at depth that heuristic signals cannot reach,
while remaining strictly query-independent by construction and cacheable at index time.
      Document priors encode structure that is invariant across queries, estimated once offline
and reused at query time across all query-document scoring interactions. The decomposition
in Equation 5.4 thus trades offline computation for reduced query-time cost, reframing priors
not as heuristic components but as reusable outputs of amortised inference, quantities whose
estimation has been paid for in advance.
      In multi-stage retrieval systems, marginalisation is temporary. Early stages aggressively
marginalise relevance factors under tight latency budgets. For example, a first-stage retriever
such as BM25 or a bi-encoder effectively marginalises over user intent, document quality,
and contextual factors, scoring documents using only surface lexical or semantic match. As
the candidate set shrinks, later stages can afford to condition on richer evidence variables
x1 , . . . , xk , such as cross-attention features between query and document tokens, user interac-
tion history, or document quality signals, progressively refining the relevance estimate:

                      p(0) (d) → p(1) (d | q, x1 ) → · · · → p(k) (d | q, x1:k ).

   More precisely, at each stage i a new evidence factor ℓi (d; q) refines the current belief:

                              p(i) (d | q) ∝ p(i−1) (d | q) ℓi (d; q),                      (5.11)

where each ℓi may be query-dependent (e.g. a cross-encoder re-ranking score) or query-
independent (e.g. a document quality or freshness filter). This staged update is analogous
to message-passing in factor graphs Kschischang et al. [2001]: just as each variable node in
a factor graph aggregates messages from its neighbouring factors, here each retrieval stage
contributes a local scoring factor, and the final ranking emerges from their product: the
MAP configuration of the approximate posterior. The decomposition makes the order and
granularity of evidence introduction an explicit design variable.
    A similar pattern appears in agentic retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems Li
et al. [2025], Jin et al. [2025], where an agent retrieves an initial set of documents, reasons
over them, and then issues refined queries to retrieve again. Each retrieval-reasoning cycle
can be understood as introducing a new evidence factor ℓi conditioned on the context ac-
cumulated so far, progressively sharpening the system’s belief about which documents are
relevant.
    Over-aggressive marginalisation of stable factors introduces systematic inefficiency. When
query-invariant structure is not retained, query-time models must repeatedly recover it. A
CHAPTER 5. EFFECTIVENESS THROUGH DECOMPOSED STRUCTURE                                      102

neural ranker trained end-to-end, for instance, must implicitly learn to account for document
length, authority, and recency (factors that could have been estimated once and supplied as
priors), wasting model capacity on recovering structure that is stable across queries. For-
mally, such a model implicitly approximates

                              p(q | d; θ ) ≈ ∑ p(q | d, x) p(x | d)                     (5.12)
                                             x

within a single estimator. This entanglement obscures sources of variation, wastes computa-
tion on invariant structure, and reduces reusability across systems and tasks.
    Retrieval systems are therefore more naturally understood as inference procedures, dif-
fering not in objective but in which variables are retained, when they are introduced, and
under what constraints they are resolved.


5.5     Systems as Approximate Inference
Under the likelihood-prior framework introduced earlier, query-document interaction corre-
sponds to the likelihood term: the component of relevance that cannot be estimated without
observing the query Robertson et al. [1995], Lafferty and Zhai [2001]. Many factors that
influence relevance, such as general document quality, topical coverage, or retrievability, can
be marginalised over queries and estimated offline Azzopardi and Vinay [2008], Chang et al.
[2024]. Query-document interaction therefore resolves residual uncertainty, the distinctions
that depend on the specific information need being expressed. This framing grounds inter-
action in theory rather than architecture, linking it directly to what remains unresolved after
offline document modelling.

Query-Conditional Models In principle, query-conditional models are well-suited to re-
solving intent: determining which facets of information are relevant for a given query and
how document evidence should be interpreted in that context Belkin [1980]. In practice,
however, contemporary query models often serve a broader role, implicitly compensating
for weak or missing document-side signals. From a decomposition perspective, intent res-
olution can be seen as the idealised role of query interaction once document utility is well
estimated offline. We make this systems-level interpretation explicit via a compact taxonomy
(Table 5.1), which summarises where different pipelines encode query-independent beliefs,
how they apply query-conditioned evidence, and which approximation operator (e.g., trun-
cation or budgeting) ultimately constrains the reachable posterior mass.
    Table 5.1 shows typical instantiations: (i) Lexical retrieval uses index statistics as pri-
ors, applies sparse matching as likelihood-like evidence, constrained by top-k truncation; (ii)
Dense retrieval amortises document encoding as a prior-like representation, applies query-
document similarity as evidence, constrained by ANN recall; (iii) Multi-stage pipelines com-
pose a cheap first-stage prior with a heavier re-ranker likelihood over a restricted candidate
set; (iv) RAG introduces an explicit context-budget operator that truncates which evidence
CHAPTER 5. EFFECTIVENESS THROUGH DECOMPOSED STRUCTURE                                      103

can be presented to the generator. These instantiations motivate analysing retrieval failures
as support restriction failures: relevant documents may not be surfaced before expensive
interaction can act.

Interaction over Structured Spaces Query-document interaction rarely occurs over the
full corpus. Instead, it is almost always applied to a candidate set produced by earlier re-
trieval stages Cambazoglu and Baeza-Yates [2016]. As a result, interaction operates over a
document space already structured by retrieval heuristics, representations, or prior rankings.
This structure may be weak, as in BM25-based retrieval where only limited prior assump-
tions such as length normalisation are enforced Robertson et al. [1995], but it nonetheless
constrains which documents interaction can affect. Viewing interaction as operating over a
structured space helps explain why retrieval pipelines with very different architectures can
exhibit similar behaviour. In the taxonomy (Table 5.1), this appears as an explicit approxima-
tion operator: early-stage retrieval defines the support on which later likelihood refinement
can act.

The Purpose of Interaction Across retrieval systems, query-document interaction func-
tions to filter candidate documents, reweight their relative importance, or gate access to
downstream processing Wang et al. [2011], Cambazoglu and Baeza-Yates [2016]. These
three roles correspond to common inference patterns: filtering instantiates support restric-
tion, reweighting instantiates score-level posterior reshaping on a fixed support, and gating
instantiates budgeted access to subsequent computation. Interaction thus selects among pre-
existing document distinctions rather than constructing relevance from scratch. This per-
spective helps explain why relatively simple fusion methods or shallow interaction models
can perform competitively when combined with strong document-side signals Cormack et al.
[2009].

Beyond Ranking Ranking is only one stage within a broader retrieval pipeline that may
include readers, generators, or iterative retrieval components Lewis et al. [2020], Pradeep
et al. [2021]. The taxonomy (Table 5.1) highlights that this shift changes the approximation
boundary, from ranking over candidates to selecting evidence under a hard context budget. In
such regimes, query-independent signals (e.g., trust, redundancy, or generic utility) become
first-class because they determine which evidence remains available to downstream reason-
ing under truncation. In retrieval-augmented generation systems, for example, ranking may
primarily serve to surface plausible contexts rather than to fully resolve relevance Shi et al.
[2023], Liu et al. [2024]. The division of labour between ranking and downstream processing
thus becomes an explicit design choice.

Queries as Constraints Queries can be interpreted as imposing constraints over a pre-
computed document space rather than generating relevance independently. Under this view,
query-document interaction restricts attention to documents whose attributes, content, or
CHAPTER 5. EFFECTIVENESS THROUGH DECOMPOSED STRUCTURE                                      104

Table 5.1: Systems as approximate inference, expressed per regime as prior, likelihood, and
approximation operator.

      Regime      Posterior view (prior / likelihood / approximation)
      Lexical     Prior: index statistics, normalisation.
                  Likelihood: lexical evidence aggregation.
                  Approx: model-form bias over full-corpus scoring.
      Dense       Prior: cached embeddings (implicit).
                  Likelihood: vector similarity (amortised).
                  Approx: ANN search; collapsed prior×likelihood.
      Multi-stage Prior: early-stage scores / cached signals.
                  Likelihood: expensive interaction / re-ranking.
                  Approx: candidate truncation; staged refinement.
      RAG         Prior: cached utilities, trust signals.
                  Likelihood: retrieval as evidence proposal.
                  Approx: context-budget truncation; evidence selection.


utility align with the expressed constraints Belkin [1980], Ingwersen [1996]. This interpre-
tation aligns with settings in which queries are short or underspecified, yet retrieval remains
effective.
    As offline document modelling becomes richer, more aspects of relevance can be re-
solved before query time Chang et al. [2024], Nogueira et al. [2019]. This does not eliminate
the need for query-document interaction, but it changes its role and emphasis. Interaction
may shift toward selecting, combining, or constraining pre-computed signals. This redis-
tribution has implications for where learning effort is invested within a retrieval system. In
taxonomy terms (Table 5.1), strengthening offline modelling shifts systems along the prior
axis and can convert unconstrained interaction into constrained operators such as selection,
fusion, and budgeted refinement.


5.6      Preliminary Findings
In this section, we provide evidence towards answering the question: “Which effectiveness
gains are achievable through document-side evidence, versus requiring query-conditioned
interaction?”, the organising question that Section 5.7 takes up at the level of implications.


5.6.1     Rank Fusion with a Learned Prior
We define a query-independent document utility uφ (d) ∈ R, interpreted as a log prior poten-
tial,
                                 uφ (d) ≈ log p̃φ (d),                                (5.13)

where p̃φ (d) is an unnormalised prior over documents. Utility is computed offline and
cached for all d ∈ D , enabling reuse with minimal additional document-side inference at
query time (only the deployed scorer sθ runs online with the addition of prior scores).
CHAPTER 5. EFFECTIVENESS THROUGH DECOMPOSED STRUCTURE                                       105

Fusion in Log-Space Motivated by Eq. (5.4), we combine the query-conditioned score
and static utility via a fused potential
                                                       
                          S(q, d) = F sθ (q, d), uφ (d) ,      d ∈ Cq ,                  (5.14)

chosen so that S is an unnormalised log-posterior up to a query-dependent constant. We
adopt additive fusion,

                         Sadd (q, d) = sθ (q, d) + γ uφ (d),     γ ≥ 0,                  (5.15)

which recovers the log-posterior of Eq. (5.4) when sθ is calibrated as a log-likelihood and
uφ as a log-prior, and more generally defines a log-linear combination of potentials whose
ranking depends only on γ and the relative scale of the two scores.
    Although additive score combination is well studied in learning-to-rank Shaw and Fox
[1994], Liu [2009], our setting differs in two respects. First, uφ (d) and sθ (q, d) are derived
from the same modality yet are functionally orthogonal: the utility model sees strictly less
input than the relevance scorer, so gains cannot stem from complementary data access but
instead reflect document-level regularities that sθ can represent in principle but struggles
to disentangle from query-dependent signal during joint optimisation. Second, the prior is
computed offline and cached, contributing zero marginal query-time cost and can be updated,
audited, or replaced independently of the deployed model.


5.6.2    Experimental Setup
Datasets We assess effectiveness on the TREC Deep Learning 2019 [Craswell et al., 2019]
and 2020 [Craswell et al., 2020] test collections, retrieving from the MSMARCO passage
collection [Nguyen et al., 2016]. All reported test collections apply normalised discounted
cumulative gain (nDCG) [Järvelin and Kekäläinen, 2002] as their primary measure, and we
report this value at a rank cutoff of 10 as is standard.

Inference Setting Our core setting fuses a static utility model uφ (d) to complement an
existing dynamic ranker sθ (q, d). In this initial investigation, θ and φ are independent (no
awareness of each other or respective score modes). This ensures that improvements reflect
complementary utility estimation rather than changes to the dynamic ranker. In this study we
deliberately operate in an uncalibrated composition setting: we do not learn fusion weights
or perform score calibration between sθ and uφ , and we fix the fusion parameter (γ = 1.0) to
isolate whether the offline utility signal is complementary in principle. This choice also pre-
serves the intended deployment constraint: uφ (d) is a cache lookup and does not introduce
any additional document-side model forward pass at query time beyond the already-deployed
scorer producing sθ . For simplicity, we perform fusion on the top-1000 documents retrieved
by the retriever, rather than combining retrieval results with the full-corpus prior ranking,
while still re-ranking only the top-100 for parity with baseline approaches.
CHAPTER 5. EFFECTIVENESS THROUGH DECOMPOSED STRUCTURE                                                106

Quality Estimators. We experiment with fine-tuned models trained by Chang et al. [2024].
All checkpoints are based on the T5 encoder-decoder architecture Raffel et al. [2020]. These
models are trained as quality classifiers using binary cross-entropy over human-annotated
relevant texts from the MSMARCO training corpus Nguyen et al. [2016] and hard negatives
drawn uniformly from the top-k documents retrieved by BM25 for each training query. Func-
tionally this training is identical to that of monoT5 Nogueira et al. [2020] but without pro-
viding the query within the input context of the model. These models output log-likelihoods
of a document being of high quality. We run inference for all models on a single NVIDIA
4090 GPU with 24GB VRAM. This places reproducibility of our work within the hard-
ware constraints of most academic labs. All experiments are carried out using the PyTerrier
framework Macdonald and Tonellotto [2020] and its associated plug-ins. The codebase for
this work is released to support reproduction and to foster further research.

Retrieval Models We employ two sparse first-stage retrieval models to generate candidate
document sets for downstream scoring and analysis.

• BM25 Robertson et al. [1995]: A classical probabilistic term-weighting model that re-
  mains ubiquitous in modern search systems due to its simplicity, efficiency, and strong
  empirical performance. BM25 ranks documents by combining term frequency saturation
  and inverse document frequency, providing a strong lexical baseline. We use Robust04-
  tuned parameters (default in our chosen framework) being k1 = 1.2, b = 0.75, k3 = 8.

• SPLADE Formal et al. [2021]: A neural sparse retrieval model based on a BERT-style
  encoder that learns to expand and reweight query and document representations in the
  lexical space2 .

Re-Ranking Models We re-rank our combined static/dynamic retrieval systems with two
state-of-the-art re-rankers. In all cases, we re-rank the top 100 documents returned by a
standalone or utility-fused retriever.

• MonoELECTRA (CE) Schlatt et al. [2025]: An ELECTRA-based Clark et al. [2020]
  point-wise cross-encoder trained using distillation from a larger list-wise ranking model.

• RankZephyr (LLM) Pradeep et al. [2023]: A Zephyr-based Tunstall et al. [2023] list-
  wise cross-encoder trained using distillation from RankGPT Sun et al. [2023]. We follow
  prior art, applying a window size of 20 documents with a stride of 10 Pradeep et al. [2023],
  Parry et al. [2024b].

the quality estimator’s capacity, though this is primarily limited to QualT5-tiny, generally
underperforming relative to larger variants. Under SPLADE, fusion consistently degrades
first-stage effectiveness, suggesting that the injected utility term is not complementary at
the retrieval stage and may partially double-count signals already expressed by SPLADE’s
learned sparse expansion. Notably, this does not preclude gains after re-ranking: improve-
ments can still arise when downstream cross-encoders or LLM re-rankers correct first-stage
misorderings under restricted depth. This further motivates the trade-off between online
and offline model capacity. We additionally note that in all but one re-ranking settings, fu-
sion outperforms index pruning suggesting that priors can surface more valuable documents
within the top-k beyond reducing the ranking of low quality texts (similar to pruning).
     Some gains are observed with cross-encoder re-ranking, but larger gains are observed
with the LLM re-ranker, which exhibits positional bias Schlatt et al. [2024], Parry et al.
[2024b]. This point holds under both first-stages. We consider that a retriever may provide
an out-of-distribution permutation for an LLM re-ranker and that in adding quality signals
orthogonal to the retriever we may provide a better ordering for the LLM. The same ob-
servation applies in retrieval-augmented generation, where a generator must select context
in the manner of a list-wise ranker; the direct optimisation of a prior to re-order candidates
conditional on downstream RAG effectiveness, without observing the query, is taken up as a
thesis-level direction in Section 8.2.3.
     Considering deeper measures, under BM25, fusion improves both AP and R@100 as
shown in Figure 5.2, indicating that the prior promotes relevant documents into the re-
ranking window that lexical matching alone misses. Under SPLADE, both measures de-
grade marginally at the first stage, consistent with signal overlap. Crucially, fusion preserves
R@1000 (the candidate pool is reordered, not reduced), whereas pruning removes docu-
ments and reduces R@1000 by 3-9 absolute points. After re-ranking, AP gains are also
present under SPLADE: RankZephyr on DL-2020 improves AP by approximately 0.03 de-
spite first-stage degradation, suggesting that the prior produces candidate orderings which
