Figure 5.3: An illustration of how posterior inference of p(d|q) allows us to connect not only
different aspects of information access but also express ad-hoc pipelines as formal objects.
Re-ranking is seen as an iterative refinement of likelihood, here expressed as taking the
outcome of some first stage, which governs our second stage.


are favourable under the LLM ranker. Crucially, these models are not calibrated to a partic-
ular likelihood; this initial evidence points towards the potential for these priors to be used
not only for efficiency through pruning but to concretely provide new signals in ranking to
improve effectiveness. The appeal of the uncalibrated setting is that we can be sure that such
a prior can still be used for pruning, an interesting problem remains in optimising a prior to
serve both purposes.

Qualitative Differences In investigating rankings produced by re-ranking pipelines with
and without QualT5 fusion, we observe a general trend: adding the prior largely improves
navigational queries. Intents associated with definitions, for instance, biographical queries
or definitions of words, or exact quantifications, such as the cost of an entity or when an
event occurred, tended to improve under both monoELECTRA and RankZephyr re-ranking.
A plausible explanation is that a query-independent quality prior disproportionately helps
intents where relevance is strongly mediated by document-side “usability” (e.g., concise def-
initional pages, canonical entity descriptions, or well-formed fact statements), because these
properties are stable across queries and therefore well matched to offline estimation. By
contrast, open-ended exploratory queries often require query-specific evidence aggregation
across multiple plausible facets; in these cases, injecting a global utility term can over-prefer
generally well-written or authoritative documents that are only weakly aligned with the par-
ticular causal or explanatory frame implied by the query, yielding small degradations that
downstream interaction must correct.
CHAPTER 5. EFFECTIVENESS THROUGH DECOMPOSED STRUCTURE                                         109

5.7     Implications and Applications
Framing retrieval as approximate posterior inference turns several “systems” questions into
modelling questions: (i) which computation should be amortised offline, (ii) which must
remain online to resolve intent, and (iii) how should multiple sources of belief be composed.
Figure 5.3 illustrates this perspective: retrieval regimes differ not in objective but in which
variables (d, q, u, z) are retained or marginalised, and in how prior and likelihood components
are composed. We organise implications around three roles that priors play in a pipeline:
efficiency, effectiveness, and control. Finally we make a broader connection to recommender
systems as hybrid information access becomes more prevalent.


5.7.1    Priors for Efficiency
Under the posterior view, efficiency is a consequence of shaping the posterior support so that
expensive online inference is applied to fewer candidates.

Index-side filtering. The prior can reduce the candidate pool before any interaction, either
by permanently removing low-utility documents (static pruning) or by dynamically gating el-
igibility for later stages. This is not an auxiliary heuristic: it is an approximation to posterior
inference in which documents with low prior mass are excluded before evaluating the likeli-
hood. First-stage retrieval and re-ranking depth therefore become joint design variables: an
informative u(d) permits reducing k while preserving posterior mass on relevant documents.

Context-side filtering. When the downstream component is an LLM, the dominant cost
shifts to context construction and generation. The same prior mechanism acts as a context
filter, determining which passages are admissible evidence and how much evidence is nec-
essary, by preferring documents whose stable properties (reliability, coherence, coverage)
make them useful across many queries.

Trading prior strength for online capacity. If priors absorb stable document-side struc-
ture, the online model can be smaller while maintaining comparable posterior quality. Con-
versely, given a fixed online model, stronger priors can reduce re-ranking depth or context
budget. This makes explicit a design trade-off usually treated implicitly through engineering
constraints.


5.7.2    Priors for Effectiveness
The decomposition clarifies two distinct mechanisms. The first is additive: priors inject
stable evidence that the likelihood is not expected to infer. The second is corrective: priors
compensate systematic error induced by the deployed likelihood.
CHAPTER 5. EFFECTIVENESS THROUGH DECOMPOSED STRUCTURE                                        110

Adding stable evidence. Document priors can encode properties that are query-invariant
and difficult to infer from interaction alone: source reliability, structural completeness, writ-
ing quality, or domain compliance. Under the posterior view, these are not re-ranking tricks
but explicit factors of posterior belief. This becomes salient in task-augmented settings where
documents must be not only topically relevant but usable as evidence (e.g., answerability, ex-
tractability, citation-worthiness), enabling the likelihood to focus on intent resolution.

Correcting systematic error. End-to-end rankers absorb biases from supervision, negative
sampling, and candidate generation; term-weighting models are restricted to exact matching.
Many of these biases are stable across queries (popularity effects, domain over-exposure,
annotation artefacts) and appear as persistent residual error in sθ (q, d). Training a prior
against a frozen likelihood provides a correction term that re-centres posterior scores where
the likelihood is consistently miscalibrated, without modifying the online model. This de-
composition also supports clearer attribution: query-side failures (intent, ambiguity) and
document-side failures (quality, trust, distributional skew) can be diagnosed independently
rather than conflated inside a monolithic scorer.


5.7.3    Priors as Control Surfaces
Making priors explicit exposes controllable degrees of freedom typically entangled within
end-to-end training. Control corresponds to adjusting prior terms while holding the likeli-
hood fixed, changing posterior behaviour without changing query understanding.

Global control. Safety, trustworthiness, diversity, and exposure policies are commonly
introduced via manual boosts or filters. Under the posterior framing, these are prior terms
that can be learned, calibrated, and audited independently of the likelihood. Policy updates
can be deployed by modifying priors alone, and audits can target the prior directly.

User control. Personalisation fits naturally as a user-conditioned prior p(d | u) that in-
teracts with sθ (q, d), matching the recommender-systems view of user-item affinity while
preserving a distinct role for the likelihood to resolve intent. The key question is whether
personalisation can be controlled primarily through priors, leaving the likelihood as a generic
intent model.

Composing multiple priors.       A single posterior can combine modular utilities,

                           log p(d | q) ∝ sθ (q, d) + ∑ λm um (d),                        (5.16)
                                                        m

where each um encodes a distinct belief (quality, trust, freshness, user affinity). Because these
sources differ in supervision, stability, and update frequency, they are difficult to express in
a single end-to-end objective but natural to maintain independently as priors composed with
a shared likelihood. Improvements to any um propagate across all pipelines sharing a corpus
CHAPTER 5. EFFECTIVENESS THROUGH DECOMPOSED STRUCTURE                                        111

without retraining the online model, directly reducing the coupling that makes end-to-end
system iteration brittle.


5.7.4    Connecting to Broader Information Access
Conditioning priors on richer context, including users, domains, tasks, or temporal signals,
extends the control surface beyond global document properties. Each additional conditioning
variable introduces a separable degree of freedom that can be tuned, audited, or removed
without modifying the query-time likelihood. This generalisation connects the posterior
view to settings beyond ad-hoc retrieval, most directly to recommender systems.

Connection to Recommender Systems. The prior-likelihood decomposition bridges ad-
hoc retrieval and recommendation, two settings that share a common inferential structure
despite being treated as distinct research areas. As Figure 5.3 illustrates, the key difference is
which variables are retained: ad-hoc IR conditions on (d, q), recommendation on (d, u), and
hybrid systems on (d, q, u) with static ranking as the limiting case where only d is modelled.
The latent variable z mediates between these regimes, representing corpus structure that may
be marginalised or explicitly resolved depending on the system.
    In collaborative filtering, a user-item score is decomposed as r̂(u, i) = f (pu , qi ), where
qi encodes item properties and pu encodes user preferences, learned jointly from interaction
histories Koren et al. [2009], He et al. [2017]. Under our framework, qi plays the role of a
user-marginalised item prior while pu conditions this prior on a specific user, analogous to
how a query conditions a document prior on a specific need. Both settings face the same
allocation problem: which aspects of the score can be precomputed from one side (docu-
ments/items) and which require observing the other (queries/users) at inference time.
    This becomes concrete in hybrid IR/recommendation settings (news recommendation,
e-commerce search, conversational assistants) that combine queries with user profiles. Per-
sonalised retrieval systems leverage persistent user profiles, including interaction history,
topical preferences, and reading level, as priors that modulate relevance estimates Salemi
et al. [2024], Pasi et al. [2018], Bassani et al. [2024]. Formally, such systems admit a three-
way factorisation,
                               p(d | q, u) ∝ p(q | d) p(d | u) p(d),                        (5.17)

where each term operates on a different timescale: corpus updates for p(d), user history
evolution for p(d | u), and per-request intent for p(q | d). Only the query likelihood must be
evaluated under strict latency constraints. The decomposition also unifies both communities’
approaches to cold-start: in recommendation (new users/items lacking history) and in re-
trieval (underspecified queries or novel documents), the remedy amounts to increasing prior
weight when the likelihood signal is weak. Exposure and propensity constraints, routine in
recommendation, are naturally expressible as prior adjustments in retrieval, counteracting
popularity bias without modifying the query-conditional ranker.
CHAPTER 5. EFFECTIVENESS THROUGH DECOMPOSED STRUCTURE                                        112

5.8     Conclusions
The results presented in this chapter demonstrate that the prior-likelihood separation, far
from being an obsolete artefact of classical probabilistic retrieval, provides a practical mech-
anism for improving neural ranking effectiveness. A query-independent document-quality
prior, computed entirely offline and fused with existing retrieval and re-ranking pipelines
through uncalibrated additive interpolation, yielded significant improvements in first-stage
retrieval under BM25 (up to ∆nDCG@10 ≈ 0.046) and in downstream re-ranking under both
cross-encoder and LLM-based re-rankers (up to ∆nDCG@10 ≈ 0.054). The prior introduced
zero additional query-time neural inference, requiring only a score lookup per document.
     Several patterns in the results merit emphasis. First, gains were larger under LLM-based
list-wise re-ranking (RankZephyr) than under cross-encoder re-ranking (monoELECTRA),
suggesting that the prior’s principal contribution is in correcting the initial ordering presented
to the re-ranker, an ordering that, without the prior, may be out-of-distribution for a list-wise
model that is sensitive to positional cues. Second, the prior disproportionately improved nav-
igational and definitional queries, where relevance is strongly mediated by document-side
usability (concise definitions, canonical entity descriptions, well-formed factual statements),
properties that are stable across queries and therefore well matched to offline estimation.
Third, fusion consistently outperformed index pruning, indicating that the prior’s value lies
not merely in removing low-quality documents from the candidate pool but in actively sur-
facing higher-quality documents within the re-ranking window. These gains came from a
prior that was trained independently of the dynamic ranker, composed without calibration,
and evaluated without any modification to the deployed pipeline.

Verdict on H2. H2 is supported for lexical first-stage retrieval and for downstream re-
ranking, with one informative exception. An offline document-quality prior, learned inde-
pendently of the deployed scorer and composed by uncalibrated additive fusion, improved
BM25 first-stage retrieval and both cross-encoder and LLM-based re-ranking at zero addi-
tional query-time neural inference. The exception is the learned sparse retriever SPLADE,
where additive fusion degraded first-stage effectiveness, consistent with the prior double-
counting document-side structure that the sparse expansion already encodes. The verdict
is therefore that an uncalibrated prior is complementary in principle wherever the likeli-
hood does not already represent document-side quality, and that the SPLADE case marks
the boundary of the uncalibrated regime rather than a failure of the decomposition; the cali-
brated formulation that addresses it directly is developed in Section 8.2.1.
    The prior demonstrates that an explicit, separable gφ (d) carries effectiveness gains that
a monolithic scorer leaves on the table. The same separability has a darker corollary. A
scorer that has absorbed document-side structure into its parameters, rather than holding it
in an inspectable component, has no internal basis for distinguishing genuine quality from
manipulated content. The next chapter turns to this vulnerability: the same entanglement is
not only an effectiveness liability but an exploitable attack surface.

Chapter 6

Adversarial Vulnerability Through
Entangled Scoring

   Hypothesis H3.1 (tested in this chapter)
   The entanglement of query-dependent and query-independent evidence in neural rank-
   ing models creates exploitable vulnerabilities: sequence-to-sequence models are sus-
   ceptible to query-independent adversarial attacks, including keyword stuffing and
   LLM-based document rewriting, that require no knowledge of the target query and
   transfer across model scales.

    The preceding chapters established that the neural ranking pipeline is both expensive and
fragile from within: evaluation cannot reliably discriminate between systems at fine margins
(Chapter 3), and the elaborate training cascades designed to achieve those margins are largely
redundant under distillation (Chapter 4). Chapter 5 then showed that the entanglement of
query-independent structure within a monolithic scorer, rather than holding it in a separable
prior gφ (d), is an effectiveness liability. This chapter shows that the same entanglement is
a security liability. Those earlier findings concerned the internal validity of neural ranking:
whether the field can measure what it claims to measure and whether it is spending wisely
to achieve it. This chapter and the next turn to external validity: whether the systems we
build are trustworthy when deployed in adversarial environments where content providers
have incentives to manipulate rankings.
    The central claim of these two chapters is causal, not merely empirical: the entanglement
of query-dependent and query-independent evidence within a monolithic scorer is a struc-
tural cause of the vulnerabilities we demonstrate. When a model cannot distinguish between
a high relevance score that arises from genuine query-document matching and one that arises
from query-independent document manipulation, it has no internal basis for rejecting the ma-
nipulation. Classical term-weighting models were also susceptible to manipulation, but their
failure modes were analytically tractable: the sub-linear term frequency saturation of BM25
bounds the gain from keyword repetition, and length normalisation ensures that injected to-
kens dilute the overall score. The scoring function is a sum of per-term contributions whose
individual effects are inspectable and whose aggregate behaviour can be predicted from the

                                             114
CHAPTER 6. ADVERSARIAL VULNERABILITY                                                       115

parameters. Neural scoring functions do not afford this transparency, and it is the absence of
separable structure, not any particular model architecture, that creates the vulnerability.


Threat Model
The attacks investigated in this chapter and the next share a common threat model charac-
terised by the following assumptions about attacker knowledge and capability:

    • Attacker knowledge. The attacker knows the model family (e.g., sequence-to-sequence,
      cross-encoder) and, where applicable, the prompt template used by the relevance model.
      The attacker does not have access to model weights, gradients, or any surrogate model.
      The attacker does not know the target query or query distribution.

    • Attacker control. The attacker controls the text content of documents prior to index-
      ing. The attacker can prepend, append, insert, or rewrite document text. The attacker
      cannot modify the query, the retrieval index structure, or the evaluation infrastructure.

    • Perturbation budget. Attacks range from minimal (insertion of a small number of
      tokens) to substantial (LLM-based rewriting of the full document or generation of
      contextualised promotional passages). We report attack effectiveness as a function of
      perturbation magnitude where applicable.

    • Attack objective. The attacker seeks to increase the rank or relevance score of a target
      document across all or most queries (query-independent promotion), rather than for a
      specific query.

    • Target models. We evaluate attacks on sequence-to-sequence models (monoT5 at
      multiple scales), cross-encoders (monoELECTRA), bi-encoders (ColBERT), and lex-
      ical baselines (BM25). Transfer across model families is assessed explicitly.

    This threat model is deliberately minimal: it assumes less attacker capability than gradient-
based or surrogate-based attacks [Song et al., 2020, Liu et al., 2022, Wu et al., 2023b], which
makes the demonstrated vulnerabilities more concerning rather than less. An attacker who
can succeed with no query knowledge, no model access, and no gradient computation poses a
realistic threat in web search, retrieval-augmented generation, and enterprise search settings
where document providers are untrusted.
    Section 6.1 situates the work against search-engine manipulation, prior gradient- and
surrogate-based attacks, prompt injection in sequence-to-sequence relevance models, and
LLM-based document rewriting. The body of the chapter then presents the keyword-stuffing
and rewriting attacks, their transfer across model families and scales, and an oracle bound
on retrieval effectiveness under attack, before closing with the verdict on H3.1.
CHAPTER 6. ADVERSARIAL VULNERABILITY                                                         116

6.1     Related Work
This section reviews the adversarial landscape: the history of search engine manipulation
(Section 6.1.1), prior adversarial attacks on neural ranking models (Section 6.1.2), the spe-
cific vulnerability of sequence-to-sequence relevance models to prompt injection (Section 6.1.3),
and LLM-based document manipulation (Section 6.1.4).


6.1.1    Search Engine Optimisation and Web Spam
Search engine optimisation (SEO) encompasses techniques used by content providers to in-
crease the visibility of their pages in search results. Web search referrals are among the most
important methods of generating traffic to web pages [Brin and Page, 1998], creating strong
economic incentives for manipulation. Good-faith SEO, structuring content clearly, using
descriptive titles, providing metadata, can make useful content more accessible to users.
Malicious SEO exploits the same mechanisms to promote spam or misleading content.
    Classical SEO attacks target the features that lexical ranking models use. Keyword stuff-
ing adds invisible or repeated terms to a page to inflate term frequency for target queries
[Gyöngyi and Garcia-Molina, 2005a]. Link farms create artificial hyperlink structures to
inflate PageRank or HITS scores [Gyöngyi and Garcia-Molina, 2005b]. Cloaking serves dif-
ferent content to search engine crawlers than to users, allowing a page to rank for queries
unrelated to its actual content [Wu and Davison, 2006]. Content spinning uses automated
paraphrasing to generate many near-duplicate pages targeting different keyword variations
[Castillo and Davison, 2011].
    These attacks were well understood and partially mitigated in classical systems through
spam detection, link analysis penalties, and editorial guidelines [Ntoulas et al., 2006, Castillo
et al., 2007]. The transition to neural ranking models creates new attack surfaces. Neural
models learn to associate textual patterns with relevance scores through training, and these
learned associations can be exploited in ways that differ fundamentally from classical key-
word stuffing. Whether neural models are more or less robust than classical models to ma-
nipulation remained an open question prior to the work in this chapter.


6.1.2    Adversarial Attacks on Neural IR
Prior work on adversarial attacks against neural ranking models has generally required sub-
stantial access to the target model. Gradient-based approaches compute the gradient of the
relevance score with respect to document tokens and iteratively perturb the document to
maximise the score for a target query [Wu et al., 2023b, Liu et al., 2022]. PRADA [Wu
et al., 2023b] applies this approach to dense retrievers, producing adversarial passages that
rank highly for specific queries. Trigger-based attacks [Song et al., 2020, Zhong et al., 2023]
learn universal adversarial token sequences that, when appended to any document, increase
its relevance score across many queries. Suffix attacks [Zou et al., 2023] extend this to longer
adversarial suffixes optimised via gradient search.
CHAPTER 6. ADVERSARIAL VULNERABILITY                                                       117

     Table 6.2 summarises the access requirements of these attacks. All prior approaches
require at least one of: access to the target query (limiting scalability), access to model
weights or a surrogate (limiting applicability to black-box systems), or gradient computation
(limiting applicability to non-differentiable models). The attacks proposed in this chapter
differ fundamentally: they require only knowledge of the model’s prompt structure (which
is typically public) and access to the document to be manipulated. No query knowledge, no
model weights, and no gradient computation are needed.


6.1.3    Prompt Injection in Sequence-to-Sequence Relevance Models
Sequence-to-sequence relevance models such as monoT5 [Nogueira et al., 2020] encode the
query and document in a structured prompt, “Query: q Document: d Relevant:”, and use the
log-probability of the token “true” as the relevance score s(q, d) = log P(true | prompt). This
architecture creates a direct coupling between document content and the model’s relevance
prediction: because the document tokens are part of the prompt, they interact with all control
tokens (including “Relevant:”) through the decoder’s cross-attention mechanism.
    This coupling creates two hypothesised attack vectors. The preemption hypothesis posits
that including “Relevant: true” within the document text preempts the model’s judgment:
the decoder, having already attended to a “Relevant: true” sequence in the input, is biased
toward predicting “true” as the next token. The stuffing hypothesis posits that repeating the
target token “true” in the document increases its unigram probability in the decoder’s context,
raising the probability that it is generated at the output position. Both hypotheses predict
that query-independent document modifications can systematically increase relevance scores
across all queries, because the prompt structure is fixed and the attack targets the model’s
generation dynamics rather than any query-specific matching.
    These vulnerabilities are specific to the prompt-based scoring paradigm. Cross-encoders
that compute relevance via a [CLS] embedding do not have an explicit output token whose
probability can be manipulated, though they may still be vulnerable to the distributional
effects of keyword stuffing. We investigate transfer to non-prompt-based models in our ex-
periments.


6.1.4    LLM-Based Document Rewriting
The availability of instruction-following large language models has created a new class of ad-
versarial document manipulations. Rather than injecting raw keyword sequences or gradient-
optimised triggers, an adversary can prompt an LLM to rewrite a document in ways that
naturally incorporate adversarial tokens while maintaining linguistic coherence [Chen et al.,
2023].
    Two LLM-based attacks are investigated in this chapter. Adversarial paraphrasing rewrites
the document to naturally include prompt tokens (e.g., replacing “fleas live a long time” with
“true, fleas live a relevant long time”), making the manipulation harder to detect through
simple string matching. Adversarial summarisation prepends a summary sentence contain-
CHAPTER 6. ADVERSARIAL VULNERABILITY                                                          118

Table 6.1: Illustration of three prompt injection attacks on the monoT5 relevance model for
the query q := How long do fleas live? to increase the predicted relevance of docu-
ment d. Besides ‘true’, other adversarial terms can be used.

Attack       Prompt qd := Query: q Document: d Relevant:                             P(true | qd )
None         d := Fleas live a long time. Buy flea remedies here.                     0.11
Preemption   d ′ := Relevant: true Fleas live a long time. Buy flea remedies here.    0.25 (+0.14)
Stuffing     d ′ := true true true Fleas live a long time. Buy flea remedies here.    0.46 (+0.35)
Rewriting    d ′ := True fleas live a long time. Buy relevant flea remedies here.     0.33 (+0.22)


ing adversarial tokens, adding a “hook” that the decoder encounters before the original doc-
ument content.


6.2      Query-Independent Attacks Against Sequence-to-Sequence
         Relevance Models
The classical attacks surveyed in Section 6.1.1 were developed against lexical ranking mod-
els, whose scoring functions are inspectable and whose response to manipulation is analyti-
cally bounded. Sequence-to-sequence relevance models offer no equivalent guarantee: they
encode the query and document jointly in a structured prompt, and that prompt structure is
itself an attack surface. This section formalises three query-independent attacks that exploit
it. A preemption attack and a keyword-stuffing attack inject the prompt’s control vocabulary
directly into the document; an LLM-based rewriting attack achieves the same effect through
fluent text that is harder to detect. Unlike the gradient-based and surrogate-based attacks
reviewed in Section 6.1.2, none of the three requires access to model parameters, a surro-
gate, or the target query; each requires at most knowledge of the model’s prompt, which is
typically public.


6.2.1    Vulnerability of Sequence-to-Sequence Relevance Models
To motivate our attacks, we explain why sequence-to-sequence relevance models may be
vulnerable to adversarial attacks. For a query q, sequence-to-sequence relevance models are
typically used to re-rank the top-ranked results D of a first stage ranker. A re-ranker usu-
ally tries to improve the relevance approximation with respect to q for each d ∈ D. Applied
to re-ranking, sequence-to-sequence cross-encoders jointly encode the query and a to-be-
re-ranked document in a structured prompt [Nogueira and Cho, 2019], as exemplified in
Table 6.1. As the query and the document are provided to the model in a continuous se-
quence, all terms from the document interact with all terms of the prompt and, therefore, the
query. This architecture implies that prompt tokens appearing in the document text inter-
act with the model’s relevance prediction through the same cross-attention mechanism that
processes the genuine query, so including prompt tokens or their synonyms in a document
should systematically inflate its predicted relevance, and therefore its rank, across all queries.
CHAPTER 6. ADVERSARIAL VULNERABILITY                                                           119

Table 6.2: Overview of requirements of different adversarial attacks on neural relevance
models. Attackers either need full (✓), partial (✓*), or no access (X).

                                                          Content                 Model
                                                  Document          Query   Prompt    Weights
 Iterative Perturbation Raval and Verma [2020]        ✓              ✓        ✓           ✓*
 PRADA Wu et al. [2023a]                              ✓              ✓        ✓           ✓*
 Trigger Based Attacks Liu et al. [2022, 2023]        ✓              ✓        ✓           ✓*
 Suffix Attack Zou et al. [2023]                      ✓              ✓        ✓           ✓*
 Ours                                                 ✓              X        ✓           X


    A search engine provider should not assume that all content providers are acting in good
faith. Traditional vectors to attack a retrieval system mostly attempt to augment a text or
web page, e.g., using keyword stuffing, aiming at specific queries or topics. When attacking
neural IR systems, one may instead use an adversarial approach to gradually transform a
text such that a relevance model assigns a higher score to that text for a target query. To
generalise these attacks, we define a transformation d ′ = f (d, c), where c is any context used
to guide the augmentation, either query information or gradient response from a target neural
model, producing the augmented text d ′ .


6.2.2    Attack Model
Table 6.2 contrasts the access requirements of the attacks studied here with prior adversarial
attacks against neural ranking models. The attacks studied here require only knowledge
of the text prompt and of the output tokens that approximate the probability of relevance.
Access to the weights of the target model, or to a surrogate model, is not required, and the
attacker augments document text without targeting particular queries. Among the attacks in
Table 6.2, these have the fewest requirements.


6.2.3    Adversarial Preemption and Keyword-Stuffing
An adversarial attack aims to produce a document d ′ from d, such that for a query q,
Rθ (q, d ′ ) > Rθ (q, d). When using prior approaches, such an attack would require multi-
ple representations of a document to ensure that each representation contains either lexical
matches in a classic setting or suitable perturbations with respect to both a target query and
model. For a set of queries Q assuming no topic overlap between queries, all augmentations
required for a given document can be given as the set, { f (d, c = q); ∀q ∈ Q}.
    The prompt-based scoring paradigm permits a query-independent attack. Because the
prompt structure is fixed and public, an attacker who injects the prompt’s own control tokens
into a document can raise its relevance score without knowing any query. The keyword-
stuffing attack studied here injects prompt tokens into each passage, controlling for injection
position and the number of token repetitions, and so preempts the relevance judgment the
prompt is designed to elicit. We consider the injection of n ∈ {1, 2, 3, 4, 5} repetitions of a
CHAPTER 6. ADVERSARIAL VULNERABILITY                                                      120

token at the start (s) or end (e) of the document and injections of a token at random (r) as
exemplified in Table 6.1.
    By controlling for both position and repetition, we aim to investigate how these tokens
affect the contextualisation in the underlying pre-trained language models. We consider vari-
ations of tokens contained in the targeted prompt, investigating the injection of (1) prompt
tokens, (2) control tokens, (3) synonyms, and (4) sub-words. Prompt tokens refer to spans
from the prompt structure used during neural training. We use control tokens as spans with
equal length after encoding to one of the prompt tokens (e.g., ‘information: baz’ to control
for ‘relevant: true’). Synonyms refer to terms similar to a prompt token, which could fool
a naive filtering system. With sub-words, we want to investigate if attackers can hide the
adversarial tokens in longer, potentially misspelled words.


6.2.4    Adversarial Document Re-Writing with Large Language Models
Two approaches increase a document’s score by automatically re-writing it with large lan-
guage models. Because a language model can produce many different responses for the same
input, such re-writing attacks are much harder to detect than the injection attacks above. Us-
ing Alpaca [Taori et al., 2023] and ChatGPT, we define two classes of adversarial re-writing:
(1) paraphrase approaches that re-write a passage, and (2) summarisation approaches that
prepend a summary sentence to a passage. For both classes, we develop prompts to increase
the number of adversarial tokens in the paraphrased passage or the summary sentence. Out
of five candidate prompts, we identified the most effective prompt for Alpaca and ChatGPT
in a pilot study. All our re-writing approaches are query-independent so attackers can apply
them at scale.
    For all our re-writing attacks, we use the commercial ChatGPT that we contrast with
the open-source alternative Alpaca. For ChatGPT, we use the official REST API by Ope-
nAI for the model gpt-3.5-turbo. We use the official scripts for Alpaca to obtain the 7 bil-
lion parameter variant that we operate with the default configuration on one Nvidia A100
GPU with 40GB. We manually develop 10 candidate prompts (5 for paraphrasing and 5 for
summarisation) using the example from Table 6.1. To identify suitable prompts for each
LLM, we sample 1000 query-document pairs from the passage re-ranking dataset of the
TREC 2019 Deep Learning track [Craswell et al., 2019] as a pilot study to identify the
prompt causing the highest rank changes. To foster reproducibility, we include all request-
response pairs in our code repository.

Adversarial Paraphrasing.

Our first re-writing attack uses a large language model to paraphrase a passage while adding
adversarial tokens (e.g., “relevant” or “true”) to the paraphrased passage.
CHAPTER 6. ADVERSARIAL VULNERABILITY                                                          121

Adversarial Summarisation.

Our second re-writing attack prepends a passage by a single sentence summarising the pas-
sage but including additional adversarial tokens. For a passage p and an adversarial summary
sentence s produced by an LLM, we use p′ = s + ' ' + p as the adversarial passage.


6.3     Evaluation
The query-independent attacks of Section 6.2 are evaluated from two analytically distinct
viewpoints. A content provider aims to increase a single document’s visibility, so the relevant
quantity is the rank improvement of that document; this is measured point-wise, simulating
a single adversarial document in an otherwise standard corpus. A search provider aims for
effective retrieval over the whole collection, so the relevant quantity is the change in retrieval
effectiveness, measured through hypothetical best- and worst-case scenarios in which only
relevant or only non-relevant documents are manipulated.


6.3.1    Experimental Setup and Evaluation Methodology
Datasets.

We use the 2019/2020 TREC Deep Learning tracks [Craswell et al., 2019, 2020] of version 1
of MSMARCO [Bajaj et al., 2016] (with 8.8 million passages; the 2021/2022 editions on
version 2 are somewhat discouraged [Voorhees et al., 2022, Fröbe et al., 2022]), re-ranking
the top-1000 BM25 results. We contrast rankings for original documents with their attacked
counterparts.

Measures.

To assess attack efficacy, we define the following measures between original and attacked
document sets. All measures are computed for some transformation f (d). We first define
the success rate (SR) as the fraction of all query-document pairs P where f (d) improves the
rank of a given document d:
                                      
                             1        1, if rank(q, f (d)) < rank(q, d)
                    SR(P) =      ∑                                                         (6.1)
                            |P| q,d∈P  0, otherwise

where rank(q, d) is the rank of d for q when ordered by descending score.
    We define the Mean Rank Change (MRC) as the mean rank difference before and after
the attack to show the visible magnitude of an attack:

                               1
                                   ∑ rank(q, d) − rank(q, f (d))
                              |P| q,d∈P
                                                                                            (6.2)

   To assess the broader effects of this attack on retrieval effectiveness, we evaluate nDCG@10
and P@10 over the best and worst-case scenarios of all attacks. As users primarily interact
CHAPTER 6. ADVERSARIAL VULNERABILITY                                                      122

with the top-10 results [Kelly and Azzopardi, 2015], search engine providers are the most
concerned with the effect of an attack at this cutoff.

Target Models.

Although our attacks primarily focus on sequence-to-sequence models, we also study if
they generalise to other neural models. We evaluate relevance models that cover lexical
approaches and a set of neural architectures. As the main target, we use monoT5 [Nogueira
et al., 2020], a T5-based sequence-to-sequence cross-encoder that we also contrast across
four model sizes. As a lexical model, we use BM25, a bag-of-words relevance model. Ad-
ditionally, we include Electra [Pradeep et al., 2022], a non-prompting BERT-based cross-
encoder, ColBERT [Khattab and Zaharia, 2020], a late interaction bi-encoder, and TAS-
B [Hofstätter et al., 2020] as a classical bi-encoder. In all cases, we use the (Py)Terrier
implementation [Ounis et al., 2005] with default parameters.


6.3.2    A Content Provider’s Perspective
Attacking monoT5 with Keyword Stuffing

Tables 6.3 and 6.4 present the effect of keyword stuffing attacks (Section 6.2.3) on variants
of monoT5, split by model size so that each table fits the page in portrait orientation. We
find that the injection of prompt tokens, which include the token ‘relevant’, improves docu-
ment rank on average in all variants apart from monoT53B . Notably, in all cases, the token
‘false’ leads to less degradation than ‘true’. This contradicts any preempting of the relevance
judgment via a suffix. Furthermore, ‘relevant: false’ performs better than ‘relevant: true’
in all cases. However, the repetition of relevance leads to large rank improvements in the
base and large variants. Significant rank increases occur in most cases for spans containing
‘information’ with rank increasing up to 111 places in the case of monoT5small on DL19. In
Figure 6.1, we observe that generally, monoT5 is less susceptible to keyword stuffing applied
to highly ranked documents, with both positive and negative effects being reduced (contrast
ranks 0-200 and 500-700), likely showing that adding content to a document already consid-
ered relevant, has little effect on sequence-to-sequence cross-encoders. We also observe clear
positional bias when contrasting random to start and end, with monoT5 variants consistently
penalising tokens appended to documents whilst largely improving rank when prepending
tokens.
    Synonyms generally do not succeed in improving document rank; however, both ‘signif-
icant’ and ‘associated’ transfer to both the base and 3B variants of monoT5, and injection
of the token ‘important’ improves MRC by 42 places on DL19 scored by monoT53B . Sub-
words only improve MRC in attacking monoT5base with large rank degradation in monoT53B .
The injection of sub-words only improves monoT5base with larger variants increasingly pe-
nalising augmented documents (the attack reduces rank in all settings for monoT53B ).
CHAPTER 6. ADVERSARIAL VULNERABILITY                                                              123

Figure 6.1: Aggregate MRC over every 100 ranks for the token ‘relevant’ injected 5 times at

Attacking monoT5 by Re-writing Passages.

Table 6.5 presents the effectiveness of passage re-writing attacks (Section 6.2.4) on various
sizes of monoT5. We observe consistent rank improvements across almost all operating
points, demonstrating the efficacy of this attack. monoT53B is less affected by paraphrasing
attacks as rank improvements are insignificant for re-writing with ChatGPT, whereas changes
are still significant for Alpaca summarisation. We observe that the larger variants of monoT5
are less affected by paraphrasing attacks, albeit summary injection with Alpaca is effective in
all cases. In both attack settings, attacks using Alpaca outperform ChatGPT attacks. Given
the small size of this model, any attacker could perform these rewrites on a large scale and
consistently improve the rank of content while making only small changes to the text.

Transfer of Injection Attacks.

Tables 6.6 and 6.7 show that though monoT5 is most affected by the injection of prompt
tokens as illustrated in Figure 6.2(b), generalisation across neural models can be seen in cases
of the injection containing the token ‘relevant’ beyond the constraints of our attack model
outlined in Section 6.2.2. Due to BM25 penalties for document length and the addition of
tokens that are almost guaranteed not to be contained in the evaluation queries, the attack
fails to improve document rank across all token groups. For the tokens ‘information’ and
‘related’, which appear in the default PyTerrier stop-word list, the augmented document
becomes a duplicate that is resolved by a tie-break, producing the small rank change at a
0.0% success rate.
    Control tokens can greatly influence the monoT5 ranking of documents. However, they
do not generalise beyond T5, suggesting that the prompt structure from which these con-
trols were inspired has a significant ranking impact for prompt-based relevance models. We
observe mixed results when injecting synonyms for ‘relevant.’ ‘Significant’ is effective,
improving document scores for both cross-encoders. TAS-B is also affected. However,
ColBERT is unaffected and insensitive to synonyms due to its max pooling token-level sim-
ilarity computation that may ignore injected tokens, contrasting the deeper interaction of
cross-encoders or the passage-level similarity of standard bi-encoders.
    Subwords significantly improve the MRC for both cross-encoders, indicating that inject-

ing words containing the token ‘relevant’ has a positive impact. Filtering keyword-stuffing
attacks on neural models may therefore be more challenging, since tokenisation allows attack
tokens to be hidden where lexical models ignore them.
    Successful attacks on neural models frequently involve multiple repetitions of injected
tokens (as can be observed as the 3rd subscript of each attack in Tables 6.6 and 6.7). This
response is unexpectedly similar to a lexical model but does not depend on the frequency of
query terms and remains context-agnostic (e.g., the upward trend in Figure 6.2(a)). We also
observe that BERT-based architectures generally prefer the injection of tokens to the end in
contrast with monoT5, which almost always prefers injection at the start (as can be observed
as the 2nd subscript of each attack in Tables 6.6 and 6.7). The stronger generalisation of ap-
pended injections may suggest that it is a more effective attack when unsure of the language
model used in the targeted relevance model.

Transfer of LLM Re-Writing Attacks.

Table 6.8 shows that cross-encoders are weaker in paraphrasing and summary attacks. In
all cases, a significant improvement is found; however, summaries from ChatGPT fail to
improve rank over 50% of the time in monoT5 on DL20. Rank improvements when attack-
ing TAS-B with paraphrasing and summary are small, only improving rank in over 50% of
documents on DL19. ColBERT is generally not affected by a summary reflecting a general
in-variance to our attacks (further outlined by low variance observed in Figure 6.2(b)). Docu-
ment rank significantly drops in all paraphrasing attacks against BM25; this can be attributed
to the increase in document length from adding the tokens ‘relevant’ and ‘true’ as well as
the potential for an LLM re-write to re-phrase terms, which may cause lexical mismatch.
However, BM25 rank is improved by the injection of a summary showing that both LLMs
have captured useful terms in their summary; as this attack also transfers to cross-encoders,
it is an effective attack against a larger search pipeline.

by contrasting hypothetical lower/upper bounds that we obtain in an oracle scenario. For
the lower bound, we simulate that only non-relevant documents apply adversarial attacks.

Figure 6.2: An overview of (a) the scaling of rank improvement for the number of token
repetitions of control and prompt tokens with maximum MRC and (b) the variance of repe-
titions on different neural models for strongest settings.


We simulate that only relevant documents apply adversarial attacks as an upper bound. In
all cases, we select the adversarial attack that causes the highest rank change to report the
maximum effect for each document. Following our previous observations that re-writing
attacks have a smaller impact than injection attacks, we only include injection attacks in our
retrieval effectiveness experiments to maintain our focus on lower/upper bounds. We report
nDCG@10 and Precision@10 (albeit controversial [Sakai, 2020], we omit MRR because
MRR has several shortcomings [Fuhr, 2017, Zobel and Rashidi, 2020]). All neural models
re-rank the top 1000 documents retrieved by BM25. We report significance compared to the
original documents using a Student’s t-test with Bonferroni correction.
    Table 6.9 shows the maximum impact of our adversarial attacks by contrasting the worst
case (lower bound effectiveness) with the original effectiveness (documents are not manip-
ulated) and the best case (upper bound effectiveness). The injection attacks do not impact
BM25, as the retrieval scores never increase by adding non-query tokens to documents. In
all other cases, adversarial attacks have a substantial impact on the retrieval effectiveness
as the lower and upper bounds introduce, in almost all cases, significant changes, caus-
ing our attacks to degrade retrieval effectiveness at scale (the lower bound on nDCG@10
for ColBERT of 0.66 being the only exception). Adversarial attacks have the highest im-
pact on monoT5 (only TAS-B on TREC DL 2020 has the same lower/upper-bound variance
of nDCG@10). Importantly, for system-oriented evaluations, we observe that the system

with the paradigm change introduced by BERT, effectiveness shot up by around 0.08 MRR
on the MSMARCO test set [Lin et al., 2021], but adversarial attacks introduce even larger
changes, e.g., 0.08 nDCG@10 or even 0.11 Precision@10 for monoT5 on TREC DL 2020.


6.4             Conclusions
The attacks presented in this chapter demonstrate that the entanglement of query-dependent
and query-independent evidence within neural ranking models creates concrete, exploitable
vulnerabilities. Keyword stuffing, a technique as old as web search, transfers effectively to
sequence-to-sequence neural relevance models when adapted to target prompt tokens and
control vocabulary. The injection of the token “relevant” five times at the start of a document
improves the mean rank of that document by over 60 positions under monoT5base . LLM-
based document rewriting produces adversarial text that is linguistically coherent and harder
to detect, yet achieves consistent and significant rank improvements across model scales.
These attacks require no knowledge of the target query, no gradient access to the model, and
no surrogate, only knowledge of the prompt structure, which is typically public.
    The engineering claim of this chapter is that the demonstrated attacks are effective in
CHAPTER 6. ADVERSARIAL VULNERABILITY                                                      131

practice under realistic threat assumptions. The analytical claim is that the vulnerability is
structural: it arises from the model’s inability to separate query-independent document ma-
nipulation from query-dependent relevance, a direct consequence of having collapsed the
prior-likelihood decomposition into a monolithic scorer. A system that maintained separable
query-independent and query-dependent components would have an internal basis for de-
tecting the disagreement between a high neural score and a low lexical score, precisely the
mitigation strategy investigated in the following chapter.

Verdict on H3.1. H3.1 is supported. Query-independent attacks that require no knowledge
of the target query, no model weights, and no gradient computation systematically raise
the rank of manipulated documents: injecting the control token five times at the start of a
document improves its mean rank by over sixty positions under monoT5base , and LLM-based
rewriting achieves consistent, significant rank gains while remaining linguistically coherent.
The effect transfers across model scales for the sequence-to-sequence family, whose fixed
prompt structure is the most direct attack surface. The scope condition is the mechanism: the
vulnerability is strongest where an explicit output token can be driven by document content,
and transfer to cross-encoders that score via a pooled embedding is weaker and assessed
explicitly rather than assumed. Within that scope the analytical claim holds, namely that the
vulnerability is a structural consequence of collapsing the prior-likelihood decomposition
into a monolithic scorer.
    The attacks described here modify document content in ways that, upon inspection, a
careful reader could detect: keyword sequences such as “true true true relevant” are not
natural text, and adversarial paraphrases introduce distributional anomalies. The following
chapter investigates a subtler form of manipulation, the exploitation of positional biases in
neural rankers through injection of promotional content that is stylistically coherent with
the host document, and proposes a mitigation strategy grounded in the asymmetry between
separable scoring components.
Chapter 7

Adversarial Mitigation Through
Separable Scoring

   Hypothesis H3.2 (tested in this chapter)
   Separable scoring components respond asymmetrically to adversarial manipulation,
   producing a detectable disagreement signal: ensembles that interpolate neural scores
   with lexical components reduce the effectiveness of positional bias exploitation and
   promotional content injection without degrading non-adversarial retrieval.

    The previous chapter demonstrated that neural ranking models are vulnerable to query-
independent adversarial attacks that exploit the entanglement of query-dependent and query-
independent evidence in monolithic scorers. The attacks described there, keyword stuffing
and LLM-based document rewriting, modify document content in ways that are effective but,
upon inspection, detectable. This chapter investigates a subtler and potentially more conse-
quential form of manipulation: the exploitation of positional biases inherent in transformer-
based rankers to inject promotional or non-relevant content into documents without degrad-
ing their neural relevance scores.
    The vulnerability arises from the attention mechanism. Transformer-based ranking mod-
els do not weight all positions within a document equally; empirically, tokens at certain po-
sitions contribute disproportionately to the relevance score. An adversary who understands
this bias can insert non-relevant content at positions where the model’s attention is weakest,
effectively concealing the injection from the neural scorer. This attack succeeds because the
monolithic scorer has no internal basis for distinguishing legitimate document structure from
adversarial structure; it is the same entanglement problem identified in Chapter 6, manifest-
ing through a different mechanism.
    The second contribution of this chapter is a mitigation strategy that exploits the structural
advantage of separable scoring. We show that contextualised promotional text, generated by
an LLM to be stylistically coherent with the host document, is effectively invisible to neural
scoring but is appropriately penalised by lexical scoring, because document length increases
without proportional relevant information. This asymmetry between separable components,


                                              132
CHAPTER 7. ADVERSARIAL MITIGATION                                                             133

the neural scorer and the lexical scorer responding differently to the same manipulation,
produces a detectable disagreement signal. By interpolating between the two scores, the
defence reduces the effectiveness of adversarial promotion without degrading performance
on non-adversarial queries. The defence is not a general solution to adversarial robustness
in neural ranking; it is a principled instantiation of the separability thesis, demonstrating that
modular systems provide internal checks that monolithic systems cannot.
    Section 7.1 reviews positional bias as an exploitation mechanism, contextualised promo-
tional content, and score disagreement as a detection signal. The body of the chapter then
characterises the attention-mediated positional bias, demonstrates the contextualised promo-
tion attack, and develops the interpolation defence, before closing with the verdict on H3.2
and an explicit statement of the defence’s scope.


7.1     Related Work

7.1.1    Positional Bias as an Exploitation Mechanism
Chapter 5 discussed positional bias in the context of user models and click data: users ex-
amine higher-ranked results more frequently, biasing click signals toward top positions. In
this chapter, we consider a different sense of positional bias: the sensitivity of neural ranking
models to where within a document content is placed.
    Transformer-based models contextualise tokens using self-attention, which is sensitive
to both content and position [Vaswani et al., 2017]. Prior work has noted that neural ranking
models exhibit sensitivity to the ordering of spans within documents: rearranging passages
can change the relevance score even when the bag-of-words content is identical [Hofstätter
et al., 2021, MacAvaney et al., 2022a]. This observation suggests that the position of injected
content within a document affects how strongly it influences the relevance score.
    We hypothesise that injected content placed after salient (query-relevant) sequences re-
ceives higher attention weight than content placed before them, because the attention mech-
anism propagates relevance signal from earlier salient tokens to adjacent positions. This
creates an exploitable asymmetry: an adversary can maximise the impact of injected promo-
tional text by placing it immediately after the most relevant passage in a document.
    This form of positional bias is fundamentally different from the evaluation-side positional
bias discussed in Chapter 5. Evaluation bias concerns where in a ranked list a document
appears; exploitation bias concerns where within a document adversarial content is placed.
The two phenomena share a common origin in attention-like mechanisms, user attention in
one case, transformer attention in the other, but they operate at different levels of the retrieval
pipeline and require different analytical frameworks.
    Classical retrieval axioms provide a baseline expectation against which positional bias
can be assessed. The TFC2 axiom [Fang et al., 2011] states that adding occurrences of a non-
query term to a document should not increase its relevance score. The LNC1 axiom [Fang
et al., 2011] states that adding content to a document should decrease (or at most preserve)
CHAPTER 7. ADVERSARIAL MITIGATION                                                           134

its score if the added content is not relevant to the query. BM25 satisfies both axioms through
its length normalisation: adding tokens increases |d|, which increases the denominator and
decreases the score unless the added tokens are query terms. Neural models that violate these
axioms exhibit behaviour that diverges from the properties the IR community has identified
as desirable for trustworthy ranking.


7.1.2    Contextualised Promotional Content
The availability of instruction-following large language models enables a form of adversarial
manipulation that is qualitatively different from the attacks investigated in Chapter 6. Rather
than injecting keyword sequences or gradient-optimised triggers, an adversary can condition
an LLM on a target document and generate promotional text that shares the document’s
vocabulary, style, and topical focus. The resulting injection is stylistically coherent with its
surroundings: it does not disrupt the document’s distributional properties, making it difficult
to detect through simple heuristics or through the neural scorer itself.
     We source static (non-contextualised) promotional examples from Wikipedia rejected ed-
its [Wikipedia contributors, 2023], which provide real-world instances of promotional con-
tent flagged by human editors. For contextualised promotion, we use Llama 2 [Touvron
et al., 2023] to generate promotional sentences conditioned on each target document, pro-
ducing injections that match the host document’s register and domain. The hypothesis is
that contextualised promotion reduces the ranking penalty because the generated text shares
distributional properties with the genuine document: a neural model that has learned to score
documents based on their overall textual coherence will be less likely to penalise an injec-
tion that does not disrupt that coherence. BM25, by contrast, should penalise both static and
contextualised injections because document length increases without proportional relevant
information, regardless of stylistic coherence.


7.1.3    Score Disagreement as a Detection Signal
The mitigation strategy proposed in this chapter rests on the observation that separable scor-
ing components respond asymmetrically to adversarial manipulation. Neural models that
have collapsed query-dependent and query-independent evidence into a single scorer are,
as demonstrated in Chapter 6, susceptible to query-independent attacks that exploit learned
associations between textual patterns and relevance. Lexical models, by contrast, are gov-
erned by term frequency statistics and length normalisation, properties that are analytically
tractable and that penalise content injection through well-understood mechanisms. When the
two scoring functions disagree, specifically when a document receives a high neural score
but a low lexical score, the discrepancy serves as evidence that the document’s neural score
may reflect manipulation rather than genuine relevance.
    This principle connects directly to the prior-likelihood decomposition discussed in Chap-
ter 5. The BM25 score functions as a proxy for the query-dependent likelihood as estimated
by a classical model; the neural score reflects an entangled estimate that includes both likeli-
CHAPTER 7. ADVERSARIAL MITIGATION                                                         135

                aesthetics of the Macbook is
                highly valued.                                 x10

        LLM     data, just like the SIGMETs used
                by pilots.

information. However, the neural ranking model (monoT5) is invariant to the addition of

hood and prior. Systematic divergence between the two signals that the neural model may be
responding to query-independent properties that an explicit prior would have handled sepa-
rately. The mitigation strategy does not recover a true prior-likelihood decomposition, but
it exploits the same structural insight: separable components provide internal checks that
monolithic systems cannot.


7.2     Exploitation of Positional Bias in Ranking Models
The robustness concern motivating this chapter is specific. A transformer-based ranking
model does not weight all positions within a document equally, and an adversary who un-
derstands this can place non-relevant content where the model’s attention is weakest. Prior
work established that neural ranking models are sensitive to span order [Jiang et al., 2021,
MacAvaney et al., 2022a]; this chapter examines how that sensitivity may be exploited by
an adversary to inject non-relevant content without degrading a document’s ranking score.
The injected content is generated by a large language model conditioned on the target doc-
ument, so that it shares the document’s vocabulary and topical focus and can be applied at
scale without query knowledge or human intervention. Figure 7.1 illustrates the resulting
manipulation: a contextualised promotional span is added to a document, and where BM25
penalises the addition as a length increase without proportional relevant content, the neural
model is largely invariant to it.
    The analysis rests on two claims. The first is that the position of an injected span has a
disproportionate effect on the relevance score even when the information need is unchanged:
CHAPTER 7. ADVERSARIAL MITIGATION                                                            136

a span placed after a query-relevant passage is penalised less than the same span placed
before it. The second is that conditioning the injected span on the host document, so that
it shares the document’s context, further conceals its effect on relevance estimation. The
empirical study in Section 7.2.1 confirms both claims, showing that the rank of a document
augmented with non-relevant text can be improved by up to nine positions through placement
alone. This motivates the concept of attention bleed-through: the propagation of positive
context from a relevant span to an adjacent non-relevant one, formalised in Section 7.2.1.
    Because lexical models penalise length increases axiomatically, a separable lexical com-
ponent provides a basis for detecting manipulation that the neural scorer alone cannot. Sec-
tion 7.2.3 develops a mitigation strategy on this principle: an independent classifier, applied
without retraining the deployed ranker, recovers a significant fraction of ranking performance
across multiple retrieval architectures without access to the generated content. The code,
data, and generated artefacts supporting these experiments are released to support reproduc-
tion. This section outlines our methodology to generate contextual text for probing neural
retrieval models.


7.2.1    Positional Information
Transformer-based models are composed of a sequence of blocks containing the attention
mechanism [Vaswani et al., 2017], which progressively manipulate the representation of a
sequence. A positional encoding either informs the model of a token’s absolute position in a
sequence [Vaswani et al., 2017] or its relative position compared to other tokens [Shaw et al.,
2018]. Though important for tasks such as text generation and, more generally, language
modelling [Shaw et al., 2018], we posit that this contextualisation conditioned on positional
information can introduce unintended consequences for retrieval.
    Given a sequence of tokens estimated to be relevant, we consider that through positional
information, the attention mechanism which allows contextualisation between tokens could
propagate “positive” attention scores from said relevant span to a subsequent non-relevant
span. Formally, we hypothesise that for a sequence of two texts {t0 ,t1 } and a query q, if t1 is
considered more relevant than t0 :

                               S (q,t1 ⊕ t0 ; θ ) > S (q,t0 ⊕ t1 ; θ )                     (7.1)

where in Equation 7.1, relevance score of a text t with respect to q by model θ is defined
as S (q,t; θ ) and ⊕ represents string concatenation to a sequence. We call these effects
attention bleed-through: in the context of retrieval, the positive effect of a relevant sentence
bleeds onto other sentences, reducing the negative effect on the downstream relevance score
even if a span lacks inherent value to a task. This creates an unintended correlation between
a span’s position and perceived relevance, potentially harming retrieval effectiveness.
    To investigate this hypothesis more concretely, we investigate how adding non-relevant
text close to document sentences with high similarity scores with the query affects retrieval.
More concretely, we introduce salience as the scoring of a span within a text to another, e.g.,
CHAPTER 7. ADVERSARIAL MITIGATION                                                            137

a document to a query. If we decompose a document d into n spans, d = {s0 , . . . , sn }, the most
salient span with respect to some query q is defined as max sim(eq , es ) where sim(eq , es ) is
                                                              s∈d
a similarity measure in vector space e.g inner product.


7.2.2    Contextualised Generation
Retrieval axioms suggest that when the length of a document increases without further sat-
isfying an information need, it should decrease the rank of that document for a given query
(TFC2, LNC1) [Fang et al., 2011]. Traditionally, under lexical approaches [Robertson et al.,
1998], adding non-relevant text decreases a document’s rank based on principles such as
document length and information need mismatch. However, we propose that controlling the
position and conditioning of promotional text can mitigate its negative impact.
     We condition the generation of promotional spans on the context of the target document.
This is achieved using a generative language model, which receives a prompt specifying the
promotional task and the document content itself. We hypothesise that this context will lead
to partially shared topicality as the generated promotional text will likely contain tokens sim-
ilar to those already present in the document, fostering positive attention interactions with the
surrounding content. This contextual similarity may effectively obfuscate the promotional
text within the document, minimising the ranking penalty imposed by retrieval models due
to its non-relevance.
     For a ranking model θ , we define the set of documents retrieved for a query q by the
model as R(q; θ ). We condition the generation of each span on a target document such that
relevance judgments are not required, creating a query-agnostic attack. Our prompt contains
the task text sequence t, which, in this case, states that the model should promote an entity
provided as input.
     The output by a generative model ω of a document-conditioned span s within document
d ∈ R(q; θ ), is governed by the conditional probability

                                                |s|
                                Pω (s| t, d) ≡ ∏ Pω (si | s0:i ,t, d)                       (7.2)
                                               i=0

where in Equation 7.2, s0:i = {s0 , . . . , si−1 } are previously generated tokens. Tokens are re-
alised by applying argmax over logits at each step i. Each token generation relies on the
previously generated sequence, the task prompt, and the document context, aiming to create
task-specific text that integrates within the document.


7.2.3    Mitigation within Retrieval
Our experiments confirm that transformers are susceptible to manipulation through the posi-
tion and contextualisation of injected text. This raises concerns about the general robustness
of neural ranking models against similar attacks. Retraining each neural ranking model may
not be feasible. Instead, we propose a simple and scalable mitigation strategy based on our
CHAPTER 7. ADVERSARIAL MITIGATION                                                          138

understanding of attention bleed-through. The core principle is that transformer-based mod-
els struggle to penalise non-relevant text due to potential bleed-through effects. We introduce
a classification model φ specifically trained to detect promotional content within documents.
This model operates independently of the ranking model.
    We exploit our hypothesis of attention bleed-through to our advantage, considering that a
sliding window scoring method may minimise opportunities for bleed-through. By process-
ing text in sliding windows, the classifier can focus on local interactions between potentially
promotional content and its surrounding text. This isolation assists the classifier in making
clearer estimations of the presence of undesirable text, potentially mitigating bleed-through’s
negative impact. As the posterior represents a higher probability of promotion being present,
we look to improve the relevance score if this estimate is lower. We subtract the posterior
from 1 such that S ′ (d; φ ) = max 1 − Pφ (Y = 1|s) reflects the posterior of the span s not
                               s∈d
containing promotion. As such, we determine the final relevance score of a document as
follows:

                         S (q, d) = α S (q, d; θ ) + (1 − α) S ′ (d; φ )                  (7.3)

In Equation 7.3, scoring combines the original neural ranking model output parameterised
by θ with the promotional content detection score parameterised by φ . The parameter α
balances relevance and penalisation of undesirable content (based on promotional content
detection). This approach offers a flexible trade-off between these competing objectives.


7.3      Evaluation of Contextualised Text
This section evaluates the positional and contextual injection attacks on a passage-ranking
task, contrasting lexical and neural retrievers and isolating the contributions of injection
position and document-conditioned generation.

IR Datasets

We use the MSMARCO passage collection [Bajaj et al., 2016], a corpus of over 8.8 million
passages extracted from Bing query searches. In all experiments, we evaluate target mod-
els on the TREC Deep Learning 2019 track test set [Craswell et al., 2019], which contains
relevance judgments for 43 queries providing human-assessed queries to validate our hy-
potheses. We use these human-judged relevance labels to inject spans of varying relevance.

Models

We investigate neural ranking models based on BERT and T5 architectures to assess how
both embedding- and decoder-determined relevance approximations are affected by our ap-
proach. Additionally, we employ BM25 to contrast against neural models and to act as an
indicator of exact term matching between queries and generated text. We evaluate two bi-
CHAPTER 7. ADVERSARIAL MITIGATION                                                                   139

encoders, Contriever [Izacard et al., 2022] and ColBERT [Khattab and Zaharia, 2020], which
uses the late interaction paradigm. We also evaluate a sequence-to-sequence cross-encoder,
monoT5 [Nogueira et al., 2020]. A description of these models can be found in Appendix
A.1.

Promotion Datasets

In determining entities to promote, we chose five spans that explicitly referenced an entity
from a subset of scraped Wikipedia rejected edits [Bertsch and Bethard, 2021]. We consider
these edits to be examples of static text as they represent promotional content outside the
context of a document because human editors rejected these edits specifically for being pro-
motional. The referenced entity becomes part of our task text in prompting each language
model (as outlined in Section 7.2.2).
    We inject promotional spans into the top 100 ranked documents for each query. We
truncate each generation to a single sentence so that the span length does not confound
comparisons. Each span is injected as described in Section 7.3.

Metrics

We perform a pairwise evaluation comparing injected static text and document contextu-
alised text, investigating how the negative effects of text injection on retrieval performance
can be reduced from the perspective of a malicious content provider. In each evaluation,
significance is determined by a 95% confidence t-test with Bonferroni correction to assess
the significance of positional bias.

ABNIRML (ABN)

Proposed by MacAvaney et al. [2022a], the ABNIRML score aims to determine the em-
pirical preference of a retrieval model comparing two document sets. Given a top-k set of
documents scored by some ranking function, we inject promotion at a controlled position into
each document in the top-k ranking K where (q, di ) ∈ K. We augment each document, yield-
ing a new triple set (q, di , di∗ ) ∈ K ∗ . For each triple t, we compute sign(Rθ (q, d) − Rθ (q, d ∗ ))
1 . The mean of this computation yields the ABNIRML score. When the ABNIRML score

is positive, the model prefers the original set; when it is 0, the augmentation does not affect
preference, and a negative score indicates a preference for the augmented set.

Mean Rank Shift (MRS)

We compute the rank change of an augmented document d ∗ compared to the original docu-
ment d by substituting the original document with the augmented document in the retrieved
top-k for a query q. We replace the original document and re-rank the set to find the differ-
ence in rank between the original and augmented document. MRS is the same quantity as the
1 This variation on ABNIRML occurs when δ = 0 as the metric was found to be insensitive to the value of δ

 [MacAvaney et al., 2022a]
CHAPTER 7. ADVERSARIAL MITIGATION                                                        140

Mean Rank Change (MRC) of Chapter 6 (Eq. 6.2), differing only in the perturbation it mea-
sures: MRC is computed over documents modified in place by keyword stuffing or rewriting,
whereas MRS is computed over documents substituted into an existing top-k ranking, so that
the comparison is made against the unaugmented ranking of the same candidate set.

Injection of Known Relevant and Non-Relevant Text

To test how relevance responds to position and context, we evaluate ranking model pref-
erence for documents with and without injected text. Re-ranking is performed for each
test query over 100 documents retrieved by BM25. We inject spans from varying levels of
human-judged relevant documents (ranging from 0-3) to assess the effect of on-topic and off-
topic injection on relevance. Per common conventions with the DL19 dataset, we consider
a judgment of 2-3 as relevant, 0-1 as related, and judgments from other queries as non-
relevant. We inject documents at the following positions. We inject at different positions to
assess how positional bias affects retrieval score when controlling for the known relevance
of a document.

• Absolute Position (Abs-P): places a span of text before, in the middle or after a document

• Relative Position (Rel-P): places a span before and after the most salient sentence in a
  document (salience as defined in Section 7.2.1)

    In particular, for obtaining embedded representations of sentences used for salience
computation (defined in Section 7.2.1), we use the Sentence-Transformers [Reimers and
Gurevych, 2019] MPNet encoder model (all-mpnet-base-v2), which uses mean pool-
ing over token embeddings.

Generation of Contextualised Promotional Text

We use a zero-shot approach to prompt a generative language model. To determine a suitable
prompt, we perform an initial qualitative pilot study to find cases where the model could
fail to contextualise to the text, or simply refuse to answer considering the task improper.
We find that appending the cue ‘Okay, here is a sentence promoting the item whilst using
important terms from the document’ to the prompt elicits the promotional generation despite
the model’s alignment training.

      Prompt Format: Using the important keywords taken from the Document,
      write a sentence mentioning and promoting the Item:
      Document: {document}
      Item: {item}
      Response:

   We apply two LLMs of varying sizes, Llama 2 (Llama-2-7b-chat-hf) with 7 billion
parameters and GPT-3.5 Turbo with 175 billion parameters accessed through the OpenAI

rejected as promotional.


7.3.1    Results and Discussion
Position largely affects relevance under augmentation.

In Table 7.2, we observe that across all neural models, position has a large effect on rele-
vance (compare ‘before’ and ‘after’). BM25 penalises the addition of non-relevant content,
reducing rank by 18 places as no query terms are likely present in the new span. Contriever
shows minimal ABNIRML preference and rank change when appending non-relevant spans
to a document. It is most likely that this results from the maximised distance between the
[CLS] token, leading to reduced change in representation. Moreover, we observe similar in-
variance when probing ColBERT and monoT5, which do not pool their representations and
use different positional encodings, suggesting that this effect can generalise across trans-
former variants. This observation correlates with our hypothesis outlined in Section 7.2.1, in
that by injecting a span after the most salient text (‘after’, determined by both absolute and
relative position), we reduce the effect of a sequence on the overall relevance approximation.
Additionally, in Figure 7.2, observe this variance in rank change reduces dramatically across
all relevance levels when injecting near a salient span. This is notable as though absolute and
relative positions show similar MRS, relative injection with respect to a salient span repre-
sents a more consistent reduction in rank penalty and, therefore, could be a more effective
attack vector.
     Across all neural ranking models, MRS is significantly reduced when injecting text di-
