rectly after the most salient span compared to injection ‘before’. Though this position does
not maximise absolute distance from the start of a document, the reduction in preference
suggests that the influence of a sequence of tokens is partially determined by its position
with respect to important tokens for a particular query. Furthermore, rank improvements
by adding relevant spans are reduced when injected after the salient span. We observe that
injection at the start of a document maximises the rank change, improving MRS by up to
18 ranks in the case of monoT5. This conforms to the findings of Liu et al. [2023] in that
tokens that improve relevance (in their case, adversarial perturbations) are best placed at the
start of a text. A small improvement in MRS is observed when appending non-relevant text
across monoT5 and ColBERT, which is likely caused by a bias induced by over-fitting to the
distribution of the corpus.

Conditioning generation on documents effectively reduces model preference and rank
degradation.

In Table 7.3, observe that BM25 rank and preference are reduced when injecting LLM-
generated text compared to a static promotional span with ABNIRML preference reducing
from 0.99 to 0.58 in the case of GPT-generated promotion, and MRS is improved from
16.97 to 10.48. This occurs because of the topical contextualisation of the injected text as
illustrated in Figure 7.1. We observe similar performance in Llama 2, with both models
improving ABNIRML preference. ColBERT and monoT5 both show a strong bias for con-

textualised text, likely due to their stronger term matching. Though ABNIRML preference
is reduced comparing static and contextualised text in Contriever, larger changes in MRS
are mainly determined by the injection position with the model showing relative invariance
to the context of an injection, suggesting that any exact term matching is minimal [Formal
et al., 2022b].

Effective positional injection amplifies contextualisation.

In the case of both ColBERT and Contriever, when text is injected before either the document
or the most salient span (Rows containing Before, columns ColBERT and Contriever), the
models are invariant to the context of injected spans as the text has a large influence on the
text which follows it. monoT5 shows a clear preference for generated promotion even when
placed before the documents (0.84 ABNIRML versus 0.60 and 0.57), suggesting that its
joint representation is susceptible to positive contextualisation between query and document
terms. When combining document-conditioned generation and positional injection, rank
change between the original and augmented sets can be reduced to an MRS of 1.86 for
ColBERT and 1.80 for monoT5. Notably, though Contriever is susceptible to positional
injection contrasting before and after in all cases when injecting contextualised promotion,
ABNIRML preference is still reduced.
CHAPTER 7. ADVERSARIAL MITIGATION                                                                 145

Qualitative analysis of generated text.

In Figure 7.3 observe examples of generated promotional content and their respective con-
text. Though the majority of inspected text successfully provides a promotional span within
the context of a document, depending on said context, the realistic success of such an attack
could be variable. Observing the example of promoting a motorcycle brand in the context
of the Isle of Mann, it is natural that such a context could include outdoor activities such as
motorcycling, so the text is not only fluent but also effective. We observe a similar effect
when promoting a vodka brand in the context of high blood pressure; this example is notable
as it represents the automated generation of potentially harmful misinformation whilst being
automated and query-agnostic, therefore reducing both human effort and the overall effect
on retrievability of the document.
    However, in a less “natural” context, we observe that generative models will instead
insert entities into a statement in a generic fashion, which leads to an unnatural and clearly
forced phrase. This latter case would occur in some cases, though due to the fully automated
process of this attack, topics in which an entity cannot be appropriately promoted are of little
concern. Table 7.4 lists representative successful generations.

Table 7.4: Representative successful promotions: spans that are reasonable in context and
succeed in promoting the entity. Further successful, failed, and absurd examples are listed in
§7.6.2.

7.4      Mitigation Evaluation
The preceding section showed that multiple architectures are affected by positional and con-
textual injection. This section evaluates whether those adversarial effects can be mitigated
without retraining the underlying search system, using an independent model applied along-
side the deployed ranker rather than modifying it.
CHAPTER 7. ADVERSARIAL MITIGATION                                                       146

              origins meet the thrill of the open road.


               Julia Roberts
              Did you know that Julia Roberts, the famous actress with a net 
              worth worth millions, enjoys riding Honda motorcycles with her 
              brother Eric Roberts, who is also an actor?

              Finlandia Vodka helps you create the perfect plastic bag for your

(red context) for each item; the relevant text has been manually annotated in green. Further
examples are listed in §7.6.2.
CHAPTER 7. ADVERSARIAL MITIGATION                                                           147

Datasets

As ground truth does not exist for this task and capturing the distribution of LLM-generated
text is dependent on the particular model [Mitchell et al., 2023, Su et al., 2023], we instead
penalise promotion directly, though our approach could feasibly be extended to a broader set
of undesirable texts. We assume no access to the output of a particular generative model.
Hence, we use a classifier in a zero-shot manner. We employ the SemEval 2020 Task 11
Propaganda Techniques Corpus (PTC) [Da San Martino et al., 2020] comprising 15000
spans of text labelled with 18 propaganda classes. We observe that the 17 classes denoting
propaganda are suitable for classifying promotion. We balance the training set after the
combination of the 17 labels such that we have an equal distribution of propaganda and
non-propaganda. The dataset contains challenging examples constituting ‘weasel’ words,
i.e., subtle promotion methods or discrediting of an entity [Ott, 2018, Bertsch and Bethard,
2021], which we consider to be a useful parallel to promotion.

Training

We finetune a RoBERTa base model [Liu et al., 2019] in a binary classification setting (de-
scribed in Section 7.6.1) to model the posterior probability of promotion being present by
taking the classifier’s confidence. We use RoBERTa as prior work has succeeded in the main
SemEval task leveraging RoBERTa [Raj et al., 2020, Singh et al., 2020].
    To evaluate the intermediate task of identifying whether a given document contains pro-
motional text, we create a balanced test set by sub-sampling examples from the different
combinations of each generative model, injection position, and promoted entities. This in-
termediate evaluation can be found in Section 7.4.1. We evaluate in a retrieval setting de-
scribed in Section 7.2.3 measuring nDCG@10 and MRR. We create a pseudo-corpus where
one augmented document is added for each entity such that we have 200 documents for each
query. Metrics are aggregated over each entity, simulating the scenario of an automated
targeted promotion of each entity in search. Relevance judgments are provided for only aug-
mented documents such that we can observe the penalisation of promotion by MRR, which
we name Mean Reciprocal Promotional Reduction (MRPR) as we want to minimise this
metric. We present the optimal value of α tuned on nDCG@10. Significance is determined
as described in Section 7.3.


7.4.1    Results and Discussion
Promotion can be detected in a zero-shot setting.

Table 7.5 shows that performance improves when using a sliding window (∆ Accuracy in
the range [0.112, 0.3915]). We observe bleed-through effects similar to those presented in
Tables 7.2 and 7.3 in which injection after a salient sentence was effective in reducing overall
performance, for brevity we place these results in Section 7.6.3. Observing high precision,
we are satisfied that negative effects caused by false positives should be minimal, meaning
CHAPTER 7. ADVERSARIAL MITIGATION                                                           148

Table 7.5: Classification performance at relative position ‘After’. A full set of classification
results can be found in Section 7.6.3.

                Generator     Type         Acc.       F1      Prec.     Recall
                Static        Full        0.548     0.232     0.767     0.137
                Static        Window      0.939     0.940     0.923     0.959
                Llama-2       Full        0.577     0.315     0.824     0.195
                Llama-2       Window      0.683     0.584     0.848     0.446

highly relevant documents without promotion are likely to maintain a high rank.

Retrieval performance is largely recovered in an offline process.

Table 7.6 shows that applying the mitigation recovers a significant fraction of retrieval per-
formance in a standard setting. In 3 of 4 models, a large weighting of the classifier weight
is required to successfully penalise promotion (generally α ∼ [0.1, 0.3], Sensitivity analysis
can be found in Section 7.4.1). We observe cases of MRR being reduced by a statistically
insignificant margin; given that the aggregate MRS of our augmentations degrades rank by
1 to 2 positions, one would expect that MRR change is minimal from our previous exper-
iments (Table 7.2). Model performance is still around 5 points of nDCG@10 lower than
standard reported values; this is partially due to a fully duplicated corpus, leading to pre-
viously ‘highly-relevant’ documents which have been augmented with positional injection
maintaining rank, as to provide a greater weight to the classifier would further reduce pre-
cision. The positional effects described in Section 7.3.1 remain present (see rows ‘after’);
however, they are consistently reduced with nDCG@10 improved in the range [0.065, 0.101]
for neural models and a significant reduction of MRPR occurs in all cases. In the case of
monoT5, we see that a larger α is required to recover performance. Due to the binary na-
ture of monoT5 relevance scoring (true and false), its relevance scoring is bi-modal; as such,
higher values of α allow for the proper discrimination of promotion as all ‘relevant’ docu-
ment scores are close. When considering a granular evaluation such as ABNIRML or MRS
in Section 7.3.1, we see that absolute position is most effective; however, when evaluating
known relevant documents, we see a similar negative effect on retrieval performance. This
is notable in that for a document that meets an information need, and relative position is as
effective under mitigation in maintaining rank as absolute position, further strengthening the
discussion of results in Section 7.3.1.

Sensitivity to the interpolation weight α.

Figure 7.4 shows that both bi-encoders follow a similar trend of preferring a larger weighting
of the promotion penalty provided by the classifier. monoT5 instead prefers larger values,
with maximum performance at α = 0.6. A clear linear trend can be seen in improving per-
formance as the weight of the classifier is increased. We consistently see that BM25 cannot

recover performance when evaluating LLM-generated text unless a small α (larger classifier
weight) is used. In Figure 7.5, we see a more granular indicator of each model’s ability to
penalise promotional content. monoT5, across all operating points, largely penalises promo-
tional content when the classifier weighting is applied (all apart from α = 1.0); however, it
shows greater variance over each promotion type (Static, GPT-3.5, Llama2). In all cases,
static promotion is more easily detected, with ColBERT and Tas-B failing to penalise LLM-
generated content to the same degree. Though we see wide margins between each model
when evaluating overall injected documents, the weighting of the classifier allows all models
to perform similarly when evaluating DL’19 relevance judgments.


7.5     Conclusions
The positional exploitation study revealed that neural models exhibit attention-mediated po-
sitional biases that can be exploited through targeted content injection. Promotional text
inserted after salient sequences is penalised less than text inserted before them, because the
CHAPTER 7. ADVERSARIAL MITIGATION                                                            150




         Figure 7.4: Sensitivity to α measuring nDCG@10 for absolute position ‘after’.


model’s attention dynamics privilege content that follows query-relevant passages. Contex-
tualised promotional text, generated by an LLM to be stylistically coherent with the host
document, is effectively invisible to neural scoring: the model cannot distinguish it from
legitimate document content. BM25, by contrast, penalises both static and contextualised
injections because document length increases without proportional relevant information, a
consequence of the explicit length normalisation that classical term-weighting maintains.
    The mitigation strategy exploits precisely this asymmetry. By interpolating between the
neural relevance score and a BM25-derived signal with a tunable parameter α, the defence
reduces the Mean Reciprocal Promotional Reduction (MRPR) of adversarial documents by
up to 0.272 while preserving or improving nDCG@10 and MRR on non-adversarial inputs.
The defence is query-independent, requiring no additional neural inference at query time
beyond the already-deployed scorer.

Verdict on H3.2. H3.2 is supported within the attack family it was designed against. The
neural and lexical components respond asymmetrically to manipulations that add length
without proportional relevant information: contextualised promotional text is effectively in-
visible to the neural scorer but penalised by BM25, so the two scores disagree exactly when
manipulation is present. Interpolating them reduces the Mean Reciprocal Promotional Re-
duction (MRPR) of adversarial documents by up to 0.272 while preserving or improving
nDCG@10 and MRR on non-adversarial inputs, confirming that the disagreement signal is
both detectable and actionable. The support is explicitly bounded, as the following para-
graph sets out: an adaptive adversary who satisfies both components simultaneously would
evade the defence, so H3.2 is established for the studied class of length-inflating, positionally
exploited, and promotional injections rather than for adversarial robustness in general.
CHAPTER 7. ADVERSARIAL MITIGATION                                                           151




           Figure 7.5: Sensitivity to α measuring MRPR for absolute position ‘after’.


Scope and limitations. The defence proposed here should not be characterised as a gen-
eral solution to adversarial robustness in neural ranking. It is effective against attacks that
increase document length without proportional relevant information, a class that includes the
keyword stuffing, LLM rewriting, and promotional injection attacks studied in Chapters 6
and 7. An adaptive adversary who could manipulate documents in ways that preserve both
neural and lexical scores, for example by replacing existing relevant content with adversarial
content of the same length and term distribution, would evade this defence. The strategy is
best understood as an instance of a broader principle: separable scoring components pro-
vide internal disagreement signals that monolithic systems cannot produce. Section 8.2.3
develops this principle as a thesis-level direction, including the question of which additional
separable components, such as learned document-quality priors (Chapter 5), readability es-
timators, or source-authority signals, extend the disagreement basis beyond BM25.

Synthesis. Taken together with the findings of Chapter 6, the results of this chapter sup-
port the analytical claim that the entanglement of query-dependent and query-independent
evidence in neural ranking models is a structural cause of exploitable vulnerabilities, and
the engineering claim that separable scoring components can mitigate these vulnerabilities
in practice. The prior-likelihood decomposition proposed in Chapter 5, the evaluation find-
ings of Chapter 3, the training-efficiency results of Chapter 4, and the adversarial analyses of
Chapters 6 and 7 converge on the same structural principle: recovering separable structure in
neural ranking, whether for effectiveness, efficiency, or robustness, yields concrete and mea-
surable improvements. The following chapter draws these threads together and articulates
the limits of this principle.
CHAPTER 7. ADVERSARIAL MITIGATION                                                         152

7.6      Additional Results
This section provides the classifier training details, additional qualitative generation exam-
ples, and the full classification and retrieval result tables for the study of query-agnostic
promotional content reported earlier in this chapter.


7.6.1     Classifier Training
We use a learning rate of 1e−5 and train for 10 epochs, with a batch size of 8 and a linear
quarter-epoch learning rate warm-up phase. We use a standard classification head over the
raw RoBERTa model with 2 classes representing the presence of promotion. We use the
Transformers library2 and select the strongest checkpoint based on validation split perfor-
mance for final in-domain test evaluation.


7.6.2     Generation Examples
A representative set of successful promotions is shown inline in the chapter (Table 7.4); in
some cases these are harmful and represent attacks that could be realised across many topics.
Here we provide the complementary failure cases. In Table 7.7, we present examples in
which the entity was mentioned and, in some cases, promoted; however, the ‘reasoning’ of
the promotion is either absurd or fails. Such cases would exist when applying this approach
across many topics as, in some examples, there is minimal connection between the topic and
entity, so it would be completely unreasonable to promote the chosen entities. In Table 7.8,
we present examples that technically promote the entity but are ridiculous. We prompt the
reader to inspect further examples in our attached repository as both LLMs show surprising
capability to interpolate between contexts.

Chapter 8

Conclusions and Future Work

8.1     Contributions and Conclusions
This thesis has argued that term-weighting, broadly construed to include both classical lexi-
cal statistics and learned document-side priors, is a primary mechanism through which neu-
ral ad hoc retrieval systems can be made more efficient, more effective, and more robust.
The argument is structural: modern neural ranking models collapse the distinction between
query-dependent and query-independent evidence into a single query-conditioned scoring
function, and this collapse has measurable costs along all three dimensions. Restoring the
separation by learning document priors offline, by using classical retrievers as training sig-
nal, by deploying term-weighting signals as adversarial defences, addresses each cost with-
out sacrificing the representational advantages that make neural models attractive in the first
place.
    H1.1 held that inter-annotator agreement under graded relevance is insufficient to reliably
discriminate between closely performing neural rankers, limiting the value of the marginal
gains claimed for increasingly expensive models. The re-annotation of TREC Deep Learning
2019 found Fleiss’ κ approaching random under four-grade relevance, and showed that the
system pairs whose orderings are most sensitive to judgment variation are precisely those at
the neural Pareto frontier, where LLM-based and LLM-distilled re-rankers are separated by
small margins. State-of-the-art models have reached or are approaching parity with human
annotators treated as oracle systems. H1.1 is supported with scope: large-effect comparisons
remain stable under re-annotation, so the claim is one about fine-margin discrimination on
saturating graded collections rather than about benchmark evaluation in general.
    H1.2 held that BM25-sampled negatives provide sufficient supervision for ranking distil-
lation, and that multi-stage mining cascades do not improve effectiveness beyond this base-
line. A density-ratio and generalisation analysis identified teacher pairwise entropy and the
essential diameter of the query manifold as the operative factors, not the expense of negative
mining, and the empirical ablation found BM25-sampled negatives statistically equivalent
to expensively mined negatives under every semi-supervised criterion tested, in-domain and
out-of-domain. H1.2 is supported within a bounded scope: distillation on MSMARCO eval-
uated on the associated TREC DL and BEIR benchmarks, with an effective teacher. It is not

                                             158
CHAPTER 8. CONCLUSIONS AND FUTURE WORK                                                 159

claimed for teacher-free contrastive training, recall-sensitive tasks, or corpora where BM25
is poorly calibrated. Taken with H1.1, the returns to increased expenditure in neural ranking,
at evaluation time and at training time, are smaller and less reliable than assumed.
     H2 held that a query-independent document-quality prior, learned offline and composed
by score fusion, improves both first-stage retrieval and downstream re-ranking without query-
time cost. An offline prior composed by uncalibrated additive fusion improved first-stage
BM25 retrieval by up to ∆nDCG@10 ≈ 0.046 and downstream re-ranking by up to ∆nDCG@10
≈ 0.054, at the cost of a cached score lookup and zero additional neural inference, and could
be updated, audited, or replaced independently of the deployed model. Gains were largest
for navigational and definitional queries and under LLM-based list-wise re-ranking, where
the prior corrected the initial ordering. H2 is supported within the studied setting, with one
informative exception: under the learned sparse retriever SPLADE, additive fusion degraded
first-stage effectiveness because the prior double-counts document-side structure the sparse
expansion already encodes. This marks the boundary of the uncalibrated regime rather than a
failure of the decomposition. The entanglement of query-independent structure within neural
scorers is therefore an empirical liability, not merely a theoretical concern.
     H3.1 held that the entanglement of query-dependent and query-independent evidence
creates exploitable, query-independent vulnerabilities. Sequence-to-sequence relevance mod-
els proved susceptible to keyword stuffing adapted to prompt tokens and to LLM-based doc-
ument rewriting, both of which significantly improve the rank of manipulated documents
with no knowledge of the target query and transfer across model scales. H3.1 is supported,
scoped to the mechanism: the vulnerability is strongest where document content can drive
an explicit output token, and transfer to pooled-embedding cross-encoders is weaker and
assessed rather than assumed. The vulnerability is structural, a direct consequence of col-
lapsing the prior-likelihood decomposition into a monolithic scorer.
     H3.2 held that separable scoring components respond asymmetrically to manipulation,
yielding a detectable disagreement signal that mitigates positional-bias and promotional-
injection attacks without degrading non-adversarial retrieval. Contextualised promotional
text proved effectively invisible to neural scoring but was penalised by BM25 through length
normalisation, and interpolating the two scores reduced the Mean Reciprocal Promotional
Reduction (MRPR) of adversarial documents by up to 0.272 while preserving or improving
nDCG@10 and MRR on clean inputs. H3.2 is supported for the studied class of length-
inflating, positionally exploited, and promotional injections; an adaptive adversary who sat-
isfies both components at once would evade the defence. The attacks exploit the very en-
tanglement the thesis argues against, and the defence restores the separation the thesis advo-
cates.
     The common thread is the prior-likelihood decomposition of Equation 2.1, which clas-
sical probabilistic retrieval maintained but neural systems have collapsed. The document
prior, encoding stable beliefs about quality, authority, and structural completeness, is not a
heuristic convenience to be discarded in the neural era. It is a structural component whose
explicit estimation, offline computation, and modular composition with query-conditioned
CHAPTER 8. CONCLUSIONS AND FUTURE WORK                                                        160

scoring yields systems that are simultaneously more efficient, more effective, and more ro-
bust. The question for future work is not whether this separation should be maintained, but
how the prior should be learned, how it should be composed with the likelihood, and how
the boundary between them should be drawn as retrieval systems grow in complexity and are
deployed in increasingly adversarial and non-stationary environments.


8.2     Directions for Future Work
The preceding chapters established that separability is beneficial along three dimensions,
but each result was obtained under deliberately simplifying choices: an uncalibrated fusion
rule with a fixed weight, a single document-quality prior, a static corpus, and a specific
family of query-independent attacks. The most productive future work either relaxes those
choices or carries the separability principle into settings the thesis did not address, and the
directions below are organised into five themes: learning and calibrating the prior, the prior
under distribution shift, separable defences beyond the studied attack family, priors as control
surfaces, and the connection to a broader view of information access.


8.2.1    Learning and Calibrating the Prior
Origin. The deliberate decision to defer calibrated, jointly optimised composition is stated
in Chapter 5 and its conclusions (§5.8); the SPLADE first-stage degradation under additive
fusion is reported in the same conclusions and is what makes calibration unavoidable rather
than merely desirable.
    The effectiveness study of Chapter 5 deliberately operated in an uncalibrated composition
setting. The document prior gφ (d) was trained independently of the deployed scorer fθ (q, d),
the fusion weight was fixed at γ = 1.0, and no score calibration was performed between the
two components. This choice isolated the question of whether an offline prior is complemen-
tary in principle, and the answer was affirmative for lexical first-stage retrieval. It was not,
however, uniformly affirmative: under the learned sparse retriever SPLADE, additive fusion
degraded first-stage effectiveness, consistent with the prior double-counting document-side
structure that the sparse expansion already encodes. This result is the clearest motivation
for the first direction. An uncalibrated prior cannot distinguish evidence that the likelihood
has failed to capture from evidence the likelihood already represents, and calibration is the
principled remedy.
    A natural next step is therefore to learn the composition rather than fix it. Two formu-
lations are worth distinguishing. In the first, the fusion weight itself is learned, so that the
contribution of the prior is scaled to the regime in which it is informative; this is a lightweight
change that preserves the offline character of the prior. In the second and more substantive
formulation, the prior is trained against a frozen likelihood, so that it functions explicitly as
a correction term that re-centres the posterior where the deployed scorer is systematically
miscalibrated. The corrective formulation is attractive because it targets the SPLADE failure
CHAPTER 8. CONCLUSIONS AND FUTURE WORK                                                      161

directly: a prior fitted to the residual error of a given likelihood cannot, by construction,
re-supply signal that the likelihood already provides. It also preserves the separability the
thesis values, since the likelihood is not modified and the prior remains an independently
estimable, independently auditable component.
    Calibration also raises the question of how prior capacity should be allocated. The
amortised-inference view holds that the cost of estimating a prior is paid once and recov-
ered across the entire query workload, but the favourability of that trade depends on the cost
profile of the downstream stage. A prior that is worth a large offline model when it serves
an expensive LLM re-ranker over many queries may not be worth the same model when it
serves a cheap cross-encoder, and the scaling relationship between prior capacity and down-
stream cost is currently unknown. A related design question is that first-stage retrieval depth
and re-ranking depth become joint variables once an informative prior is available: a prior
that reliably promotes relevant documents into the re-ranking window permits a shallower
window at fixed effectiveness, trading offline prior computation against online re-ranking
cost under a fixed latency budget. Characterising this trade-off, rather than treating retrieval
depth as a fixed engineering constant, is a concrete and tractable next step.


8.2.2    The Prior under Distribution Shift
Origin. The static-corpus scope of the prior is set in Chapter 5 (§5.8); the cross-corpus
question is informed by the generalisation analysis in Chapter 4 (§4.3); the evaluation-floor
constraint that any genuine but small improvement must clear is established in Chapter 3
(§3.5).
    A central promise of the separability argument is that the prior can be updated inde-
pendently of the query-time model. Chapter 5 demonstrated that an offline prior can be
composed with a deployed scorer without retraining it, but the experiments were conducted
on a static corpus, and the harder claim, that the prior can be re-estimated as the collection
evolves without disturbing the likelihood, was not tested. Corpora in deployment are not
static: documents are added, content decays in reliability, and the distribution of document
quality drifts. Whether a prior can track this drift through periodic offline re-estimation,
while the query-time model is left untouched, is an open empirical question and a direct test
of whether the modularity the thesis advocates holds in practice rather than only in principle.
    Distribution shift also arises across corpora rather than only within one over time. The
generalisation analysis of Chapter 4 found that out-of-domain transfer in ranking distillation
is governed by teacher entropy and the geometry of the query manifold rather than by the
expense of negative mining. The analogous question for priors is whether a document-quality
prior fitted on one corpus transfers to correct likelihood residuals on another. Because the
prior is by design independent of the query-time model, it is a more portable object than a
jointly trained scorer, and a prior that transfers across corpora would be a strong practical
argument for the decomposition. The factors identified in Chapter 4 suggest where to look:
transfer should succeed to the extent that the notion of document quality is stable across the
CHAPTER 8. CONCLUSIONS AND FUTURE WORK                                                     162

two corpora and fail where quality is corpus-specific, and this is a testable prediction.
    Finally, the evaluation findings of Chapter 3 bear on future work in their own right. If
widely used test collections are approaching saturation, and if the systems most affected by
annotation noise are precisely the expensive models at the frontier, then measuring a genuine
but small improvement from a prior requires evaluation methodology that can discriminate
below the annotation-noise floor. Two approaches are worth pursuing: triangulating effec-
tiveness across multiple collections so that a real effect is distinguished from collection-
specific noise, and measuring the contributions of the prior and the likelihood separately, so
that a modest genuine gain from the prior is not absorbed into the variance of an aggregate
metric computed over an entangled scorer. The decomposition that the thesis advocates for
modelling is, in this sense, also a decomposition for evaluation.


8.2.3    Separable Defences beyond the Studied Attack Family
Origin. The disagreement-based defence and its explicit scope statement appear in Chap-
ter 7 (§7.5); the structural argument that motivates extending it lives in Chapter 6 (§6.4),
which identifies entanglement as the source of the attack surface.
    Chapter 7 demonstrated a defence against positional-bias exploitation and promotional
content injection, and was explicit that this defence should not be characterised as a general-
purpose adversarial defence. The mechanism it relies on is specific: a classical lexical scorer
penalises manipulations that a neural scorer rewards, and the disagreement between the two
components exposes the manipulation. This mechanism succeeds for attacks that move a
document in the neural score space without producing the lexical evidence that genuine
relevance would carry, and it offers no guarantee against attacks constructed to satisfy both
components at once. The first direction is therefore to characterise the boundary: which
families of query-independent attacks produce an asymmetric disagreement signal between
neural and lexical scores, and which do not. A principled account of when the defence
applies is more valuable than an accumulation of further attack-specific results, because it
tells a system operator what the defence does and does not protect against.
    A second direction follows from the trajectory of the field. Retrieval is increasingly
embedded within agentic retrieval-augmented generation, in which a system retrieves, rea-
sons over what it has retrieved, and issues refined queries in successive cycles. Each cycle
introduces a new evidence factor, and a manipulated document admitted in an early cycle
can shape the queries issued in later ones. Whether query-independent attacks of the kind
studied in Chapter 6 propagate through multi-hop agentic retrieval, and whether a separa-
ble lexical check applied at each cycle arrests that propagation, is an open and increasingly
consequential question. The separability principle suggests a natural defensive posture for
these pipelines: rather than trusting a single opaque relevance score at each hop, retain an
inspectable lexical component throughout.
    A third direction reframes the defence as an auditing tool rather than only a mitigation.
Because a separable prior and a separable lexical component can each be inspected, the
CHAPTER 8. CONCLUSIONS AND FUTURE WORK                                                       163

disagreement between them carries diagnostic information beyond a binary accept-or-reject
decision. A large disagreement localised to a particular document, or to a particular region of
the corpus, is a signal that can be surfaced to a human operator together with the component
scores that produced it. This turns the separable architecture into an instrument for explaining
suspected manipulation, not merely suppressing it, and connects the robustness contribution
of the thesis to the interpretability argument that motivates separability in the first place.


8.2.4    Priors as Control Surfaces
Origin. The control-surface view follows from the explicit prior of Chapter 5 (§5.8) and the
modular composition developed in the perspective section embedded in that chapter (§5.7.3,
within §5.7).
    The thesis measured the benefits of separability along efficiency, effectiveness, and ro-
bustness, but the same property has a consequence the empirical chapters did not pursue: an
explicit prior is a control surface. When document-side beliefs are entangled within a jointly
trained scorer, adjusting the behaviour of the system means retraining it; when those beliefs
are held in a separable prior, behaviour can be adjusted by modifying the prior alone, with
the likelihood left intact as a generic intent model. This observation opens a class of future
work concerned not with effectiveness but with governance and personalisation.
    The most immediate instance is global policy control. Safety, trustworthiness, diversity,
and exposure constraints are commonly imposed on deployed retrieval systems through man-
ual boosts and filters layered onto an opaque scorer. Expressed instead as prior terms, these
constraints become objects that can be learned, calibrated, and audited independently of the
likelihood, and a policy change can be deployed by updating a prior rather than retraining a
model. This is the point at which the interpretability argument running through the thesis is
cashed out for deployment: separability is valuable not only because it isolates document-
side evidence for modelling, but because it isolates document-side policy for inspection.
    Personalisation fits the same pattern. A user-conditioned prior p(d | u) composed with a
generic likelihood fθ (q, d) corresponds directly to the recommender-systems view of user-
item affinity, and it raises a sharp design question: whether personalisation can be confined
to the prior, leaving the likelihood a shared, user-independent intent model. If so, a single
query-time model could serve all users, with personalisation maintained entirely offline as a
per-user prior. More generally, a deployed system need not rely on a single prior. Distinct
beliefs, document quality, source trust, freshness, and user affinity, differ in their supervision
and in the timescale on which they change, and a composition of the form

                           log p(d | q) ∝ fθ (q, d) + ∑ λm gφm (d),                         (8.1)
                                                        m

maintains each belief gφm as an independent component with its own update cadence. An
improvement to any one prior propagates to every pipeline that shares the corpus, without
retraining the query-time model. The open problem is operational rather than architectural:
maintaining a set of independently valid priors requires monitoring and auditing methods,
CHAPTER 8. CONCLUSIONS AND FUTURE WORK                                                      164

including drift detection and calibration checks, that can validate each prior on its own. The
thesis has argued that separability makes such per-component validation possible; future
work should establish what that validation, in practice, requires.


8.2.5    Towards a Unified View of Information Access
Origin. The three-way factorisation that connects ad hoc retrieval to recommendation is
developed in the perspective section embedded in Chapter 5 (§5.7); the priors-for-cold-start
unification originates in the same place.
    The final direction is the most exploratory, and widens the lens before the thesis closes.
The prior-likelihood decomposition is not specific to ad hoc retrieval. Recommendation can
be expressed in the same terms: a user-item score decomposes into an item representation,
which plays the role of a user-marginalised item prior, and a user representation, which con-
ditions that prior on a specific user, just as a query conditions a document prior on a specific
information need. Ad hoc retrieval conditions on the query, recommendation conditions on
the user, and hybrid systems that combine a query with a persistent user profile condition on
both, admitting a three-way factorisation

                             p(d | q, u) ∝ p(q | d) p(d | u) p(d),                        (8.2)

in which each term operates on a different timescale, corpus updates for p(d), user-history
evolution for p(d | u), and per-request intent for p(q | d), and only the query likelihood need
be evaluated under strict latency.
    This common structure suggests that retrieval and recommendation, usually treated as
separate research areas, face the same underlying allocation problem: deciding which part
of a relevance estimate can be precomputed from one side and which requires observing the
other at request time. The decomposition also unifies their treatment of cold start. An under-
specified query in retrieval and a history-poor user in recommendation are the same situation,
a weak likelihood signal, and the same remedy applies, namely increasing the weight on the
prior when the likelihood is uninformative. Several concrete questions follow. Whether pri-
ors learned from recommendation interaction logs can transfer to ad hoc retrieval to improve
personalisation without sacrificing topical precision is one. How prior-likelihood weighting
should adapt as the length of a user’s history grows is another. The relationship between
retrievability-based document priors and item-popularity priors, and whether a single for-
mulation can serve both, is a third. None of these is resolved here; they mark the horizon
along which the separability principle, established in this thesis for neural ranking, extends
naturally into information access more broadly.


8.3     Concluding Remarks
This thesis has addressed a structural question in neural information retrieval: what a system
can know about a document before any query arrives, and what it can determine only once
CHAPTER 8. CONCLUSIONS AND FUTURE WORK                                                    165

a query is observed. Classical retrieval systems kept these two sources of evidence sepa-
rate; neural systems collapsed them into a single query-conditioned score. This thesis has
shown that the separation is worth restoring, and that restoring it through term-weighting,
spanning both classical lexical statistics and learned document-side priors, yields neural ad
hoc retrieval systems that are simultaneously more efficient, more effective, and more robust.
In Section 8.2, a number of directions have been identified, from calibrated composition of
the prior to its extension into broader settings of information access. This thesis has laid a
foundation and provided motivation for pursuing these directions, and the prior-likelihood
decomposition is expected to remain a productive lens as retrieval systems are absorbed into
increasingly complex and adversarial pipelines.

navigate from an entry back to its first use.

A.3      Software and Libraries
The software below underlies the experiments of all contribution chapters.

• Retrieval, indexing, and pipeline composition use PyTerrier Macdonald and Tonellotto
  [2020] over a Terrier backend Ounis et al. [2005]. All BM25 candidate generation in
  Chapters 3–7 is produced this way.

• All relevance metrics are computed with the ir-measures evaluator of MacAvaney et al.
  [2022b]. Unless a chapter states otherwise, graded judgments are binarised at a relevance
  grade > 2 for the recall-oriented and precision-oriented measures that require it.

• Neural model training and inference use the Transformers library Wolf et al. [2020] with a
  PyTorch backend Paszke et al. [2019]. This covers the negative-mining students of Chap-
  ter 4, the quality-prior inference of Chapter 5, and the RoBERTa promotion classifier of
  Chapter 7.

• We serve Llama-2 using the vLLM library with float16 precision. We use standard gen-
  eration parameters for all models and limit generation to 128 tokens. GPT-3.5 Turbo is
  accessed through the OpenAI API rather than served locally.
CHAPTER 8. CONCLUSIONS AND FUTURE WORK                                                    169

• The re-annotation exercise of Chapter 3 was administered through a purpose-built web in-
  terface presenting one query-passage pair at a time on the four-grade TREC scale; agree-
  ment statistics and swap rates were computed with NumPy and SciPy.


A.4      Computational Resources
All models trained in this thesis were trained on NVIDIA RTX 4090 hardware (24GB
VRAM), and inference was run on the same hardware with one exception: the Alpaca re-
writing attacks of Section 6.2.4 were generated on one NVIDIA A100 (40GB). No exper-
iment required multi-GPU training, and no result depends on access to a shared cluster; a
single RTX 4090 reproduces every trained model reported here.

• Chapter 3 (evaluation reliability). The re-annotation itself is a human-annotation exer-
  cise and consumed assessor time rather than compute: eight retrieval academics served
  as secondary annotators. The accompanying system runs and agreement analysis are
  inference-only and complete in a few GPU-hours on one card.

• Chapter 4 (training efficiency). Each experiment (one parameter instantiation) took ap-
  proximately 12 hours to train on a single card, with all runs held to an equal budget of 12M
  observed documents.

• Chapter 5 (effectiveness). Inference-only. The quality priors are scored once offline
  over the collection and cached, after which fusion adds no neural forward pass; all prior
  inference was run on a single card.

• Chapter 6 (adversarial attacks). Inference-only across the target models, with two ex-
  ceptions: the commercial generator, which is billed API usage rather than local compute,
  and the Alpaca re-writing of Section 6.2.4, which was generated on one A100 rather than
  the RTX 4090 used elsewhere.

• Chapter 7 (positional bias). Llama-2 generation was run locally on one card, deliberately,
  so that the promotional-generation results remain reproducible without API access. The
  RoBERTa classifier trains in well under an hour at the settings given in Section 7.6.1.

CHAPTER 8. CONCLUSIONS AND FUTURE WORK                                                  170

General Public License v3.0. HotpotQA and Climate-Fever are provided under the CC BY-
SA 4.0 license. NFCorpus is released for research use by its authors without a separate
license instrument. FiQA and Quora are distributed under the terms of the original challenge
and forum data respectively, and we use both solely for non-commercial research; neither
carries a standalone open-source license, and we therefore rely on the redistribution terms
under which the BEIR benchmark Thakur et al. [2021] makes them available. TREC-Covid
test queries and judgments are provided under open domain, however the underlying CORD-
19 collection has variable licensing and we point readers to this metadata for more details.
    Models. The BERT and ELECTRA base checkpoints, the T5 checkpoints from which
monoT5 and the QualT5 quality estimators are derived, and the all-mpnet-base-v2
sentence encoder are provided under Apache-2.0. RoBERTa and the RankZephyr checkpoint
are provided under the MIT license. Two checkpoints carry non-commercial terms and are
used here for academic research only: the SPLADE checkpoint naver/splade-cocon
denser-ensembledistil under CC BY-NC-SA 4.0, and Contriever under CC BY-NC
4.0. Llama 2 is released under the Llama 2 Community License rather than a standard open-
source instrument. The monoT5 and Tas-B checkpoints are released by their authors without
a separate license instrument, and we use them, as released, for non-commercial research.
GPT-3.5 Turbo is not distributed; it is accessed under the OpenAI API terms of service.
